Advanced Machine Learning

The full teaching manual for the 13-session Advanced Machine Learning kit (Modules 7 to 13; one 3-hour session, two lessons each). Timing, live-demo scripts, the mistakes to pre-empt, lab facilitation, and checks for understanding, for every session.

Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 1: Interpreting Models and SHAP

How to teach the first session of Advanced Machine Learning. Welcome a room in which some learners finished Machine Learning Foundations and others bring equivalent experience; recap the conventions this kit builds on; prove that every machine has the full environment, PyTorch and the image data included; then open the churn model up: odds ratios, the impurity trap, permutation importance and partial dependence (7.1), and SHAP values that split one customer's prediction into exact shares (7.2).

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 7.1–7.2 Session 1 of 13

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm for both lessons: frame (the goal and one mental model) → live demo (you type, they type along) → guided lab (a kit lab, new practice on the lesson's skills) → start the lesson's own project. The lesson exercises, quiz and journal are homework.
  • Type, do not paste. Every code block in a demo script below was run to produce the output printed under it (Python 3.12.14, pandas 3.0.6, scikit-learn 1.9.1, shap 0.53.0, PyTorch 2.14.1 on 4 CPU threads). Ask for a prediction before every run that prints a number, and read errors from the last line up, out loud.
  • The lessons are the reference. Keep model_interpretation.html and interpretability_shap.html open in browser tabs for the pictures (the PDP and ICE panels, the waterfall, the beeswarm, the SHAP scatter plots). Labs live in labs/module-01/: _starter.py files are handouts, _solution.py files are yours.
  • Session 1 of Advanced Machine Learning opens with 30 minutes of welcome, environment check and a Foundations refresher instead of the usual 10-minute warm-up. This kit is sold separately: some learners finished Machine Learning Foundations last week, others learned scikit-learn at work. The opening makes sure everyone shares the same conventions before Module 7 builds on them.
  • Teal blocks are hands-on, amber blocks are watch-outs. Lesson codes (7.1, 7.2) match the course site.
The #1 risk of Session 1: a room with two different backgrounds

Learners who did Foundations know Pinecrest's churn table, the canonical split, Pipeline([("prep", ...), ("model", ...)]) and the $15 / $60 campaign economics by heart. Learners who come "with equivalent experience" may know scikit-learn well and still have never seen this data, these names or these numbers. Module 7 quotes all of them without explanation. Before: send the setup guide and the one-page "What Kit B assumes" table (it is in the workbook's Start Here page) a week ahead, with check_environment.py. In the room: the refresher is six predict-the-output questions, one per convention; the tally tells you who needs a partner. After: diagnostic_starter.py is homework for anyone who missed two or more. The second risk is the image data: Fashion-MNIST, EuroSAT and two pretrained networks (about 175 MB) are needed from Session 5 on. The environment check reports them as "to download"; nobody downloads on venue Wi-Fi.

Before the Session

  • ☐ A week before: email the setup guide (Python 3.12 or newer, a pinecrest-ml folder, .venv, on Linux the CPU-only PyTorch line FIRST, then python -m pip install -r requirements.txt, then pinecrest-ml-data.zip unzipped into the folder). Attach labs/module-01/l7_0-orientation/check_environment.py; ask learners to run it once plainly and once with --download at home (about 175 MB, once) and to reply with its last line. Learners who did Foundations already have the environment; they only need the download.
  • ☐ Files to hand out (shared folder or USB stick): requirements.txt, pinecrest-ml-data.zip (1.4 MB), the whole labs/ folder without the *_solution.py files (keep labs_paths.py: every lab imports it), and the workbook. Learners put labs/ inside pinecrest-ml, next to data/.
  • ☐ Run the kit check once: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-01 from this kit's folder. Every line should say PASS (10 files, about a minute and a half). Run python labs/module-01/l7_0-orientation/check_environment.py on the projector machine so you can show what READY looks like (up to a minute the first time).
  • ☐ Downloads and caches: none needed today. Pre-load the image data on the projector machine anyway (check_environment.py --download), so you can show what the full READY line looks like, and so Session 5 does not start with a download.
  • ☐ Projector machine: a terminal with (.venv) active in your own pinecrest-ml, JupyterLab (or VS Code) started from that folder, font size 18 pt or more, two empty notebooks: lesson_7_1 and lesson_7_2. Browser tabs: the course home page, model_interpretation.html, interpretability_shap.html. Run import shap once before the session: the first import takes several seconds.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: the "What Kit B assumes" six rows (split, baseline, named pipeline, CV, metrics, $15 / $60); three boxes "recipe / taste test / dial" for 7.1; and "base value + shares = prediction (log-odds)" for 7.2.
  • ☐ Print or share: the workbook (its Start Here page has the refresher), and sticky notes for help flags and the exit ticket.
Known environment traps
SymptomCauseFix
check_environment.py ends with READY for Sessions 1 to 4The image data and the pretrained weights are not downloaded yetNothing to do today. At home: python labs/module-01/l7_0-orientation/check_environment.py --download (about 175 MB, once), before Session 5.
ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'shap' (or lime)An older Foundations environment from before the full requirements, or the wrong kernelWith (.venv) active: python -m pip install -r requirements.txt. In a notebook, check the kernel is the .venv one.
import shap sits silent for 10 to 20 seconds, or prints a numba warningshap compiles parts of itself the first timeWait; the second import is fast. Warnings on the first import are harmless.
Their SHAP numbers differ in the third decimal from the pageAnother shap or scikit-learn versionThe check prints both versions (shap 0.53, scikit-learn 1.9). Reinstall from requirements.txt.
ValueError: The column 0 contains integer data from a partial dependence callX was built without .astype(float)Rebuild X with .astype(float) and refit: train and explain on the same X (demo Step 9).
On Linux, pip install -r requirements.txt starts downloading nvidia-... filespip chose the GPU build of PyTorchCtrl+C. Install the CPU line from Lesson 0.1 first, then the requirements line again.
FileNotFoundError: ... 'data/customers_churn.csv'Python is working in another folderprint(Path.cwd()). Start JupyterLab from pinecrest-ml.

Session 1 Agenda (3 hours)

Total180Ends at 3:00.

In the room. The lesson pages estimate 95 + 95 = 190 minutes for a solo learner; after the 30-minute opening, the break and the wrap-up the room has 130. So the room does, for each lesson: the mental model, the live demo, one guided kit lab, and the first steps of the lesson project. The plots are shown from the lesson pages; the demo types the numbers behind them.

Goes home (2 to 3 hours). The churn-drivers briefing Steps 3 to 5 and the call-list reasons Steps 4 and 5; each lesson's Hands-on Exercises 1 to 3 (Exercise 4 is a stretch); both Quick Quizzes; both journal entries; the 7.2 "Before the Next Lesson" list. Anyone whose check said NOT READY fixes their machine; anyone at "READY for Sessions 1 to 4" downloads the image data before Session 5.

Protect, in this order: the environment check and the refresher tally (nobody leaves without knowing whether their machine works and which Foundations habits to revisit); 7.1 Steps 4 and 5 (the noise trap and permutation importance); 7.2 Steps 4 to 7 (additivity, log-odds, one customer explained). Cut first: 7.2 Step 9 (the beeswarm: show the lesson figure), then 7.1 Step 6 (side by side: say the 0.064 vs 0.003 sentence), then the 7.2 project start (send all five steps home).

Opening Segment

0:00

Welcome, Environment Check and Foundations Refresher

30 min
Timing
5mWelcome and introductions
7mHow Kit B runs, and what it assumes from Foundations (the six-row table)
10mEnvironment check: check_environment.py ends with READY on every machine (or the machine gets a helper)
8mRefresher: six predict-the-output questions on the split, pipelines, CV, metrics and the threshold, not graded
Sequence and talking points
  • Welcome (5m). One minute on yourself, including a time an explanation of a model changed a decision. Then a fast round: name, where they use machine learning today, and whether they did Machine Learning Foundations. Note who did not: they get a Foundations graduate as a neighbor for the labs. Housekeeping in one breath: break at 1:30, where the lab files are, sticky note on the laptop lid when stuck.
  • How Kit B runs (3m). "Thirteen sessions of three hours: explaining models and checking their fairness, then finding groups without labels, reducing dimensions, deep learning with PyTorch, text and recommendations, putting models into production, and a capstone you choose. Same company, Pinecrest Outfitters, same data. In the room we do what is hard alone: the mental model, a live demo, a guided lab and the start of each project. At home you finish the projects and do the exercises, quiz and journal: two to three hours a week."
  • What Kit B assumes (4m). Walk the six rows on the board (the same table is on the workbook's Start Here page): the canonical split and its first test customer C102112; a baseline before any score; Pipeline([("prep", preprocess), ("model", model)]); cross-validation on training rows only; the metric that matches the decision; $15 per offer and $60 per churner reached, break-even 0.25. Say: "If any row is new to you, that is fine: the refresher in ten minutes shows which, and diagnostic_starter.py practices each one."
  • Environment check (10m). On the projector, then theirs: 1 open a terminal; 2 cd pinecrest-ml; 3 activate and look for (.venv); 4 python labs/module-01/l7_0-orientation/check_environment.py. Three possible last lines: READY for Advanced Machine Learning; READY for Sessions 1 to 4 (images not downloaded yet: homework, with --download); NOT READY with a numbered list (activation and the data folder you fix now; a missing install you do not start on venue Wi-Fi: pair them).
  • Refresher (8m). "Six quick questions, one per row of the table. Not a test, nobody collects it." Learners answer D1 to D6 on the Start Here page (about four minutes), then show fingers (1 to 4 for A to D) as you reveal each. Settle arguments by running the code. Tally wrong answers per question on a sticky note.
Refresher answers and how to read the tally

Answers: D1 B) 4500 1500 C102112; D2 D) ['prep', 'model'] LogisticRegression; D3 A) [900, 900, 900, 900, 900]; D4 C) 0.333 0.5; D5 B) 0.75; D6 D) 4 2 60.

  • D1 (split) and D2 (named pipeline) are the shape of every Module 7 code block: logreg[-1].coef_, logreg[1].scale_, the first test customer C102112. Two or more misses on D2: say "last step" every time you type [-1] today.
  • D3 (CV) comes back in Lesson 7.1's FAQ ("validation set or test set?") and in Session 3's stability checks. Misses are not urgent today.
  • D4 (recall, precision) and D6 (economics) are the vocabulary of Lesson 7.3's fairness audit in Session 2. A third of the room missing either: plan five extra minutes on them in Session 2's warm-up.
  • D5 (AUC) is the number permutation importance measures drops in. Misses here: narrate demo Step 5 slowly ("0.14 of AUC is most of the way from 0.86 to a coin toss").
  • Three or more wrong: pair them with a Foundations graduate today, and at the wrap-up hand them labs/module-01/l7_0-orientation/diagnostic_starter.py as extra homework: six small functions with a checker, one per question, ending with a real churn pipeline, a threshold chosen on out-of-fold probabilities and a profit.
Watch-outs
  • Do not re-teach Foundations. The refresher is diagnostic; the fixes are the homework file and a neighbor. Two minutes over is fine; ten is not.
  • "I already use SHAP at work." Welcome them, and give them a job: watch for the units (log-odds) and the background, which experienced users get wrong most. Their stretch is Lab 7.2 A's interaction and Lesson 7.2's Exercise 3.
  • Do not debug one machine in front of the room. Two minutes per machine, then pair and move on.

Lesson Plans

7.1

Interpreting Models

60 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Read the coefficients of a scaled logistic regression as odds ratios, and say when they mislead
  • Compute permutation importance on the test set with permutation_importance and explain why impurity-based importance can fool you
  • Draw partial dependence and ICE plots with PartialDependenceDisplay and read thresholds, curves and interactions from them
  • State what feature importance does and does not mean: it describes the model, not the causes of churn
Timing in the room
10mFrame: three people, three questions (what drives churn, is it using anything it should not, why this person); the recipe, the taste test, the dial
25mLive demo: two models, coefficients and odds ratios, natural units, the noise trap, permutation importance, PDP numbers, an interaction, two errors
15mGuided lab: fraud_drivers_starter.py
10mStart the lesson project: the churn-drivers briefing, Steps 1 and 2
Mental model to say

"Before the retention team acts on a churn model, three people ask questions. The marketing manager: what drives churn? The data scientist next to you: is it using anything it should not? An agent looking at one flagged customer: why this person? The first two are about the whole model, global explanations; the third is about one prediction, a local explanation. Today: all three, the first two this hour."

Then the picture, drawn as three boxes on the board: "Think of the model as a dish. Coefficients are reading the recipe: only possible when the recipe is short and written down, as in a linear model. Permutation importance is a taste test: swap one ingredient for a random one and see how much worse the dish gets; it works on any dish, even from a kitchen you cannot see into. Partial dependence is a dial: turn one ingredient up and down, keep everything else, and watch the taste change."

Then the one rule for the whole module, written under the boxes: these tools describe the model, not the world. "The model relies on recency" is a measurement. "Recency causes churn" needs an experiment.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook, lesson_7_1, in pinecrest-ml. The lesson's charts stay on the lesson page; you type the code that produces the numbers behind them, and ask for a prediction before every run.

  1. Two models to explain (3m). "Before we explain anything, we check it is worth explaining. Same 11 numeric features for both, the canonical split, a baseline." Narrate .astype(float): partial dependence refuses integer columns (Step 9 shows the error). 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingClassifier
from sklearn.dummy import DummyClassifier
from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user",
            "email_opens_90d"]

X = customers[features].astype(float)
y = customers["churned"]
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=42)

baseline = DummyClassifier().fit(X_train, y_train)
logreg = make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median"), StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression())
logreg.fit(X_train, y_train)
hgb = HistGradientBoostingClassifier(random_state=42).fit(X_train, y_train)

for name, model in [("baseline", baseline), ("logistic regression", logreg), ("gradient boosting", hgb)]:
    auc = roc_auc_score(y_test, model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1])
    print(f"{name:20s} test ROC AUC {auc:.3f}")
    baseline             test ROC AUC 0.500
logistic regression  test ROC AUC 0.828
gradient boosting    test ROC AUC 0.865
    Write 0.500 / 0.828 / 0.865 on the board. Ask: "Where will the 0.04 gap between the two models come from?" Park the answers; Step 6 settles it.
  2. Read the recipe: coefficients (2m). "A scaled logistic regression adds weighted features to get the log-odds. np.exp turns each weight into an odds ratio: above 1 raises the odds, below 1 lowers them." 
    coefs = pd.Series(logreg[-1].coef_[0], index=features)
table = pd.DataFrame({"coef": coefs, "odds_ratio": np.exp(coefs)}).sort_values("coef")
print(table.round(2))
                           coef  odds_ratio
orders_12m            -0.32        0.73
app_user              -0.29        0.75
email_opens_90d       -0.28        0.75
tenure_months         -0.13        0.88
spend_12m             -0.12        0.89
returns_12m           -0.03        0.97
avg_order_value       -0.01        0.99
return_rate            0.08        1.09
support_tickets_12m    0.40        1.50
discount_share         0.68        1.97
days_since_last_order  0.89        2.43
    logreg[-1] is the last pipeline step, the fitted model: the Pipeline naming from Foundations. Read only the top and the bottom rows aloud.
  3. Say it in natural units (2m). "Per standard deviation is precise and unfriendly. Divide by the scaler's standard deviation, multiply by a step that means something." 
    scale = pd.Series(logreg[1].scale_, index=features)
per_unit = coefs / scale

print(f"days_since_last_order: sd = {scale['days_since_last_order']:.1f} days")
print(f"odds ratio per 30 more days without an order: {np.exp(30 * per_unit['days_since_last_order']):.2f}")
print(f"odds ratio for an app user vs a non-user:     {np.exp(per_unit['app_user']):.2f}")
    days_since_last_order: sd = 87.2 days
odds ratio per 30 more days without an order: 1.36
odds ratio for an app user vs a non-user:     0.55
    The sentence for the briefing: every extra month without an order multiplies the odds by about 1.4; app users have about half the odds, other features held fixed. Then the three warnings on the slide: a line cannot bend, overlapping features share credit, "held fixed" may be impossible.
  4. The built-in importance trap (4m). "Forests come with feature_importances_. Free, fast, tempting. Watch what it does with a column of pure noise." 
    from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier
from sklearn.inspection import permutation_importance

rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
X_train_noise = X_train.assign(random_noise=rng.normal(size=len(X_train)))
X_test_noise = X_test.assign(random_noise=rng.normal(size=len(X_test)))

forest = RandomForestClassifier(n_estimators=200, random_state=42, n_jobs=-1)
forest.fit(X_train_noise, y_train)

mdi = pd.Series(forest.feature_importances_, index=X_train_noise.columns)
perm = permutation_importance(forest, X_test_noise, y_test, scoring="roc_auc",
                              n_repeats=10, random_state=42, n_jobs=-1)
compare = pd.DataFrame({"impurity (train)": mdi,
                        "permutation (test)": perm.importances_mean}).sort_values("impurity (train)", ascending=False)
print(compare.round(3))
                           impurity (train)  permutation (test)
days_since_last_order             0.218               0.152
discount_share                    0.130               0.080
tenure_months                     0.125               0.057
spend_12m                         0.091               0.001
avg_order_value                   0.086               0.004
random_noise                      0.083               0.005
return_rate                       0.079               0.006
email_opens_90d                   0.072               0.010
orders_12m                        0.041               0.003
support_tickets_12m               0.041               0.012
app_user                          0.018               0.009
returns_12m                       0.016               0.001
    Ask before you reveal the table: "Where will the noise land?" It lands sixth by impurity, above app_user and support_tickets_12m, and near 0.005 by permutation on the test set. Rule of thumb on the board: impurity for a quick look, permutation on held-out data for anything you tell someone.
  5. The taste test: permutation importance (3m). "Shuffle one column of the test set so it no longer matches its customers. If the score drops, the model was relying on it. Nothing is retrained." 
    result = permutation_importance(hgb, X_test, y_test, scoring="roc_auc", n_repeats=10, random_state=42)
importance = pd.DataFrame({"mean": result.importances_mean, "std": result.importances_std},
                          index=features).sort_values("mean", ascending=False)
print(importance.round(3))
                            mean    std
days_since_last_order  0.140  0.012
discount_share         0.088  0.006
tenure_months          0.064  0.009
app_user               0.019  0.003
support_tickets_12m    0.015  0.003
email_opens_90d        0.009  0.004
orders_12m             0.006  0.005
return_rate            0.004  0.003
avg_order_value        0.003  0.001
returns_12m           -0.000  0.001
spend_12m             -0.004  0.002
    A slightly negative value (spend_12m) reads as zero. Point at the std column: two features within one standard deviation of each other are not a ranking you defend.
  6. Same features, different model, different story (2m). "Importance describes a model. Ask the logistic regression the same question." 
    lr_result = permutation_importance(logreg, X_test, y_test, scoring="roc_auc", n_repeats=10, random_state=42)
side_by_side = pd.DataFrame({"boosting": result.importances_mean,
                             "logistic": lr_result.importances_mean}, index=features)
print(side_by_side.sort_values("boosting", ascending=False).round(3).head(6))
                           boosting  logistic
days_since_last_order     0.140     0.150
discount_share            0.088     0.092
tenure_months             0.064     0.003
app_user                  0.019     0.010
support_tickets_12m       0.015     0.021
email_opens_90d           0.009     0.010
    Tenure: 0.064 for boosting, 0.003 for the line. That is the coefficient warning, measured, and a large part of the AUC gap from Step 1.
  7. The dial: partial dependence in numbers (3m). "Set everyone's days to 15, predict, average. Then 60, 120 ... That curve is partial dependence. method="brute" averages real probabilities." 
    from sklearn.inspection import partial_dependence

pd_days = partial_dependence(hgb, X_test, ["days_since_last_order"], method="brute",
                             custom_values={"days_since_last_order": [15, 60, 120, 150, 180, 240, 300]})
for days, p in zip(pd_days["grid_values"][0], pd_days["average"][0]):
    print(f"{days:5.0f} days -> average predicted churn {p:.3f}")
       15 days -> average predicted churn 0.077
   60 days -> average predicted churn 0.077
  120 days -> average predicted churn 0.095
  150 days -> average predicted churn 0.214
  180 days -> average predicted churn 0.390
  240 days -> average predicted churn 0.443
  300 days -> average predicted churn 0.490
    Show the lesson page's three-panel PDP and ICE figure for the shapes (threshold, U, kink). The briefing sentence: risk roughly quadruples between 120 and 180 days.
  8. An interaction the average hides (3m). "A PDP averages everybody. Split the customers by app use and compute it twice." 
    for flag, label in [(0, "non-app users"), (1, "app users")]:
    group = X_test[X_test["app_user"] == flag]
    pd_tickets = partial_dependence(hgb, group, ["support_tickets_12m"], method="brute",
                                    custom_values={"support_tickets_12m": [0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5]})
    print(f"{label:14s}", pd_tickets["average"][0].round(3))
    non-app users  [0.137 0.2   0.234 0.288 0.296 0.438]
app users      [0.087 0.098 0.107 0.11  0.081 0.145]
    For non-app users each ticket raises predicted churn; for app users tickets barely matter. "It depends" is what trees capture and a plain line does not.
  9. Error on purpose 1: integer columns (1m). "Here is why we converted to float at the start." 
    from sklearn.inspection import PartialDependenceDisplay
raw = customers[features].iloc[:300]
PartialDependenceDisplay.from_estimator(hgb, raw, ["tenure_months"])
    ValueError: The column 0 contains integer data. Partial dependence plots are not supported for integer data: this can lead to implicit rounding with N ...
    Read only the first sentence aloud. Fix: .astype(float) when you build X, and train and explain on the same X.
  10. Error on purpose 2: a pipeline has no coefficients (1m). "The pipeline is a container. The coefficients live on its last step." 
    logreg.coef_
    AttributeError: 'Pipeline' object has no attribute 'coef_'
    Fix: logreg[-1].coef_ (or logreg.named_steps["logisticregression"]). Then show Common Mistake 3 from the page without running it: importance on the training rows looks several times bigger for the noisy columns (0.026 vs 0.009 for email opens). No error, and that is the problem.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Reading importance as a lever. "App use is a top-5 driver, so the app campaign will cut churn." Coach with a question: "Would pushing a disengaged customer to install the app make them engaged? What would you need to know that?" The answer is a randomized test. Make them rephrase as "customers who use the app are predicted to churn less".
  • Importance on the training rows, or impurity importance as the answer. No error, bigger and more confident numbers. Coach: "Which rows did you shuffle? Would the noise column survive this?" In the lab, ask them to add a random column if they doubt a ranking.
  • Partial dependence read off the edges. The PDP sets every customer's spend to $2,500, including customers with one $60 order: a customer who cannot exist. Coach: "Check the curve against a groupby of the real rate" (Lab A's TODO 5 does exactly that for the hour).
Guided lab

File: learners open labs/module-01/l7_1-interpreting-models/fraud_drivers_starter.py; your reference is labs/module-01/l7_1-interpreting-models/fraud_drivers_solution.py. The same tools on a new question: what drives the FRAUD model (the stratified 70/30 split from Foundations, a regularized booster)? Step 1 is done for them and prints test ROC AUC 0.863 and average precision 0.513.

  • TODO 1 and 2 together on the projector (5m). Permutation importance by average precision, then by ROC AUC. Pause on the surprise before anyone explains it: hour costs 0.074 of AP and nothing (-0.005) of AUC.
  • TODO 3 to 5 in pairs (8m). The hour dial (night 0.0174 vs day 0.0072), the same dial for known and new devices (2 a.m.: 0.006 vs 0.122), and the check against the data (night 6.40% real, 5.89% predicted; under 1% in every daytime band).
  • Debrief (2m): "Which metric would the review team want, and why?" Average precision: they work from the top of the list, and the hour pushes a few night frauds there. Then: "Is the hour effect the same for every payment?" No: it is a night-and-new-device interaction. Lesson 7.2's lab finds it again in SHAP values.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-01/l7_1-interpreting-models/pipeline_importance_starter.py: the full Foundations pipeline with one-hot categories. Odds ratios with the pipeline's own column names; Asheville looks protective (0.66) while region Southeast looks risky (1.32), and loyalty tier gets 1.48 although Gold churns least; permutation importance per business column puts all five descriptive columns at zero. Then labs/module-01/l7_1-interpreting-models/debug_interpret.py: two loud bugs and two silent ones.
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is the churn-drivers briefing: a ranked table, grouped importance and turning points, saved as churn_drivers.csv. In the room, Steps 1 and 2 (10 minutes).

  • Read the brief aloud ("Pinecrest's retention team meets on Monday and wants one page"). Step 1: train and confirm test ROC AUC 0.865 (baseline 0.500). Step 2: permutation importance with mean and std; the top line is days_since_last_order 0.140 0.012.
  • Goes home: Step 3 (group_importance: purchase volume 0.010, more than its single columns' 0.004), Step 4 (turning points: recency lowest 0.072 at 18 days, highest 0.490 at about 293), Step 5 (churn_drivers.csv, 11 rows, with the caveat line).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • What does np.exp of a scaled logistic-regression coefficient give you? Answer: an odds ratio per standard deviation of that feature, other features held fixed.
  • Why permutation importance on the TEST set? Answer: on training rows the model's memorized noise looks important; on new rows it does not help, so the test numbers are the honest ones.
  • A feature has permutation importance 0. Is it unrelated to churn? Answer: not necessarily: the model may get the same information from overlapping columns (spend from orders and order value). Shuffle the group together.
  • What does a fan of diverging ICE lines under a flat PDP tell you? Answer: an interaction: the feature's effect depends on another feature, and the average hides it.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q1 C) One more standard deviation of days without an order multiplies the odds of churn by about 2.4, other features held fixed; Q2 [1.49, 0.5]; Q3 B) Impurity importance cannot be trusted for the ranking: it is measured on training data and rewards columns the trees can memorize with; Q4 D) Importance depends on the metric: the hour lifts a few night frauds to the very top of the list, which average precision rewards and the whole-ranking AUC hardly notices; Q5 [ 2. 12.]; Q6 A) Customers with long gaps since their last order are predicted to churn more; the risk jumps after about four months. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: a card with the three boxes (recipe, taste test, dial) and one line each on what it answers. In the lab, let a struggling pair copy the permutation_importance call from the demo notebook and change only the model, the data and scoring.

Stretch: Lab 7.1 B; then the lesson's Exercise 4 (permutation importance by hand) and the Bonus Challenge (add cancellation_reason and watch a routine importance check catch the leak).

7.2

SHAP Values

70 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain the Shapley idea (average contribution over every order in which features join) and compute it by hand for a tiny model
  • Compute SHAP values for a gradient-boosting model with shap.TreeExplainer and check that they add up to the prediction
  • Read waterfall, beeswarm and scatter plots, including the units (log-odds) they are drawn in
  • Use a model-agnostic explainer on a pipeline, and turn SHAP values into plain-language reason codes
Timing in the room
10mFrame: why this customer? Splitting a team bonus fairly; base value + shares = prediction
25mLive demo: Shapley by hand, the Exact explainer, TreeExplainer and additivity, log-odds, one customer, mean |SHAP|, the interaction, a PermutationExplainer, two errors
20mGuided lab: shapley_by_hand_starter.py in pairs (8m), then fraud_reasons_starter.py (12m)
15mStart the lesson project: reasons for the call list, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

"A team of three wins a bonus. How much did each person contribute? 'What would the team have earned without Ana?' is unfair when people work together, because the answer depends on who else is in the room. Lloyd Shapley's answer, in 1953: imagine them arriving one at a time, in every possible order; each time someone arrives, credit them with how much the result went up; average each person's credit over all the orders."

Then the translation, on the board: the players are the features; the bonus is how far this customer's prediction is from the model's average prediction (the base value). Under it: base value + all shares = this prediction, exactly. And the warning, boxed: for a boosting classifier, all of it is in log-odds; squash only the end points with expit.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook, lesson_7_2. The toy model first, so that every number can be checked by hand; then the churn model, so that the same bookkeeping holds for 1,500 real customers.

  1. Shapley values by hand (4m). "A toy risk score with an interaction: a long silence is worse without the app. Let the features arrive in every order and credit each with what its arrival adds." Ask for a prediction first: how will the 0.10 interaction bonus be split? 
    import itertools

def toy_risk(days, disc, app):
    risk = 0.10
    if days > 150:
        risk += 0.30
    if disc > 0.35:
        risk += 0.15
    if days > 150 and app == 0:
        risk += 0.10
    return risk

reference = {"days": 40, "disc": 0.15, "app": 1}   # a typical customer
customer = {"days": 240, "disc": 0.45, "app": 0}   # the customer to explain
names = list(customer)

def value(present):
    """Risk when the features in `present` take the customer's values and the rest the reference's."""
    return toy_risk(**{n: customer[n] if n in present else reference[n] for n in names})

shares = dict.fromkeys(names, 0.0)
orders = list(itertools.permutations(names))
for order in orders:
    present = set()
    for name in order:
        before = value(present)
        present.add(name)
        shares[name] += value(present) - before
shares = {n: round(s / len(orders), 3) for n, s in shares.items()}

print("orders tried:", len(orders))
print("reference risk:", value(set()), " customer risk:", value(set(names)))
print("Shapley values:", shares, " sum:", round(sum(shares.values()), 3))
    orders tried: 6
reference risk: 0.1  customer risk: 0.65
Shapley values: {'days': 0.35, 'disc': 0.15, 'app': 0.05}  sum: 0.55
    Days gets 0.30 + 0.05, app gets 0.05: whichever of the two arrives second collects the 0.10, and each arrives second in half the orders. The shares add up to the gap, 0.55, exactly.
  2. The library agrees (1m). "shap's Exact explainer tries every subset, like our loop." 
    import numpy as np
import shap

toy_model = lambda rows: np.array([toy_risk(*row) for row in rows])
toy_explainer = shap.explainers.Exact(toy_model, np.array([[40, 0.15, 1]]))
print(toy_explainer(np.array([[240, 0.45, 0]])).values.round(3))
    [[0.35 0.15 0.05]]
    With 11 features there are 39,916,800 orders: that is why SHAP's fast tree algorithm matters.
  3. Back to the churn model (1m). "Same 11 features, same split as 7.1." 
    import pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingClassifier
from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user",
            "email_opens_90d"]
X = customers[features].astype(float)
y = customers["churned"]
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=42)

hgb = HistGradientBoostingClassifier(random_state=42).fit(X_train, y_train)
print(f"test ROC AUC {roc_auc_score(y_test, hgb.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]):.3f}")
    test ROC AUC 0.865
  4. TreeExplainer, and the bookkeeping (2m). "One row of 11 shares per customer. Base value plus shares must equal the model's output: check it." 
    explainer = shap.TreeExplainer(hgb)
sv = explainer(X_test)

print(type(sv).__name__, sv.values.shape)
print(f"base value: {sv.base_values[0]:.3f}")

raw_score = hgb.decision_function(X_test)
gap = np.abs(sv.base_values + sv.values.sum(axis=1) - raw_score).max()
print(f"largest additivity gap over 1,500 customers: {gap:.1e}")
    Explanation (1500, 11)
base value: -2.951
largest additivity gap over 1,500 customers: 1.5e-14
    Stress which output: decision_function, the raw score, not predict_proba.
  5. The units are log-odds (2m). "Boosting adds trees in log-odds and squashes at the very end. SHAP explains the sum before the squash." 
    from scipy.special import expit   # the logistic function: log-odds to probability

print(f"base value as a probability: {expit(sv.base_values[0]):.3f}")
print(f"average predicted probability: {hgb.predict_proba(X_train)[:, 1].mean():.3f}")
    base value as a probability: 0.050
average predicted probability: 0.166
    5% is not 16.6%: the squash of an average is not the average of the squashes. Compare sizes and signs of SHAP values freely; never add them to a probability.
  6. Why this customer? (3m). "The riskiest test customer. What should the agent talk about?" Have the room guess the top three before you run it (this was 7.1's Before the Next Lesson task). 
    proba = hgb.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
i = int(proba.argmax())
row_id = X_test.index[i]
print(customers.loc[row_id, "customer_id"], f"churn probability {proba[i]:.3f}")

why = pd.DataFrame({"value": X_test.iloc[i], "shap": sv.values[i]}).sort_values("shap", ascending=False)
print(why.round(3))
    C104051 churn probability 0.972
                         value   shap
days_since_last_order  364.000  3.095
discount_share           0.432  1.827
support_tickets_12m      3.000  0.767
app_user                 0.000  0.316
orders_12m               2.000  0.267
return_rate              0.051  0.157
spend_12m              223.780  0.110
email_opens_90d          6.000  0.084
avg_order_value        111.890  0.061
returns_12m              0.000 -0.032
tenure_months           65.000 -0.149
    Silence first (+3.10 log-odds), discounts (+1.83), complaints (+0.77); a long tenure pulls slightly the other way. Draw the waterfall on the board as a staircase, or show it on the lesson page.
  7. Speak in probabilities at the end points only (2m). "Squash the start and the end, not the bars." 
    start = sv.base_values[i]
end = start + sv.values[i].sum()
print(f"log-odds {start:.2f} -> {end:.2f}")
print(f"probability {expit(start):.3f} -> {expit(end):.3f}")
    log-odds -2.95 -> 3.55
probability 0.050 -> 0.972
  8. From 1,500 explanations to the big picture (2m). "Average the sizes and you get a global importance." 
    mean_abs = pd.Series(np.abs(sv.values).mean(axis=0), index=features).sort_values(ascending=False)
print(mean_abs.round(3))
    days_since_last_order    0.903
tenure_months            0.802
discount_share           0.742
email_opens_90d          0.278
orders_12m               0.278
app_user                 0.273
support_tickets_12m      0.267
return_rate              0.193
spend_12m                0.171
avg_order_value          0.137
returns_12m              0.043
dtype: float64
    Same top three as 7.1's permutation importance; tenure and discounts swap. Not a contradiction: mean |SHAP| measures how much a feature moves predictions, permutation how much the score depends on it.
  9. The beeswarm (optional, 2 minutes) (2m). "Every customer is a dot: position is the share, color is the feature's value." Run it if your projector shows plots well; otherwise show the lesson page's figure. 
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

plt.figure()
shap.plots.beeswarm(sv, max_display=11, show=False)
plt.gcf().set_size_inches(9, 6)
plt.title("SHAP values for 1,500 test customers")
plt.tight_layout()
plt.show()
    Read recency's long red tail: most customers ordered recently and get a small negative share; a few silent ones get a large positive one.
  10. The interaction, in SHAP numbers (2m). "7.1's tickets-and-app interaction, now from 1,500 explanations." 
    tickets = pd.DataFrame({
    "tickets": X_test["support_tickets_12m"].clip(upper=4),
    "app_user": X_test["app_user"],
    "shap": sv[:, "support_tickets_12m"].values,
})
print(tickets.pivot_table(index="tickets", columns="app_user", values="shap", aggfunc="mean").round(2))
    app_user   0.0   1.0
tickets             
0.0      -0.39 -0.23
1.0       0.09  0.07
2.0       0.35  0.23
3.0       0.78  0.46
4.0       0.92  0.40
    Non-app users at 4+ tickets: +0.92 log-odds; app users +0.40.
  11. Any model: the PermutationExplainer (2m). "TreeExplainer only reads trees. For a pipeline, pass a function and a background sample." 
    from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression

logreg = make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median"), StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression())
logreg.fit(X_train, y_train)

def churn_probability(rows):
    return logreg.predict_proba(pd.DataFrame(rows, columns=features))[:, 1]

background = X_train.sample(100, random_state=42)
perm_explainer = shap.PermutationExplainer(churn_probability, background, seed=42)
lr_sv = perm_explainer(X_test.iloc[[i]])

lr_why = pd.Series(lr_sv.values[0], index=features).sort_values(ascending=False)
print(f"base {lr_sv.base_values[0]:.3f} + shares {lr_sv.values[0].sum():.3f} = {churn_probability(X_test.iloc[[i]])[0]:.3f}")
print(lr_why.round(3).head(5))
    base 0.157 + shares 0.791 = 0.948
days_since_last_order    0.506
discount_share           0.113
support_tickets_12m      0.104
orders_12m               0.037
app_user                 0.028
dtype: float64
    Probability units now, because the function returns probabilities. Two different models, the same story for this customer.
  12. Errors on purpose (1m). "Two mistakes everybody makes once." 
    shap.TreeExplainer(logreg)

wrong = proba.mean() + sv.values[i].sum()
right = expit(sv.base_values[i] + sv.values[i].sum())
print(f"wrong: {wrong:.2f}   right: {right:.3f}")
    InvalidModelError: Model type not yet supported by TreeExplainer: <class 'sklearn.pipeline.Pipeline'>
wrong: 6.65   right: 0.972
    First: TreeExplainer cannot read a Pipeline (use PermutationExplainer, or explain pipe[-1] on pipe[:-1].transform(X)). Second: adding log-odds to a probability gives 6.65 with no error. Add to the base value, then expit.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Adding log-odds to a probability. "The base is 16% and discounts add 1.83, so ..." Coach: "What units is the base value in? What does expit(-2.95) give?" Then the rule: add in log-odds, squash at the end. Lab 7.2 A's base value (-5.84 log-odds, 0.0029) is the same trap on fraud.
  • Handing a Pipeline to TreeExplainer, or a forest's 3-D values to a plot. Two errors with clear last lines. Coach: "Print .values.shape before you plot a new model's explanation." A forest gives (rows, features, 2): pick class 1.
  • Reading a top SHAP feature as the cause. An agent offers fewer discounts because discount_share got +1.83. Coach: "flagged because", never "leaving because"; reasons start a conversation, they do not prescribe a treatment.

One types, one predicts: how is the 0.30 bonus split, and what does gift_card get? Expected: 24 orders; velocity 0.2, new_device 0.15, night 0.15, gift_card 0.0, sum 0.5; shap Exact agrees; against a typical NIGHT payment, night drops to 0.0 and new_device rises to 0.3 (the total stays 0.5).

Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • What do base value and the shares add up to for the boosting churn model? Answer: the model's raw score in log-odds (decision_function), exactly.
  • Why is the base value 5% when 16.6% of customers churn? Answer: it is the average log-odds, squashed; the squash of an average is not the average of the squashed values.
  • When do you need a PermutationExplainer instead of TreeExplainer? Answer: for any model TreeExplainer cannot read: a Pipeline, a linear model on its own data, a neural network, a model behind an API.
  • Why only positive shares as reasons on a call list? Answer: the agent wants what raised the risk; negative shares are protective factors (useful for deciding whom NOT to call).

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q7 B) 0, because adding it never changes the score in any arrival order; Q8 0.0 0.5; Q9 C) Mean |SHAP| measures how much a feature moves predictions; permutation importance measures how much the score depends on it, and the two need not agree; Q10 D) forest_sv[0, :, 1]; Q11 A) The base value and the shares change; the customer's predicted probability does not; Q12 43% of spend on discounted items | no order in 364 days. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Put the three questions on the slide. Learners answer in the workbook's Exit Ticket box or on a sticky note. Sort the notes before Session 2: the pile for question 2 tells you how much of Session 2's warm-up goes to units.

  1. In one sentence each: what does permutation importance tell you about a model, what does partial dependence tell you, and what does neither tell you? Answer: how much the held-out score depends on a feature; how the average prediction moves as one feature changes; neither says what would happen if Pinecrest changed that feature (cause and effect).
  2. A SHAP waterfall for the boosting churn model starts at -2.95 and ends at +0.50. What churn probability does the model give, and why can you not add the bars to 16.5%? Answer: expit(0.50) = 0.62. The bars are log-odds; 16.5% is a probability, and the base value is -2.95 log-odds (5%), not 16.5%.
  3. Write the reason code an agent could read aloud for days_since_last_order = 240 with a SHAP value of +2.1, and one thing the agent must not say. Answer: "no order in 240 days" (neutral, factual, about the account). Not: "you are leaving because you stopped buying", or any promise based on the model ("order now and you will stay").

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: every machine's check result; 7.1 Steps 4 and 5; 7.2 Steps 4 to 7; Lab 7.2 B (Shapley by hand) in full.
  • Cut first (saves 4m): 7.2 Step 9 (beeswarm) and Step 10 (tickets pivot): show the lesson figures and say the two sentences.
  • Cut second (saves 5m): 7.1 Steps 6 and 8 become one sentence each ("the line gets 0.003 from tenure, boosting 0.064"; "tickets matter for non-app users only").
  • Cut third (saves 12m): Lab 7.2 A moves to homework; do only its TODO 1 together (the additivity check and the base value in two units).
Running short: what to add
  • Run Lab 7.1 B for everyone and debate the Asheville/Southeast and tier puzzles: why does a sensible model give "wrong-looking" coefficients?
  • Lesson 7.1 Exercise 3 live: bin recency and compare the real churn rate with the PDP (0.133 → 0.416 between 91 to 150 and 151 to 250 days).
  • Lesson 7.2 Exercise 1 live: the safest customer (C101889, 0.0002), and why protective reasons are not the riskiest customer's reasons reversed.
Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 2: Local Explanations, Fairness and K-Means

How to teach the second session of Advanced Machine Learning: explain one prediction with a flat local map (LIME), learn when not to trust it, and audit the churn campaign for fairness across age groups (7.3); then leave labels behind and find customer groups with K-Means, learning the algorithm, its pitfalls and why scaling is a choice (8.1).

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 7.3–8.1 Session 2 of 13

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm as every session: warm-up → for each lesson: frame and mental model → live demo → guided lab → start the lesson project; exercises, quiz and journal go home.
  • Every code block below was run to produce the output printed under it (Python 3.12, scikit-learn 1.9.1, shap 0.53, lime 0.2, 4 CPU threads). LIME is random: the outputs match because every explainer gets random_state.
  • Keep interpretability_lime.html and clustering_kmeans.html open for the figures (LIME's bar chart, the four-panel Lloyd figure, the stuck run, raw vs scaled scatter, moons). Labs live in labs/module-02/.
  • This session changes gear at the break: Module 7 ends with fairness, Module 8 starts unsupervised learning. Say so out loud; the second half has no target column at all.
The #1 risk of Session 2: fairness becomes a debate, or a verdict

The age-group table invites strong reactions ("the model discriminates against young people!") and equally strong dismissals ("the model never sees age, so it is fair"). Both skip the lesson. Keep the room on the procedure: which harm, which metric, how many people per group, is the gap bigger than chance, what was not checked. The permutation test is the hinge: practice saying "no evidence of a difference, and not enough data to rule one out" before the session. Do not let the audit drift into whether Pinecrest should collect age at all; park it for the journal prompt.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Run the kit check: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-02 (8 files, about a minute). LIME and K-Means labs are all under 30 seconds.
  • ☐ Projector machine: notebooks lesson_7_3 and lesson_8_1; run import lime, shap once beforehand (first imports are slow). Browser tabs: the two lesson pages.
  • ☐ Downloads: none. (Image data is needed from Session 5; remind anyone whose check said READY for Sessions 1 to 4.)
  • ☐ Board: "fidelity · stability" (two checks before trusting LIME); the three fairness views (flag rate, recall, precision) with "which harm?" above them; for 8.1, a sketch of campfires: assign, move, repeat.
  • ☐ Sort the Session 1 exit tickets. Question 2 (units) usually splits the room; W3 in the warm-up revisits it.

Warm-up

0:00

Retrieval: Interpreting Models and SHAP

10 min
Questions (on the slide; learners answer in the workbook)
W1
A scaled logistic regression gives discount_share a coefficient of 0.68. Say what that means in one sentence.
W2
Shuffling days_since_last_order in the TEST set lowers the boosting model's ROC AUC by 0.140. What does that measure, and why the test set?
W3
What does this print?
import numpy as np
from scipy.special import expit
base = -2.95                     # C104051's waterfall starts here (log-odds)
total_shares = 6.50              # and its 11 SHAP values add up to this
print(round(base + total_shares, 2), round(expit(base + total_shares), 3))
W4
Why should a briefing never rank features with a forest's feature_importances_?
W5
Which phrase can an agent read aloud: "you are leaving because you stopped buying" or "no order in 240 days"? Why?
Answers
  • W1 One more standard deviation of discount share multiplies the odds of churn by e^0.68, about 2.0, other features held fixed.
  • W2 How much the model's held-out score depends on that feature (permutation importance); on training rows memorized noise would look important too.
  • W3 3.55 0.972: base + shares = 3.55 log-odds; expit(3.55) = 0.972, C104051's churn probability.
  • W4 It is measured on training data and favors many-valued columns: in Lesson 7.1 it ranked a column of pure noise sixth.
  • W5 "No order in 240 days": a neutral fact about the account. The other turns a model's association into a claim about the person's motives.
How to run it

Four minutes writing, four minutes cold-calling, two minutes on the homework: who finished both projects? Ask one learner to read their three reason codes for C104051 aloud, and the room to vote on whether an agent could say them. W3 is the units question from Session 1's exit ticket: if more than a third get it wrong, redo the end points on the board (add in log-odds, squash at the end).

Lesson Plans

7.3

LIME and Fairness Checks

70 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Describe LIME's recipe (perturb, predict, weight by closeness, fit a simple model) and build a basic version by hand
  • Explain a prediction with lime.lime_tabular.LimeTabularExplainer and judge it by its fidelity score and its stability across random seeds
  • Say when LIME and SHAP answer different questions, and run simple what-if checks on one customer
  • Run a fairness check: compare flag rate, recall and precision across age groups and test whether a gap is larger than chance
Timing in the room
10mFrame: a flat map near one customer; fidelity and stability; then: does the model treat groups alike?
25mLive demo: LIME by hand, the library, stability, a what-if, the age audit, a permutation test, two errors
20mGuided lab: lime_fraud_starter.py (12m), then fairness_calibration_starter.py TODO 1 and 2 in pairs (8m)
15mStart the lesson project: auditing the retention campaign, Steps 1 and 2
Mental model to say

"The Earth is round, but a map of your neighborhood can be flat and still be accurate, because you only use it nearby. LIME draws a flat map of the model around one customer: make thousands of nearby variants, ask the model about each, weight them by closeness, fit a straight line. Useful for 'what pushes this customer's risk around?'; misleading if you carry it across the country."

Then the second half, a different question entirely: "Explanations tell you how a model decides. A fairness check asks whether its decisions land differently on different groups of people. Not using a sensitive attribute guarantees nothing; you have to measure, and measure honestly when groups are small."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook, lesson_7_3.

  1. Same model, same customer (2m). "Lesson 7.2's model and its riskiest customer. This time we split the whole table, because we will need the age groups later." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingClassifier

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user",
            "email_opens_90d"]
train, test = train_test_split(customers, test_size=0.25, stratify=customers["churned"], random_state=42)
X_train, X_test = train[features].astype(float), test[features].astype(float)

model = HistGradientBoostingClassifier(random_state=42).fit(X_train, train["churned"])
proba = model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
i = int(proba.argmax())
x0 = X_test.iloc[i]
print(test["customer_id"].iloc[i], f"churn probability {proba[i]:.3f}")
    C104051 churn probability 0.972
  2. LIME by hand: the flat map (5m). "Make 3,000 variants of this customer, ask the model about each, weight each by how close it is, fit a weighted straight line. The line's slopes are the explanation." Walk the four numbered comments. 
    from sklearn.linear_model import Ridge

rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
sd = X_train.std()

# 1. variants near the customer, kept inside the training range
noise = rng.normal(size=(3000, len(features)))
variants = pd.DataFrame(x0.to_numpy() + noise * sd.to_numpy(), columns=features)
variants = variants.clip(lower=X_train.min(), upper=X_train.max(), axis=1)
variants["app_user"] = (variants["app_user"] > 0.5).astype(float)

# 2. ask the model
scores = model.predict_proba(variants)[:, 1]

# 3. weight by closeness (distance in standard deviations)
steps = (variants - x0) / sd
distance = np.sqrt((steps ** 2).sum(axis=1))
width = 0.75 * np.sqrt(len(features))        # LIME's default kernel width
weights = np.exp(-distance ** 2 / width ** 2)

# 4. fit a simple weighted model: its slopes are the explanation
surrogate = Ridge(alpha=1.0).fit(steps, scores, sample_weight=weights)
local = pd.Series(surrogate.coef_, index=features).sort_values(key=abs, ascending=False)
print(local.round(3).head(5))
print(f"fidelity (weighted R2): {surrogate.score(steps, scores, sample_weight=weights):.3f}")
print(f"surrogate at the customer: {surrogate.intercept_:.3f}   model: {proba[i]:.3f}")
    discount_share           0.086
tenure_months            0.080
app_user                -0.059
days_since_last_order    0.050
orders_12m              -0.016
dtype: float64
fidelity (weighted R2): 0.496
surrogate at the customer: 0.897   model: 0.972
    The surprise: recency is NOT the top slope, though SHAP gave it the biggest share. Ask why before you explain: at 364 days the risk curve is already flat, so nudging recency changes little; SHAP measures the gap from a typical customer, LIME the sensitivity nearby. Both right, different questions. Fidelity 0.50 means "roughly".
  3. The lime library (3m). "The package wraps the same recipe. Two details: fill missing values first, and give it a function that returns probabilities." 
    from lime.lime_tabular import LimeTabularExplainer

fill = X_train.median()

def predict_fn(rows):
    return model.predict_proba(pd.DataFrame(rows, columns=features))

lime_explainer = LimeTabularExplainer(
    X_train.fillna(fill).to_numpy(), feature_names=features,
    class_names=["stayed", "churned"], mode="classification", random_state=42,
)
exp = lime_explainer.explain_instance(x0.fillna(fill).to_numpy(), predict_fn, num_features=5, num_samples=5000)

for rule, weight in exp.as_list():
    print(f"{rule:35s} {weight:+.3f}")
print(f"fidelity {exp.score:.3f}   surrogate prediction {exp.local_pred[0]:.3f}   model {proba[i]:.3f}")
    days_since_last_order > 110.00      +0.295
discount_share > 0.29               +0.238
39.00 < tenure_months <= 68.00      -0.069
support_tickets_12m > 1.00          +0.066
app_user <= 0.00                    +0.060
fidelity 0.512   surrogate prediction 0.580   model 0.972
    Binned rules ("days > 110") turn the question back to "what about this customer is unusual and risky?", so the ranking resembles SHAP's. Point at the last line: the surrogate says 0.58 for a 0.97 customer.
  4. Is it stable? (3m). "LIME is random. Rerun with three seeds, at 300 and at 5,000 variants." 
    for n_samples in [300, 5000]:
    print(f"num_samples={n_samples}")
    for seed in [1, 2, 3]:
        explainer_s = LimeTabularExplainer(X_train.fillna(fill).to_numpy(), feature_names=features,
                                           class_names=["stayed", "churned"], random_state=seed)
        e = explainer_s.explain_instance(x0.fillna(fill).to_numpy(), predict_fn, num_features=3,
                                         num_samples=n_samples)
        print(f"  seed {seed}:", " | ".join(f"{rule} {w:+.2f}" for rule, w in e.as_list()))
    num_samples=300
  seed 1: days_since_last_order > 110.00 +0.29 | discount_share > 0.29 +0.27 | 39.00 < tenure_months <= 68.00 -0.07
  seed 2: days_since_last_order > 110.00 +0.29 | discount_share > 0.29 +0.21 | support_tickets_12m > 1.00 +0.08
  seed 3: days_since_last_order > 110.00 +0.29 | discount_share > 0.29 +0.25 | support_tickets_12m > 1.00 +0.07
num_samples=5000
  seed 1: days_since_last_order > 110.00 +0.30 | discount_share > 0.29 +0.24 | 39.00 < tenure_months <= 68.00 -0.08
  seed 2: days_since_last_order > 110.00 +0.30 | discount_share > 0.29 +0.24 | 39.00 < tenure_months <= 68.00 -0.07
  seed 3: days_since_last_order > 110.00 +0.30 | discount_share > 0.29 +0.26 | 39.00 < tenure_months <= 68.00 -0.07
    The top two hold everywhere; the third reason wanders at 300 variants and settles at 5,000. On the board: fidelity and stability, the two checks before trusting a LIME explanation.
  5. A what-if check (2m). "The simplest local explanation: change one thing and ask again." 
    what_if = X_test.iloc[[i]].copy()
for days in [364, 200, 150, 120, 60]:
    what_if["days_since_last_order"] = days
    print(f"{days:3d} days -> predicted churn {model.predict_proba(what_if)[0, 1]:.3f}")
    364 days -> predicted churn 0.972
200 days -> predicted churn 0.899
150 days -> predicted churn 0.716
120 days -> predicted churn 0.619
 60 days -> predicted churn 0.645
    Even at 60 days the model says 0.645: discounts and tickets still hold the risk up. And a what-if describes the model's answer, not what the customer would do.
  6. A fairness audit by age group (4m). "The model never sees age. That settles nothing: other features can act as proxies. Age is used here only to audit, never as an input." Write the three definitions on the board: flag rate, recall, precision. 
    audit = test[["customer_id", "age_group", "churned"]].copy()
audit["age"] = audit["age_group"].fillna("unknown")
audit["p"] = proba
audit["flag"] = audit["p"] >= audit["p"].quantile(0.80)   # calls for the top 20%

by_age = audit.groupby("age").agg(customers=("churned", "size"), churners=("churned", "sum"),
                                  churn_rate=("churned", "mean"), flag_rate=("flag", "mean"))
by_age["recall"] = audit[audit["churned"] == 1].groupby("age")["flag"].mean()
by_age["precision"] = audit[audit["flag"]].groupby("age")["churned"].mean()
print(by_age.round(3))
             customers  churners  churn_rate  flag_rate  recall  precision
age                                                                   
18-24          162        28       0.173      0.173   0.536      0.536
25-34          309        55       0.178      0.184   0.564      0.544
35-44          295        51       0.173      0.173   0.569      0.569
45-54          255        31       0.122      0.212   0.710      0.407
55-64          204        34       0.167      0.255   0.765      0.500
65+            147        24       0.163      0.211   0.708      0.548
unknown        128        25       0.195      0.211   0.680      0.630
    77% of churners aged 55 to 64 are reached, 54% of those aged 18 to 24: a 23-point gap. Before anyone reacts, point at the churners column: 28 people.
  7. Is the gap more than chance? (3m). "Shuffle the age labels among the churners, so age has nothing to do with being flagged, and see how big the gap gets by chance. 2,000 times." 
    def recall_gap(frame):
    recall = frame.groupby("age")["flag"].mean()
    return recall.max() - recall.min()

churners = audit[audit["churned"] == 1]
observed = recall_gap(churners)

rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
shuffled_gaps = np.array([recall_gap(churners.assign(age=rng.permutation(churners["age"].to_numpy())))
                          for _ in range(2000)])
print(f"observed recall gap: {observed:.3f}")
print(f"typical gap by chance (median): {np.median(shuffled_gaps):.3f}")
print(f"share of shuffles with a gap at least as large: {(shuffled_gaps >= observed).mean():.3f}")
    observed recall gap: 0.229
typical gap by chance (median): 0.223
share of shuffles with a gap at least as large: 0.473
    Half the shuffles produce a gap this large. The honest sentence: no evidence that recall differs by age, and not enough data to rule out a moderate difference.
  8. Errors on purpose (3m). "Two LIME errors whose messages do not say what is wrong." 
    broken = LimeTabularExplainer(X_train.to_numpy(), feature_names=features, random_state=42)
broken.explain_instance(x0.fillna(fill).to_numpy(), predict_fn)

lime_explainer.explain_instance(x0.fillna(fill).to_numpy(),
                                lambda rows: model.predict(pd.DataFrame(rows, columns=features)))
    ValueError: Domain error in arguments. The `scale` parameter must be positive for all distributions, and many  ...
NotImplementedError: LIME does not currently support classifier models without probability scores. I ...
    The first is missing values in email_opens_90d (LIME's summary statistics become NaN); the second is predict instead of predict_proba. A third (a pandas row instead of .to_numpy()) raises KeyError: 0: it is in debug_lime.py.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Trusting a LIME explanation without its two checks. Coach: "What is exp.score? Is exp.local_pred on the same side of your threshold as the model? Does the top reason survive another seed?" The fraud lab makes this vivid: fidelity 0.12, surrogate 0.077 for a 0.990 payment.
  • "We removed age, so the model is fair." Coach with the audit table: the model never saw age, and recall still varies from 54% to 77%. Whether that is chance is a separate question; you only know because you measured.
  • A gap read as a verdict, or a non-significant test read as proof. Coach the two-part sentence: "no evidence of a difference" AND "groups too small to rule one out". Ask for group sizes every time a rate is quoted.
Guided lab
  • Lab A (12m, everyone): labs/module-02/l7_3-lime-fairness/lime_fraud_starter.py; reference labs/module-02/l7_3-lime-fairness/lime_fraud_solution.py. LIME meets a rare class: three fraud payments at different risk levels. Expected: the riskiest payment (T031633, 0.990) gets velocity and country-mismatch rules with fidelity 0.12 and a surrogate of 0.077; at 500 variants the top rule agrees in 3, 2 and 1 of 3 seeds, at 5,000 in 2, 3 and 3, fidelities 0.05 to 0.35; SHAP names velocity, velocity, new device.
  • Debrief Lab A (2m): "Why does the flat map fail here when it worked roughly on churn?" Fraud is 0.88% of the data: almost every random variant of a payment looks legitimate, so the neighborhood the line is fitted to is nearly flat at zero. For this model, report SHAP.
  • Lab B (8m, pairs, TODO 1 and 2 in class): labs/module-02/l7_3-lime-fairness/fairness_calibration_starter.py; reference labs/module-02/l7_3-lime-fairness/fairness_calibration_solution.py. Two views the lesson did not take: calibration by group and campaign profit by group, at the $15 / $60 break-even (which flags exactly the same 300 customers as the lesson's top 20%). Expected: gaps from -0.042 (35 to 44) to +0.025 (45 to 54); 45 to 54 earns $2.00 per customer, unknown $4.80; the largest gap is matched by 64% of shuffles. TODO 3 and 4 go home.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-02/l7_3-lime-fairness/debug_lime.py: three errors whose messages hide the cause, and one silent problem (100 variants).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is auditing the retention campaign: LIME on three flagged customers with stability checks, flag rates by age with a permutation test, saved as fairness_audit.csv. In the room, Steps 1 and 2 (15 minutes).

  • Step 1: train and flag the top 20%. Done = flagged 300 of 1500; churners among them 52.3%.
  • Step 2: three customers at different risk levels. Done = C104051 fidelity 0.48, C103674 0.36 (its surrogate lands on the other side of 0.5), C104266 0.27. Ask: "Which of these three explanations would you give an agent?"
  • Goes home: Step 3 (stability: 5 of 5 seeds for all three), Step 4 (flag rates by age, permutation p = 0.395), Step 5 (fairness_audit.csv and a note that lists what was NOT checked).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud:

  • What are LIME's four steps? Answer: perturb, predict, weight by closeness, fit a simple weighted model.
  • Why can LIME's top slope differ from SHAP's top share? Answer: the slope is sensitivity nearby; the share is the contribution to the gap from a typical customer. At 364 days the curve is flat, so the slope is small and the share large.
  • Which fairness view matches "a churner who misses out on an offer"? Answer: recall by group (equal opportunity).
  • What does a permutation test of a recall gap shuffle? Answer: the group labels among the churners, so group has nothing to do with being flagged.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6.

Answers: Q1 A) Treat it as unreliable: the flat map does not imitate the model near this payment; report SHAP's reasons, or fewer reasons; Q2 [1. 0.368 0.018]; Q3 C) A single top reason hides the rest: discounts and tickets still hold the risk up, and the what-if describes the model, not the customer; Q4 B) Recall among churners (equal opportunity); Q5 [0.5, 0.67]; Q6 D) No evidence of a real calibration difference at this sample size; the groups are small, so keep monitoring. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: a card with the two LIME checks and the three fairness definitions. In Lab A, let pairs skip the 500-variant loop and only compare seeds at 5,000.

Stretch: Lesson 7.3 Exercise 4 (a counterfactual: the smallest change that flips C104051 below 0.5) and the Bonus Challenge (the recall audit at 10%, 20% and 40% of customers called).

8.1

K-Means Clustering

80 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain what clustering is for and how it differs from the supervised models of Modules 1 to 7
  • Describe Lloyd's algorithm (assign each point to the nearest center, move each center to the mean) and write it in NumPy
  • Fit KMeans and read labels_, cluster_centers_, inertia_ and n_iter_
  • Explain why K-Means needs scaled features and several starts (n_init, k-means++), and show what goes wrong without them
  • Profile clusters in the original units and recognize the cluster shapes K-Means cannot find
Timing in the room
10mFrame: no answer column; campfires on a campground; inertia
30mLive demo: blobs, K-Means, inertia by hand, Lloyd's loop, local minima, k-means++, raw vs scaled, new customers, mistakes
25mGuided lab: scaling_choices_starter.py (12m), then payment_clusters_starter.py (13m)
15mStart the lesson project: Pinecrest's first customer groups, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

"Every model so far learned from examples with an answer. Marketing now asks what kinds of customers Pinecrest has, and nobody has labeled them. Picture a campground at night: hundreds of hikers and k campfires lit at random spots. Each hiker walks to the nearest fire. Each fire is moved to the middle of its crowd. Some hikers are now closer to another fire and walk over; the fires move again. After a few rounds nobody switches."

Then on the board: fires = centroids; walk to the nearest = assign; move to the middle = update; k = the number of fires, you choose it (Lesson 8.2 asks how). The one number each round lowers: inertia, the total squared distance to the own center. And the warning: "K-Means always answers, even in data with no groups. Checking the answer is your job."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook, lesson_8_1.

  1. Data where we know groups exist (2m). "Before real customers, round clouds where we know the answer. The underscore throws the true labels away: a clustering algorithm never sees them." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.datasets import make_blobs
from sklearn.cluster import KMeans

X_toy, _ = make_blobs(n_samples=300, centers=4, cluster_std=1.0, random_state=42)
print(X_toy.shape)
print(X_toy[:3].round(2))
    (300, 2)
[[-9.3   6.47]
 [-9.7   6.94]
 [-1.69  7.79]]
  2. A first K-Means (3m). "An estimator like any other, except that fit takes only X." 
    km = KMeans(n_clusters=4, n_init=10, random_state=42)
km.fit(X_toy)

print(km.labels_[:12])
print(km.cluster_centers_.round(2))
print(f"inertia: {km.inertia_:.1f}   iterations: {km.n_iter_}")
    [3 3 0 1 3 1 2 1 0 2 0 2]
[[-2.71  8.97]
 [-6.83 -6.83]
 [ 4.72  2.04]
 [-8.87  7.17]]
inertia: 564.9   iterations: 2
    Four learned attributes, trailing underscores as always. Labels are names, not ranks.
  3. Inertia is not magic (2m). "Each point's own center, subtract, square, add up." 
    own_center = km.cluster_centers_[km.labels_]      # one row per point: the center of its cluster
inertia_by_hand = ((X_toy - own_center) ** 2).sum()
print(f"{inertia_by_hand:.1f}")
    564.9
    cluster_centers_[labels_] uses the 300 labels as row numbers into the 4-row table of centers.
  4. Lloyd's loop in fifteen lines (5m). "Assign, move, repeat until nothing moves." Narrate the broadcasting line once: every point minus every center at once, shape (points, k, features). 
    def lloyd(X, centers, max_steps=20):
    """Plain K-Means: assign each point to its nearest center, move each center to its points' mean."""
    history = [centers]
    for _ in range(max_steps):
        sq_dist = ((X[:, None, :] - centers[None, :, :]) ** 2).sum(axis=2)   # shape (n_points, k)
        labels = sq_dist.argmin(axis=1)                                      # step 1: assign
        new_centers = np.array([X[labels == j].mean(axis=0) for j in range(len(centers))])  # step 2: move
        history.append(new_centers)
        if np.allclose(new_centers, centers):                                # nothing moved: converged
            break
        centers = new_centers
    return labels, centers, history

rng = np.random.default_rng(12)     # a seed whose random start is clearly poor, so there is something to watch
start = X_toy[rng.choice(len(X_toy), size=4, replace=False)]     # 4 random points as starting centers
labels, centers, history = lloyd(X_toy, start)

my_inertia = ((X_toy - centers[labels]) ** 2).sum()
print(f"rounds: {len(history) - 1}   inertia: {my_inertia:.1f}")
    rounds: 6   inertia: 564.9
    Show the lesson page's four-panel figure of the centers drifting. Same inertia as scikit-learn.
  5. Local minima (3m). "Downhill from where you start is not the lowest point there is. Five purely random starts:" 
    for seed in range(5):
    one_run = KMeans(n_clusters=4, init="random", n_init=1, random_state=seed).fit(X_toy)
    print(f"seed {seed}: inertia {one_run.inertia_:7.1f}   rounds {one_run.n_iter_}")
    seed 0: inertia   564.9   rounds 3
seed 1: inertia  8422.6   rounds 4
seed 2: inertia  2062.1   rounds 4
seed 3: inertia  2059.1   rounds 2
seed 4: inertia   564.9   rounds 2
    Same data, same k: 565 to 8,423. Show the "stuck run" figure: one center between two clouds, two splitting one.
  6. The two defenses (2m). "k-means++ spreads the starting centers out; n_init runs several starts and keeps the best." 
    for seed in range(5):
    one_run = KMeans(n_clusters=4, init="k-means++", n_init=1, random_state=seed).fit(X_toy)
    print(f"seed {seed}: inertia {one_run.inertia_:7.1f}")

print("default n_init:", KMeans(n_clusters=4).n_init)
    seed 0: inertia   564.9
seed 1: inertia   564.9
seed 2: inertia   564.9
seed 3: inertia   564.9
seed 4: inertia   564.9
default n_init: auto
    auto means one run with k-means++. The course writes n_init=10 whenever the result matters.
  7. Real customers, two columns (2m). "Orders run from 1 to 21; order values from about $30 to $625." 
    customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
two = customers[["orders_12m", "avg_order_value"]]
print(two.describe().loc[["mean", "std", "min", "max"]].round(1))
          orders_12m  avg_order_value
mean         5.0            140.9
std          3.2            103.6
min          1.0             29.8
max         21.0            625.2
    Ask: "K-Means measures straight-line distance. Which column will decide the clusters?"
  8. Raw versus scaled (5m). "Cluster the raw numbers, then the standardized ones, and read both sets of centers in dollars and orders." 
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler

raw_km = KMeans(n_clusters=4, n_init=10, random_state=42).fit(two)

scaler2 = StandardScaler().set_output(transform="pandas")
two_scaled = scaler2.fit_transform(two)
scaled_km = KMeans(n_clusters=4, n_init=10, random_state=42).fit(two_scaled)

print("raw centers:")
print(pd.DataFrame(raw_km.cluster_centers_, columns=two.columns).round(1).sort_values("avg_order_value"))
print("scaled centers, back in original units:")
print(pd.DataFrame(scaler2.inverse_transform(scaled_km.cluster_centers_), columns=two.columns)
      .round(1).sort_values("avg_order_value"))
