Opening Segment

0:00

Warm-Up: Density Methods and Mixtures

10 min
How to run it
  • W1 to W5 on the slides, two minutes, no notes, fingers 1 to 4. Run W1 and W3 to settle them.
  • Homework check (2 minutes): who has customer_density_segments.csv and customer_soft_segments.csv? Ask for one borderline and one atypical customer from a journal: the distinction is today's bridge.
Warm-up answers

Answers: W1 B) [ 0 0 0 1 1 1 -1] 2; W2 B) It has fit_predict but no predict: it describes the data it saw; W3 C) 1.0; W4 B) The big drop is from 1 to 2; after that gains shrink: choose 2 to 4 and defend it with profiles; W5 B) Atypical.

  • W1 wrong (3 clusters): the noise label again. Today's predict(-1/1) convention will confuse the same learners: pair them in the lab.
  • W3 wrong (a very small number): they expect probabilities to fall far from the data. That is exactly what score_samples measures, the 8.6 anomaly score.
  • W5 wrong: spend one extra minute; borderline (low top probability) and atypical (low density) are the two ideas 8.6 builds on.

Lesson Plans

8.6

Anomaly Detection

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Tell point, contextual and collective anomalies apart and choose between supervised and unsupervised detection
  • Apply simple statistical rules (z-score, interquartile range) as a baseline
  • Score anomalies with Isolation Forest and Local Outlier Factor, and read the -1/1 and score conventions correctly
  • Choose a threshold from a review budget instead of tuning contamination
  • Evaluate a detector with ROC AUC, average precision and catches within a budget on a time-based split
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the night guard; point, contextual and collective anomalies; supervised vs unsupervised vs novelty
25mLive demo: rules, isolation by hand, IF vs LOF, contamination, three convention mistakes, accuracy
25mGuided lab: monthly_queue_starter.py, then unusual_customers_starter.py in pairs
15mStart the lesson project: A Fraud Review Queue, Steps 1 and 2
Mental model to say

"A night guard in a big building does not carry a list of every possible burglary. After a few weeks they know what normal sounds like: the server room hum, the cleaner at 2 a.m., the heating at five. Anything that does not fit gets a closer look. Most closer looks are nothing, and that is fine: the point is to look in the right places."

Then the three kinds (point, contextual, collective) with one Pinecrest example each, and the setup table: supervised (labels, Lesson 2.5), unsupervised (no labels, training data may contain anomalies), novelty (trained on clean normal data). "Labels for fraud arrive weeks late and only for the fraud someone noticed. A detector works on day one."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_8_6. Steps follow the lesson's sections, then its mistakes.

  1. How rare is fraud? (2m). "Under 1% of payments. Remember the number every detector must beat: doing nothing is 99.1% accurate." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
pd.set_option("display.width", 150)
tx = pd.read_csv("data/transactions.csv", parse_dates=["timestamp"])
print(tx.shape)
print(f"fraud: {tx['is_fraud'].sum()} of {len(tx):,} ({tx['is_fraud'].mean():.3%})")
    (50000, 13)
fraud: 438 of 50,000 (0.876%)
  2. Two classic rules (3m). "One column and a threshold. The z-score uses the mean and standard deviation, which the extremes drag around; the IQR fence uses quartiles." 
    amount = tx["amount"]
z = (amount - amount.mean()) / amount.std()
q1, q3 = amount.quantile([0.25, 0.75])
upper_fence = q3 + 1.5 * (q3 - q1)
rules = {"z-score above 3": z > 3, f"IQR: amount above ${upper_fence:.0f}": amount > upper_fence}
for name, flagged in rules.items():
    print(f"{name:26s} flags {flagged.sum():5d}, fraud among flagged {tx.loc[flagged, 'is_fraud'].mean():.1%}, "
          f"frauds caught {tx.loc[flagged, 'is_fraud'].sum()} of {tx['is_fraud'].sum()}")
    z-score above 3            flags   909, fraud among flagged 4.7%, frauds caught 43 of 438
IQR: amount above $482     flags  2693, fraud among flagged 4.3%, frauds caught 115 of 438
    Both raise the fraud rate among flagged payments far above 0.9%, and both miss most fraud and flag hundreds of honest tents. Rules are the baseline, and they stay in real systems because they are explainable.
  3. Isolation by hand (3m). "Pick a random cut between the min and max; keep the side with your point; repeat. A point in a crowd needs many cuts; an odd one, very few." Draw a number line and do two cuts on the board. 
    rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
amounts = np.append(rng.normal(150, 40, size=30).round(2), 900.0)

def isolation_depth(values, target, rng):
    """Random cuts needed until target is the only value left."""
    depth = 0
    while len(values) > 1:
        cut = rng.uniform(values.min(), values.max())
        values = values[values < cut] if target < cut else values[values >= cut]
        depth += 1
    return depth

for target in [np.median(amounts), 900.0]:
    depths = [isolation_depth(amounts, target, rng) for _ in range(1000)]
    print(f"amount {target:7.2f}: average cuts to isolate {np.mean(depths):.2f}")
    amount  155.11: average cuts to isolate 7.55
amount  900.00: average cuts to isolate 1.15
    That is the whole idea; a forest does it with many random trees in all features at once.
  4. Isolation Forest vs Local Outlier Factor (4m). "A tight cluster, a loose cluster, five planted oddballs. One sits just outside the tight cluster." Show the lesson's two-panel figure after the run. 
    from sklearn.datasets import make_blobs
from sklearn.ensemble import IsolationForest
from sklearn.neighbors import LocalOutlierFactor
X_toy, _ = make_blobs(n_samples=[300, 200], centers=[[0, 0], [6, 6]], cluster_std=[0.4, 1.6], random_state=42)
planted = np.array([[1.8, 1.8], [-1.6, 1.4], [10, -2], [-3, 9], [12, 12]])
X_toy = np.vstack([X_toy, planted])
y_toy = np.r_[np.zeros(500, dtype=int), np.ones(5, dtype=int)]
iso_toy = IsolationForest(n_estimators=200, random_state=42).fit(X_toy)
lof_toy = LocalOutlierFactor(n_neighbors=20).fit(X_toy)
scores = pd.DataFrame({"isolation_forest": -iso_toy.score_samples(X_toy), "lof": -lof_toy.negative_outlier_factor_})
ranks = scores.rank(ascending=False).astype(int)
print(pd.concat([pd.DataFrame(planted, columns=["x", "y"]), ranks.tail(5).reset_index(drop=True)], axis=1))
          x     y  isolation_forest  lof
0   1.8   1.8                 8    1
1  -1.6   1.4                 4    4
2  10.0  -2.0                 2    3
3  -3.0   9.0                 3    2
4  12.0  12.0                 1    5
    They disagree on (1.8, 1.8): globally it is near things (IF ranks it 8th), locally it is much sparser than its packed neighbors (LOF ranks it 1st). Draw the LOF diagram from the lesson.
  5. contamination only moves the line (3m). "Same forest, three contamination values. Predict: do the scores change?" Then write the convention table: score_samples (higher = normal), decision_function (negative = outlier), predict (-1 / 1). 
    for contamination in ["auto", 0.01, 0.05]:
    model = IsolationForest(n_estimators=200, contamination=contamination, random_state=42).fit(X_toy)
    flagged = (model.predict(X_toy) == -1).sum()
    same_scores = np.allclose(model.score_samples(X_toy), iso_toy.score_samples(X_toy))
    print(f"contamination={str(contamination):5s} offset {model.offset_:.3f}  flagged {flagged:3d}  same scores: {same_scores}")
    contamination=auto  offset -0.500  flagged 103  same scores: True
contamination=0.01  offset -0.645  flagged   6  same scores: True
contamination=0.05  offset -0.591  flagged  26  same scores: True
    Never tune contamination to make a detector better: it cannot reorder anything. Choose the threshold from the review budget.
  6. Mistake: reading predict as 0 and 1 (2m). "Code written for classifiers counts the wrong group, silently." 
    toy_pred = iso_toy.predict(X_toy)
print("labels returned:", np.unique(toy_pred))
print("wrong 'outliers found':", (toy_pred == 1).sum())
print("real outliers flagged :", (toy_pred == -1).sum())
    labels returned: [-1  1]
wrong 'outliers found': 402
real outliers flagged : 103
  7. Mistake: the sign (2m). "score_samples is a normality score. Fed into a metric that wants 'higher = more anomalous'..." 
    from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score
print("AUC of score_samples         :", round(roc_auc_score(y_toy, iso_toy.score_samples(X_toy)), 3))
print("AUC of -score_samples        :", round(roc_auc_score(y_toy, -iso_toy.score_samples(X_toy)), 3))
    AUC of score_samples         : 0.001
AUC of -score_samples        : 0.999
    An AUC far below 0.5 is almost always a flipped sign. Negate once, name it anomaly_score.
  8. Mistake: default LOF on new data (1m). "novelty=False describes the data it saw." 
    lof_toy.predict(X_toy[:3])
    AttributeError: This 'LocalOutlierFactor' has no attribute 'predict'
    Fix: LocalOutlierFactor(novelty=True) for fit-on-one-month, score-the-next.
  9. Mistake: accuracy (1m). "Last one, and it closes the loop with the first step." 
    all_normal_accuracy = 1 - tx["is_fraud"].mean()
print(f"accuracy of flagging nothing at all: {all_normal_accuracy:.4f}")
    accuracy of flagging nothing at all: 0.9912
    Judge with ROC AUC, average precision and catches within the budget, next to a rule baseline. Draw the review loop from the lesson: reviewed cases become labels.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • A flipped sign. Symptom: an AUC of 0.153 in TODO 2 of the lab, or a queue that catches nothing. Coach: "Which way does score_samples point?"
  • Counting predict == 1 as outliers. Symptom: "the detector flags 98% of payments". Coach: "What does an inlier get?"
  • Tuning contamination to catch more. Symptom: a learner raising contamination and expecting a better AUC. Coach: "Did the scores change?" Then: "How many can the team review?"
Guided lab
  • Lab A (15m, everyone): labs/module-05/l8_6-anomaly-detection/monthly_queue_starter.py; reference labs/module-05/l8_6-anomaly-detection/monthly_queue_solution.py. Fit once on January to March, score April, May and June month by month.
  • Expected, Lab A: April AUC 0.153 for score_samples and 0.847 negated; caught 33 of 72 (April), 35 of 65 (May), 33 of 64 (June) in 2% queues, against about 1.3 for a random queue; a fixed contamination=0.02 threshold flags 1.88% to 2.16% in the fit months and 1.72% in June.
  • Debrief Lab A (2m): "The ranking held up for three months; the fixed threshold did not. That is why the team reviews the top N."
  • Lab B (10m, pairs): labs/module-05/l8_6-anomaly-detection/unusual_customers_starter.py; reference labs/module-05/l8_6-anomaly-detection/unusual_customers_solution.py. The 60 most unusual customers by Isolation Forest and by LOF.
  • Expected, Lab B: rank correlation 0.515; only 9 customers in both top-60 lists; the forest's top 60 are heavy buyers (9.67 orders, $1,810 spend, 4.78 returns) who churn at 0.067; LOF's top 60 are closer to average but churn at 0.333 (overall 0.165). Debrief: unusual is not bad, and two detectors find two kinds of unusual.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-05/l8_6-anomaly-detection/debug_anomaly.py (a random split for time data, pred == 1, the sign, a default LOF).
Start the lesson project

A Fraud Review Queue (lesson page, about 55 minutes alone). In the room: Steps 1 and 2.

  • Step 1: the time split and the drift table. Done = train 41,738 payments, test (June) 8,262 payments with 64 frauds; budget 165.
  • Step 2: review_report and two rules. Done = amount rule AUC 0.726, caught 8 of 64; velocity rule AUC 0.830, caught 26.
  • Goes home: Steps 3 to 6: Isolation Forest 32, LOF 16, mixture 24, supervised boosting 41; review budgets with $10 per review and $375 per missed fraud; the false-alarm check for new accounts.
Checks for understanding

Ask out loud:

  • Why a time split for payments? Answer: you deploy on the future; fraud patterns drift (1.0% in January to 0.77% in June).
  • What does contamination change? Answer: only the threshold (offset_); never the scores or the ranking.
  • Why does LOF catch the point next to the tight cluster? Answer: it compares each point's density with its neighbors'.
  • Which number does the payments manager care about? Answer: frauds caught within the review budget, against a rule baseline.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6:

Answers: Q1 B) (array([-1, 1]), array([ 1, 49])); Q2 D) score_samples is a normality score (higher = more normal); negate it and the AUC is 0.88; Q3 0.01 2 / 0.05 10; Q4 B) June's payments are distributed differently from the fit months, so a fixed threshold drifts: rank each month and review the top N the team can handle; Q5 C) An anomaly score finds the unusual, not the bad: these are heavy buyers; whether unusual means a problem is a separate question; Q6 C) LocalOutlierFactor(novelty=True) fitted on May, then -score_samples(june). Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Support: a printed card with the convention table; in Lab A give the loop skeleton of TODO 3 on the projector.

Stretch: debug_anomaly.py; the lesson's Exercise 1 (a contextual amount rule) and the three-tier Bonus Challenge.

9.1

Principal Component Analysis

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain what a principal component is and why scaling matters
  • Choose the number of components from explained variance
  • Interpret loadings to name what each component captures
  • Test whether PCA before a model helps, inside a pipeline
  • Use reconstruction for compression, denoising and anomaly scores
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the flashlight and the shadow; unsupervised, a mix of all columns, linear
25mLive demo: toy components, eigenvectors, unscaled vs scaled, scree, loadings, the map, PCA before a model, reconstruction
25mGuided lab: payment_pca_starter.py, then daily_sales_pca_starter.py in pairs
15mStart the lesson project: Seasonal Fingerprints, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

"Hold a hiking boot under a flashlight. From the side its shadow is clearly a boot; from straight above the toe it is a blob. Both are flat pictures of the same object, but one keeps far more of its shape. PCA chooses the angle for you: the one where the shadow is most spread out, because spread is where the differences between rows live."

Three facts on the board: unsupervised (it never sees churn), each component is a mix of all columns (not a selection), linear (straight directions; curves are Lesson 9.3).

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_9_1.

  1. The direction of most spread (3m). "Two columns that move together. PCA turns the cloud until its shadow is widest." Show the arrow figure. 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
a = rng.normal(0, 1, 300)
toy = pd.DataFrame({"feature_a": a, "feature_b": 0.6 * a + rng.normal(0, 0.35, 300)})
toy_pca = PCA(n_components=2).fit(toy)
print("components (one row per component):")
print(toy_pca.components_.round(3))
print("share of variance:", toy_pca.explained_variance_ratio_.round(3))
    components (one row per component):
[[ 0.826  0.564]
 [-0.564  0.826]]
share of variance: [0.932 0.068]
    Each row of components_ is a unit-length direction; the first explains 93% of the variance.
  2. It is an eigenvector (2m). "For the curious: the top eigenvector of the covariance matrix." 
    cov = np.cov(toy.to_numpy(), rowvar=False)
eigenvalues, eigenvectors = np.linalg.eigh(cov)
print("eigenvalues, largest first:", eigenvalues[::-1].round(3))
print("top eigenvector:", eigenvectors[:, -1].round(3))
    eigenvalues, largest first: [1.227 0.089]
top eigenvector: [-0.826 -0.564]
    Same numbers, opposite sign: an arrow and its opposite describe the same line. Signs are arbitrary.
  3. Unscaled churn columns (3m). "The ten churn columns, the usual split. Predict: what does PC1 look like?" 
    from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
pd.set_option("display.width", 150)
customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user"]
X = customers[features]
y = customers["churned"]
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=42)
raw_pca = PCA().fit(X_train)
print("share of variance, first three:", raw_pca.explained_variance_ratio_[:3].round(3))
print(pd.Series(raw_pca.components_[0], index=features).round(3).iloc[[1, 2, 3, 4]])
    share of variance, first three: [0.916 0.052 0.027]
orders_12m               0.004
spend_12m                0.991
avg_order_value          0.125
days_since_last_order   -0.052
dtype: float64
    PC1 IS spend_12m (weight 0.991): dollars dwarf fractions. PCA on unscaled data tells you which column has the largest units.
  4. Scale first (2m). "Every column starts with variance 1." 
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
scaler = StandardScaler().set_output(transform="pandas")
X_train_s = scaler.fit_transform(X_train)
X_test_s = scaler.transform(X_test)
pca = PCA().fit(X_train_s)
print("share of variance:", pca.explained_variance_ratio_.round(3))
print("cumulative:       ", pca.explained_variance_ratio_.cumsum().round(3))
    share of variance: [0.253 0.175 0.131 0.099 0.09  0.085 0.072 0.061 0.029 0.006]
cumulative:        [0.253 0.427 0.558 0.657 0.748 0.833 0.905 0.966 0.994 1.   ]
    No elbow: the variance is spread over many components. Honest: these columns are only partly redundant.
  5. A variance target (1m). "A fraction as n_components keeps enough components to reach it." 
    pca90 = PCA(n_components=0.90).fit(X_train_s)
print("components kept:", pca90.n_components_)
print("variance kept:  ", pca90.explained_variance_ratio_.sum().round(3))
    components kept: 7
variance kept:   0.905
  6. Reading the loadings (3m). "Large weights, either sign." 
    loadings = pd.DataFrame(pca.components_[:3].T, index=features, columns=["PC1", "PC2", "PC3"])
print(loadings.round(2))
                            PC1   PC2   PC3
tenure_months          0.08 -0.01 -0.24
orders_12m             0.45 -0.17 -0.41
spend_12m              0.46  0.41 -0.22
avg_order_value       -0.01  0.65  0.03
days_since_last_order -0.31  0.18  0.35
returns_12m            0.47  0.04  0.42
return_rate            0.40  0.07  0.56
support_tickets_12m    0.27 -0.23  0.13
discount_share         0.02 -0.52  0.22
app_user               0.18 -0.12 -0.21
    PC1 how active, PC2 big tickets versus deals, PC3 returns relative to orders. Names are your interpretation.
  7. A two-dimensional map (2m). "43% of the variance in one picture. Where are the churners?" Show the lesson's scatter. 
    pca2 = PCA(n_components=2).fit(X_train_s)
map_train = pd.DataFrame(pca2.transform(X_train_s), columns=["PC1", "PC2"], index=X_train_s.index)
map_train["churned"] = y_train
print(map_train.groupby("churned")[["PC1", "PC2"]].mean().round(2))
              PC1   PC2
churned            
0        0.14  0.06
1       -0.72 -0.28
    A lean, heavy overlap: the biggest sources of variation are not the churn signal.
  8. PCA before a model: test it (4m). "Scaler, PCA and logistic regression in one pipeline, so each fold fits its own. Compare with no PCA." 
    from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_score, StratifiedKFold
cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
for k in [2, 4, 6, 8]:
    pipe = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), PCA(n_components=k), LogisticRegression())
    auc = cross_val_score(pipe, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
    print(f"PCA {k:2d} components   AUC {auc.mean():.3f}")
auc = cross_val_score(make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression()), X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
print(f"no PCA (10 columns)  AUC {auc.mean():.3f}")
    PCA  2 components   AUC 0.687
PCA  4 components   AUC 0.786
PCA  6 components   AUC 0.812
PCA  8 components   AUC 0.829
no PCA (10 columns)  AUC 0.829
    Eight components to match the plain model. PCA ranks directions by spread, and it never saw churn (Lesson 9.2 fixes exactly this).
  9. Running PCA backward (3m). "inverse_transform rebuilds rows from the kept components; the gap is what they could not express." 
    for k in [2, 5, 7, 9, 10]:
    model_k = PCA(n_components=k).fit(X_train_s)
    rebuilt = model_k.inverse_transform(model_k.transform(X_test_s))
    rmse = np.sqrt(((X_test_s.to_numpy() - rebuilt) ** 2).mean())
    print(f"{k:2d} components   reconstruction RMSE {rmse:.3f}")
     2 components   reconstruction RMSE 0.765
 5 components   reconstruction RMSE 0.509
 7 components   reconstruction RMSE 0.313
 9 components   reconstruction RMSE 0.081
10 components   reconstruction RMSE 0.000
    This is compression, denoising and an anomaly score. The lesson's fraud version: AUC 0.819 but 19 caught against the forest's 32. Say why (extreme columns vs broken correlations).
  10. Mistake: more components than columns (1m). "Ten columns, at most ten directions." 
    PCA(n_components=12).fit(X_train_s)
    ValueError: n_components=12 must be between 0 and min(n_samples, n_features)=10 with svd_solver='covariance_eigh'
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Forgetting to scale. Symptom: one column with weight near 1.0 in PC1 (account age in the lab). Coach: "What units is that column in?"
  • Reading signs. Symptom: "PC1 is negative for loyal customers, so loyalty is bad". Coach: multiply the arrow by -1; does anything change?
  • Most variance = most useful. Symptom: expecting PC1 to separate fraud in the lab. Coach: "Which component does?" (PC2.)
Guided lab
  • Lab A (15m, everyone): labs/module-05/l9_1-pca/payment_pca_starter.py; reference labs/module-05/l9_1-pca/payment_pca_solution.py. PCA on the nine payment features.
  • Expected, Lab A: unscaled PC1 explains 0.95 and is account_age_days alone; scaled shares from 0.159 down to 0.064, eight components for 90%; PC1 = basket size (amount and items 0.70 each), PC2 = risk signals (card attempts and velocity 0.52, country mismatch 0.39, new device 0.35); frauds average PC2 3.10 against -0.03; fraud rate 0.084 in the top 2% of PC1 and 0.206 in the top 2% of PC2 (base 0.009).
  • Debrief: "Here a direction of spread DID carry the fraud signal, but the second one, not the first. PCA never knew; you found out by checking."
  • Lab B (10m, pairs, finish at home): labs/module-05/l9_1-pca/daily_sales_pca_starter.py; reference labs/module-05/l9_1-pca/daily_sales_pca_solution.py. Days × four stores, one unit: PCA's home ground.
  • Expected, Lab B: 1,090 open days; shares 0.841, 0.067, 0.051, 0.042; PC1 weights about 0.5 for every store (the chain's day: 1.16 to 1.23 on the three sale events, -0.05 on ordinary days); PC2 Asheville 0.70 vs Flagstaff -0.66, falling from 0.117 in 2023 to -0.130 in 2025 as Flagstaff grows from $4,997 to $6,597 a day; PC1 alone leaves a gap of 0.105 log units.

Checks for understanding

Ask out loud:

  • What does explained_variance_ratio_ tell you? Answer: each component's share of the total variance.
  • When should you NOT standardize before PCA? Answer: when all columns share one meaningful unit (monthly sales, pixels).
  • Why put PCA inside the pipeline for cross-validation? Answer: each fold fits its own scaler and PCA: no leakage.
  • What is reconstruction error? Answer: the gap between a row and its rebuild from the kept components: what they cannot express.

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12:

Answers: Q7 D) [0.732 0.268 0. ]; Q8 B) Account age is measured in days, up to about 7,000, so its variance dwarfs the 0/1 flags and small counts; Q9 (50, 3) (3, 6); Q10 D) 784 highly correlated pixel columns feeding a slow k-nearest-neighbors model; Q11 A) Much of this fraud is extreme on a few columns rather than breaking the correlations most payments share; Q12 7: hardly a reduction. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Answers on sticky notes. Question 3 tells you how much of the Session 6 warm-up goes to scaling and to supervised vs unsupervised projections.

  1. An Isolation Forest's predict returns -1 and 1, and score_samples gives an AUC of 0.2. Write the two lines you would use instead to build a review queue of 165. Answer: anomaly_score = -model.score_samples(X) and queue = np.argsort(-anomaly_score)[:165] (then count frauds in it).
  2. Your manager asks you to raise contamination from 0.01 to 0.05 "so the detector catches more fraud". What do you explain? Answer: contamination only moves the threshold; the ranking (and the AUC) is unchanged. Choose how many to review from the team's budget and the costs.
  3. You run PCA on ten customer columns without scaling and PC1 explains 92%. What happened, and when would skipping the scaler be right? Answer: the column with the largest units (spend) took over. Skip scaling only when all columns share one meaningful unit, like monthly sales or pixels.

Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 6: LDA, t-SNE and UMAP

How to teach the sixth session of Advanced Machine Learning: LDA, the supervised counterpart of PCA (and its leak), then t-SNE and UMAP, the most persuasive and most over-read pictures in machine learning, checked with numbers. The session ends with Checkpoint 1, a scored 30-minute open-notebook task on Modules 7 to 9.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 9.2–9.3 Checkpoint 1 Session 6 of 13

How to Use This Guide

  • Two lessons in shorter blocks (60 minutes each) because the last 30 minutes before the wrap-up are Checkpoint 1, a scored open-notebook task on Modules 7 to 9.
  • Every code block below was run to produce the output under it (Python 3.12, scikit-learn 1.9.1, umap-learn, 4 CPU threads). t-SNE and UMAP outputs can differ in the last digits on another machine: say "yours may vary slightly" once.
  • Keep dimensionality_lda.html and dimensionality_tsne.html open for the figures (PCA vs LDA panels, loyalty-tier maps, the garment maps, the three-blob and noise panels). Labs live in labs/module-06/.
  • Teal blocks are hands-on, amber blocks are watch-outs.
The #1 risk of Session 6: running out of time for the checkpoint

t-SNE and UMAP take seconds per run, and a room of laptops will be slower than yours. Protect the 2:20 start of Checkpoint 1: if the 9.3 block runs over, cut the lab to TODO 1 and 2 and send the rest home. The second risk is technical: Fashion-MNIST not downloaded. Check it in the warm-up (one command, below) and have the FashionMNIST/ folder on a USB stick.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Run the kit check once: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-06 (10 files, about 90 seconds; the Fashion-MNIST lab about 30 s).
  • ☐ Downloads and caches: Fashion-MNIST (about 30 MB download, about 86 MB on disk) must be in each learner's pinecrest-ml/data/FashionMNIST/ (Session 5 bring list). Bring a USB copy of that folder. No pretrained weights today.
  • ☐ Projector machine: run import umap once before the session (it compiles its routines on first import, several seconds). Two notebooks lesson_9_2 and lesson_9_3.
  • ☐ Checkpoint: print or share labs/module-06/checkpoint-1/checkpoint_starter.py and the workbook's Checkpoint 1 page; keep checkpoint_solution.py and the rubric (answer key) to yourself.
  • ☐ Board: the team photo (two clubs, two camera angles), and the five reading rules for maps.

Lesson Plans

9.2

Linear Discriminant Analysis

60 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain how LDA differs from PCA: it uses the labels to find directions that separate classes
  • Use LDA to reduce dimensions for a plot or as a step before another model
  • Use LDA as a classifier and compare it with logistic regression
  • Know the limit on components (classes minus one) and when shrinkage helps
  • Keep LDA inside the pipeline so cross-validation stays honest
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the team photo; Fisher's criterion in words; at most classes minus one axes
20mLive demo: PCA vs LDA, the churn axis, LDA as a classifier, more classes, shrinkage, mistakes, the noise leak
20mGuided lab: payment_category_lda_starter.py, then noise_leak_starter.py in pairs
10mStart the lesson project: The Loyalty-Tier Map, Steps 1 and 2
Mental model to say

"Two hiking clubs pose on a field and you photograph them from the side, so the picture flattens everyone onto one line. PCA picks the angle that makes the crowd look widest, even if the clubs end up mixed. LDA walks around the field until the two clubs appear as two tight, separate groups: each bunched up, the two far apart."

Then the consequence to memorize: the class means of C classes span at most C - 1 directions, so two classes give one axis and three tiers give two, however many features.

Live demo (you type, they follow)
  1. Two methods, one picture (3m). "Two long, thin, parallel classes. Predict: where does PCA point, where does LDA point?" Show the lesson's three-panel figure. 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
from sklearn.discriminant_analysis import LinearDiscriminantAnalysis
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
n = 200
along = rng.normal(0, 3, 2 * n)
across = rng.normal(0, 0.6, 2 * n) + np.repeat([-1.0, 1.0], n)
angle = np.deg2rad(30)
rotation = np.array([[np.cos(angle), -np.sin(angle)], [np.sin(angle), np.cos(angle)]])
toy_X = np.column_stack([along, across]) @ rotation.T
toy_y = np.repeat([0, 1], n)
toy_pca = PCA(n_components=1).fit(toy_X)
toy_lda = LinearDiscriminantAnalysis(n_components=1).fit(toy_X, toy_y)
lda_dir = toy_lda.scalings_[:, 0] / np.linalg.norm(toy_lda.scalings_[:, 0])
print("PCA direction:", toy_pca.components_[0].round(3))
print("LDA direction:", lda_dir.round(3))
    PCA direction: [0.866 0.5  ]
LDA direction: [-0.489  0.872]
    Almost at right angles: PCA follows the long axis (the spread), LDA points across the classes.
  2. What each one keeps (2m). "Distance between the class means, in within-class standard deviations." 
    def separation(z, labels):
    """Distance between the two class means, in units of the average within-class std."""
    a, b = z[labels == 0], z[labels == 1]
    return abs(a.mean() - b.mean()) / np.sqrt((a.var() + b.var()) / 2)
print(f"separation on PC1: {separation(toy_pca.transform(toy_X)[:, 0], toy_y):.2f}")
print(f"separation on LD1: {separation(toy_lda.transform(toy_X)[:, 0], toy_y):.2f}")
    separation on PC1: 0.05
separation on LD1: 3.32
  3. One axis for churn (4m). "Two classes, at most one axis. Its weights read like a churn story." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user"]
X = customers[features]
y = customers["churned"]
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=42)
scaler = StandardScaler().set_output(transform="pandas")
X_train_s = scaler.fit_transform(X_train)
X_test_s = scaler.transform(X_test)
lda = LinearDiscriminantAnalysis()
lda.fit(X_train_s, y_train)
churn_axis = lda.transform(X_test_s)
print("transformed shape:", churn_axis.shape)
print(pd.Series(lda.scalings_[:, 0], index=features).round(2).sort_values().iloc[[0, 1, -3, -2, -1]])
    transformed shape: (1500, 1)
app_user                -0.21
tenure_months           -0.09
support_tickets_12m      0.30
discount_share           0.50
days_since_last_order    0.92
dtype: float64
    Days since the last order dominates, then discounts and tickets; app users on the other side. Lesson 9.1's PCA buried recency; LDA puts it first because the labels told it to.
  4. LDA as a classifier (3m). "The same object predicts: Gaussian classes with one shared covariance, so a straight boundary." 
    from sklearn.dummy import DummyClassifier
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.discriminant_analysis import QuadraticDiscriminantAnalysis
from sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score, roc_auc_score
models = {"baseline": DummyClassifier(strategy="prior"), "LDA": LinearDiscriminantAnalysis(),
          "logistic regression": LogisticRegression(), "QDA": QuadraticDiscriminantAnalysis()}
for name, model in models.items():
    model.fit(X_train_s, y_train)
    proba = model.predict_proba(X_test_s)[:, 1]
    acc = accuracy_score(y_test, model.predict(X_test_s))
    print(f"{name:20s} accuracy {acc:.3f}   ROC AUC {roc_auc_score(y_test, proba):.3f}")
    baseline             accuracy 0.835   ROC AUC 0.500
LDA                  accuracy 0.853   ROC AUC 0.824
logistic regression  accuracy 0.848   ROC AUC 0.823
QDA                  accuracy 0.813   ROC AUC 0.803
    Neck and neck with logistic regression. QDA (one covariance per class) is worse: flexibility is not free.
  5. More classes, more axes (2m). "Four classes in ten dimensions." 
    from sklearn.datasets import make_blobs
blobs_X, blobs_y = make_blobs(n_samples=400, centers=4, n_features=10, cluster_std=3.0, random_state=42)
blob_lda = LinearDiscriminantAnalysis().fit(blobs_X, blobs_y)
print("transform shape:", blob_lda.transform(blobs_X).shape)
print("separation carried by each axis:", blob_lda.explained_variance_ratio_.round(3))
    transform shape: (400, 3)
separation carried by each axis: [0.781 0.136 0.083]
    The ratio is the share of class SEPARATION, not of total variance.
  6. Many features, few rows: shrinkage (2m). "100 rows, 200 features." 
    from sklearn.datasets import make_classification
from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_score, StratifiedKFold
wide_X, wide_y = make_classification(n_samples=100, n_features=200, n_informative=10, n_redundant=0, random_state=42)
cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
for name, model in [("plain LDA (svd)", LinearDiscriminantAnalysis()),
                    ("LDA with shrinkage", LinearDiscriminantAnalysis(solver="lsqr", shrinkage="auto"))]:
    print(f"{name:20s} CV accuracy {cross_val_score(model, wide_X, wide_y, cv=cv).mean():.3f}")
    plain LDA (svd)      CV accuracy 0.540
LDA with shrinkage   CV accuracy 0.680
  7. Two loud mistakes (1m). "Too many axes; shrinkage with the default solver." 
    LinearDiscriminantAnalysis(n_components=2).fit(X_train_s, y_train)
LinearDiscriminantAnalysis(shrinkage="auto").fit(X_train_s, y_train)
    ValueError: n_components cannot be larger than min(n_features, n_classes - 1).
NotImplementedError: shrinkage not supported with 'svd' solver.
  8. The quiet mistake: LDA before cross-validation (3m). "Pure noise, random labels. No honest model beats one in three." 
    from sklearn.neighbors import KNeighborsClassifier
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
noise_X = rng.normal(size=(100, 500))
noise_y = rng.integers(0, 3, size=100)
reduced = LinearDiscriminantAnalysis(n_components=2).fit_transform(noise_X, noise_y)
leaky = cross_val_score(KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=5), reduced, noise_y, cv=cv).mean()
honest_pipe = make_pipeline(LinearDiscriminantAnalysis(n_components=2), KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=5))
honest = cross_val_score(honest_pipe, noise_X, noise_y, cv=cv).mean()
print(f"LDA fitted on all rows, then CV: {leaky:.2f}")
print(f"LDA inside the pipeline:         {honest:.2f}")
    LDA fitted on all rows, then CV: 0.62
LDA inside the pipeline:         0.34
    Write both numbers on the board. Every supervised step (LDA, target encoding, selection that looks at y) belongs inside the pipeline. The lab repeats this on real customers.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Asking for too many axes. n_components=2 for churn. Coach: "How many classes? Minus one."
  • Reading LDA's explained_variance_ratio_ as PCA's. Coach: "Share of what? Of the separation between classes."
  • Reducing before cross-validating. Coach with the board: 0.62 vs 0.34, and in the lab 0.998 vs 0.481.
Guided lab
  • Lab A (12m, everyone): labs/module-06/l9_2-lda/payment_category_lda_starter.py; reference labs/module-06/l9_2-lda/payment_category_lda_solution.py. Ten product categories from nine payment columns.
  • Expected, Lab A: 9 axes; LD1 carries 0.995 of the separation and is amount (1.42) against items (-0.59): price per item; median LD1 from Accessories (-0.93, $65) to Tents (1.41, $350); accuracy 0.290 against a 0.219 baseline; six categories never predicted, Apparel recall 0.86.
  • Debrief: "A weak result, honestly reported, and LDA told you WHY in one axis: categories differ mainly by price per item."
  • Lab B (8m, pairs): labs/module-06/l9_2-lda/noise_leak_starter.py; reference labs/module-06/l9_2-lda/noise_leak_solution.py. 400 customers, 10 real + 290 noise columns.
  • Expected, Lab B: shuffled labels: leaky AUC 0.998, honest 0.481; true labels: leaky 1.000, honest 0.541; the 10 real columns alone: 0.776. Second lesson: noise columns drown real signal for a reducer that has to estimate a covariance.
Start the lesson project

The Loyalty-Tier Map (about 45 minutes alone). In the room: Steps 1 and 2.

  • Step 1: stratify on loyalty_tier; done = Basic 2129, Silver 2015, Gold 356.
  • Step 2: baseline 0.473, LDA 0.909, logistic regression 0.921.
  • Goes home: Steps 3 to 6: LD1 carries 0.948 and is spend plus tenure; PCA vs LDA maps; errors only between neighboring tiers; tier_map.csv.
Checks for understanding

Ask out loud:

  • How many axes for three tiers? Answer: two.
  • What do priors change? Answer: the threshold, not the axis or the ranking.
  • When do you need shrinkage? Answer: many features, few rows; eigen solver if you also need transform.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6:

Answers: Q1 B) (200, 2); Q2 A) Class means far apart relative to the spread inside each class; PCA chooses the largest total spread and never sees the labels; Q3 [0.5 0.5]; Q4 C) The payment details separate categories along essentially one direction, price per item, and only weakly (accuracy 0.290 against 0.219); Q5 D) LDA used every row's label, including the test folds', to build its axis; with 300 columns it can separate any labels; Q6 B) LinearDiscriminantAnalysis(solver="eigen", shrinkage="auto"). Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: the team-photo drawing with both camera angles. Stretch: Lab B; the lesson's Exercise 1 (kNN on two LDA axes, 0.916 vs 0.864).

9.3

t-SNE and UMAP

60 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain what t-SNE and UMAP preserve and what they distort
  • Tune perplexity (t-SNE) and n_neighbors and min_dist (UMAP), and see how the picture changes
  • Read an embedding without over-interpreting cluster sizes or distances
  • Check a map with numbers (neighbors kept, trustworthiness) instead of by eye
  • Choose between PCA, t-SNE and UMAP for a task, and place new rows with UMAP
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the seating chart; what is kept, what is lost
20mLive demo: the garment map, perplexity, distortion, noise, neighbor checks, UMAP, transform
20mGuided lab: fashion_map_starter.py (start TODO 1, talk while it runs), then fraud_map_starter.py
10mStart the lesson project: The Customer Map, Steps 1 and 2
Mental model to say

"You seat 1,000 wedding guests in one hall; the only rule is: sit near the people you know best. The tables of friends mean something. How far one table is from another, and how big a table is, mean nothing: the only goal was friends together." Then the five reading rules on the board: neighbors meaningful; cluster sizes not; distances between clusters not; axes meaningless; validate clusters in the original data.

Live demo (you type, they follow)
  1. 1,000 garments (2m). "Each image is a row of 784 pixels. The labels only color the picture." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from torchvision import datasets
fashion = datasets.FashionMNIST("data", train=False, download=True)
images = fashion.data.numpy().reshape(len(fashion), -1) / 255
labels = fashion.targets.numpy()
class_names = fashion.classes
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
pick = rng.choice(len(images), size=1000, replace=False)
img, lab = images[pick], labels[pick]
print(img.shape, img.min(), img.max())
print(np.bincount(lab))
    (1000, 784) 0.0 1.0
[102  90  88 101  99 103 106 100 102 109]
  2. PCA to 50, then t-SNE (3m). "The standard recipe: PCA first removes pixel noise and is much faster." Show the lesson's PCA vs t-SNE figure. 
    from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
from sklearn.manifold import TSNE
pca50 = PCA(n_components=50, random_state=42).fit(img)
img50 = pca50.transform(img)
pca2 = img50[:, :2]
tsne = TSNE(n_components=2, perplexity=30, random_state=42)
emb_tsne = tsne.fit_transform(img50)
print(emb_tsne.shape, "KL divergence:", round(tsne.kl_divergence_, 3))
    (1000, 2) KL divergence: 0.676
    Footwear, trousers and bags get islands; shirts, pullovers and coats stay mixed because they really look alike. Output label: yours may vary slightly (another machine's math libraries).
  3. Perplexity is a lens (3m). "Roughly how many neighbors each point listens to." 
    small50, small_lab = img50[:500], lab[:500]
for perp in [5, 30, 100]:
    emb = TSNE(perplexity=perp, random_state=42).fit_transform(small50)
    print(f"perplexity {perp:3d}: map spans {np.ptp(emb[:, 0]):.0f} by {np.ptp(emb[:, 1]):.0f} units")
    perplexity   5: map spans 132 by 115 units
perplexity  30: map spans 47 by 57 units
perplexity 100: map spans 19 by 19 units
    The scale of the map changes with its settings: axis numbers have no units. Look at more than one perplexity.
  4. What a map distorts (3m). "Three groups whose truth we know: tight, wide, far away." 
    from sklearn.datasets import make_blobs
blob_X, blob_y = make_blobs(n_samples=[100, 100, 100], centers=[[0, 0], [6, 0], [30, 0]],
                            cluster_std=[0.3, 2.0, 1.0], random_state=42)
blob_emb = TSNE(perplexity=30, random_state=42).fit_transform(blob_X)
def spread(Z, g):
    return Z[blob_y == g].std(axis=0).mean()
def gap(Z, g1, g2):
    return np.linalg.norm(Z[blob_y == g1].mean(axis=0) - Z[blob_y == g2].mean(axis=0))
print("spread original:", "  ".join(f"{spread(blob_X, g):.2f}" for g in range(3)),
      "   map:", "  ".join(f"{spread(blob_emb, g):.2f}" for g in range(3)))
print(f"gap A-B vs B-C original: {gap(blob_X, 0, 1):.1f} vs {gap(blob_X, 1, 2):.1f}"
      f"   map: {gap(blob_emb, 0, 1):.1f} vs {gap(blob_emb, 1, 2):.1f}")
    spread original: 0.28  1.96  0.99    map: 3.04  4.48  3.08
gap A-B vs B-C original: 6.3 vs 23.7   map: 31.9 vs 41.1
    Sizes nearly equalized, gaps compressed. Then the pure-noise map (next step) invents clumps. Put the red reading-rules card on the board.
  5. Clumps from nothing (1m). "Random noise, perplexity 5." Show the lesson's right-hand panel. 
    noise = rng.normal(size=(200, 10))
noise_emb = TSNE(perplexity=5, random_state=42).fit_transform(noise)
print("rows of pure noise mapped:", len(noise_emb))
    rows of pure noise mapped: 200
  6. Check a map with numbers (3m). "Of each image's 10 true neighbors, how many are map neighbors?" 
    from sklearn.neighbors import NearestNeighbors
from sklearn.manifold import trustworthiness
def neighbors_kept(original, embedded, k=10):
    """Average share of each point's k nearest original neighbors that are also map neighbors."""
    nn_orig = NearestNeighbors(n_neighbors=k + 1).fit(original).kneighbors(original, return_distance=False)[:, 1:]
    nn_emb = NearestNeighbors(n_neighbors=k + 1).fit(embedded).kneighbors(embedded, return_distance=False)[:, 1:]
    return np.mean([len(set(a) & set(b)) / k for a, b in zip(nn_orig, nn_emb)])
for name, emb in [("PCA (2 components)", pca2), ("t-SNE", emb_tsne)]:
    print(f"{name:20s} neighbors kept {neighbors_kept(img50, emb):.3f}"
          f"   trustworthiness {trustworthiness(img50, emb, n_neighbors=10):.3f}")
    PCA (2 components)   neighbors kept 0.235   trustworthiness 0.923
t-SNE                neighbors kept 0.599   trustworthiness 0.988
  7. UMAP (2m). "Same spirit, a graph laid out flat, faster, and it can place new rows." The import compiles on first use. 
    import warnings
import umap
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="n_jobs value")
reducer = umap.UMAP(n_neighbors=15, min_dist=0.1, random_state=42)
emb_umap = reducer.fit_transform(img50)
print(f"neighbors kept {neighbors_kept(img50, emb_umap):.3f}"
      f"   trustworthiness {trustworthiness(img50, emb_umap, n_neighbors=10):.3f}")
    neighbors kept 0.519   trustworthiness 0.981
  8. Placing new rows (2m). "Through the same fitted PCA, then the fitted UMAP." 
    from sklearn.neighbors import KNeighborsClassifier
rest = np.setdiff1d(np.arange(len(images)), pick)
new_pick = rng.choice(rest, size=500, replace=False)
new_umap = reducer.transform(pca50.transform(images[new_pick]))
knn_on_map = KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=10).fit(emb_umap, lab)
print("new rows placed:", new_umap.shape)
print(f"accuracy on 500 new images: {knn_on_map.score(new_umap, labels[new_pick]):.3f}")
    new rows placed: (500, 2)
accuracy on 500 new images: 0.690
    In real work: make_pipeline(PCA(50), umap.UMAP(...)) so the order cannot be forgotten.
  9. Two loud mistakes (1m). "t-SNE has no transform; perplexity must be below the number of rows." 
    tsne.transform(img50[:5])
TSNE(perplexity=30, random_state=42).fit_transform(img50[:20])
    AttributeError: 'TSNE' object has no attribute 'transform'
ValueError: perplexity (30) must be less than n_samples (20)
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Reading sizes and distances. Coach: "Which of the five rules does that sentence break?"
  • Clustering on the map. Coach: "Cluster in the original space; the map only illustrates."
  • Unscaled business columns. Coach with Lesson 9.3's Mistake 4: neighbors kept 0.201 raw vs 0.739 scaled.
Guided lab
  • Lab A (12m, everyone): labs/module-06/l9_3-tsne-umap/fashion_map_starter.py; reference labs/module-06/l9_3-tsne-umap/fashion_map_solution.py. 1,500 TRAINING images; t-SNE and UMAP; neighbor checks; 500 new images placed. TODO 1 runs about 20 seconds: start it, then explain TODO 2.
  • Expected, Lab A: 50 components keep 0.869 of the pixel variance; neighbors kept 0.195 (PCA view), 0.573 (t-SNE), 0.495 (UMAP); trustworthiness 0.922 / 0.991 / 0.986; hardest classes Shirt 0.32, Coat 0.51, Pullover 0.62, easiest Trouser 0.90; 0.730 of the new images land among their own class. Yours may vary slightly.
  • Lab B (8m, pairs): labs/module-06/l9_3-tsne-umap/fraud_map_starter.py; reference labs/module-06/l9_3-tsne-umap/fraud_map_solution.py. Is fraud its own island?
  • Expected, Lab B: 0.614 of a fraud's neighbors are frauds in the original space; 0.630 and 0.631 on the maps (perplexity 5 and 30, neighbors kept 0.633 and 0.696); K-Means in the original space: one cluster of 239 frauds and 20 honest payments, the other 199 frauds among 1,042 honest ones. The colleague is partly right, and the evidence is the original space.
Start the lesson project

The Customer Map (about 45 minutes alone). In the room: Steps 1 and 2.

  • Step 1: four behavior columns, two logged, scaled: (6000, 4).
  • Step 2: K-Means in the 4-D space: silhouette 0.479; profile and name the five segments from the table, never from a map.
  • Goes home: Steps 3 to 6: t-SNE and UMAP maps of 2,000; silhouette 0.477 in 4-D vs 0.572 / 0.596 on the maps (maps flatter); 98.5% of 4,000 new customers land in their region; customer_map.csv.
Checks for understanding

Ask out loud:

  • What does a t-SNE map keep? Answer: neighborhoods.
  • Which method can place new rows? Answer: UMAP (and PCA), not t-SNE.
  • How do you check a map? Answer: neighbors kept, trustworthiness, and clusters validated in the original space.

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12:

Answers: Q7 C) Little about size or distance: check spread and separation in the original data; Q8 A) The map reproduces a neighborhood structure that exists in the data; now confirm any group in the original space (a clustering found 239 frauds together, 199 elsewhere); Q9 ValueError: perplexity (30.0) must be less than n_samples (12); Q10 D) UMAP: fit once, then transform new rows; it can sit in a pipeline; Q11 B) Speed, and the last few hundred components of image data are mostly noise; Q12 D) Try several perplexities and confirm with a clustering and its profiles in the original space. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Checkpoint 1: Modules 7 to 9

What it checks
  • Module 7: permutation importance on held-out rows, and the sentence that importance describes the model, not causes.
  • Module 8: a Gaussian mixture on behavior only, profiles with churn, named by profile, borderline customers counted.
  • Module 9: a variance count with PCA, a supervised axis with LDA fitted on training rows, the two compared with one number each.

  • If the room is slower than planned, drop Part D to one sentence and say so; score row 5 on that sentence.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 3 minutes)

The checkpoint ran long on attention; keep this brief.

  1. LDA on churn gives one axis, PCA gives ten components. Why the difference? Answer: LDA's axes are at most classes minus one (two classes, one axis); PCA has one component per column.
  2. Name two things you must NOT read off a t-SNE map. Answer: cluster sizes, distances between clusters (also: the axes).
  3. You need tomorrow's customers on today's map. Which method, and how? Answer: UMAP: fit once, transform the new rows through the same fitted steps (scaler or PCA first).
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish both projects: The Loyalty-Tier Map Steps 3 to 6 (tier_map.csv) and The Customer Map Steps 3 to 6 (customer_map.csv).
  • Lesson exercises: 9.2 Exercises 1 to 3 (kNN on LDA axes, priors, Fisher's ratio); 9.3 Exercises 1 to 3 (seeds, perplexity, min_dist). Exercise 4 of each is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes and both journal entries.
  • Before the Next Lesson (from 9.3): make sure import torch works; keep customer_map.csv for the capstone.
  • Optional: noise_leak_starter.py, fraud_map_starter.py.
Bring list for Session 7
  • A laptop on which import torch works (it did in check_environment.py in Session 1). Session 7 builds a neural network in NumPy, then in PyTorch, on the CPU.
  • The pinecrest-ml folder with data/ and labs/; Session 7's starters are in labs/module-07/.
  • Fashion-MNIST stays in data/: Module 10 uses it again for a CNN.
  • Your checkpoint file if you want to finish Part D at home (the debrief opens Session 7).
  • The workbook and the journal.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: the checkpoint's 30 minutes; the noise leak (9.2); distortion and noise (9.3).
  • Cut first (saves 4m): 9.2 Steps 5 and 6.
  • Cut second (saves 3m): 9.3 Step 3.
  • Cut third (saves 20m): both project starts: give the check numbers.
Running short: what to add
  • Debrief the checkpoint immediately with the model answer (otherwise it opens Session 7).
  • Lesson 9.3 Exercise 4 live: UMAP features inside a pipeline do not beat PCA here (0.725 vs 0.735).
Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 7: Neural Networks and PyTorch

How to teach Session 7 of Advanced Machine Learning. Build a neural network by hand in NumPy (forward pass, loss, backpropagation checked numerically, the four-line training loop), let PyTorch's autograd prove it right, then rebuild everything the professional way: tensors, nn.Module, a loss and an optimizer, a DataLoader, early stopping and a saved state_dict. Both projects end with the same honest finding: on Pinecrest's churn table a network beats logistic regression and, at best, ties gradient boosting.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 10.1–10.2 Session 7 of 13

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm for both lessons: frame (the goal and one mental model) → live demo (you type, they type along) → guided lab (a kit lab, new practice on the lesson's skills) → start the lesson's own project. Exercises, quiz and journal are homework.
  • Type, do not paste. Every code block in the demo scripts was run to produce the output printed under it (Python 3.12, scikit-learn 1.9.1, PyTorch 2.14.1 on 4 CPU threads). Ask for a prediction before every run that prints a number, and read errors from the last line up, out loud.
  • Outputs marked "yours may vary slightly" are PyTorch results. On one machine with a fixed seed and thread count they repeat exactly; another processor or PyTorch build can change the last digits. Say this once at the first such output, then compare trends, not third decimals.
  • The lessons are the reference. Keep deep_learning_basics.html and deep_learning_pytorch.html open for the pictures (the panel diagram, the loss curve and decision boundary, the two-curve chart). Labs live in labs/module-07/: _starter.py files are handouts, _solution.py files are yours.
  • Teal blocks are hands-on, amber blocks are watch-outs. Lesson codes (10.1, 10.2) match the course site.
The #1 risk of Session 7: the math swallows the session

Lesson 10.1 contains a forward pass, a loss, gradient descent, backpropagation, a numeric gradient check and autograd. Each is one demo step of two or three minutes, not a lecture. Derive nothing on the board beyond the shape table and p - y. The learners who want the calculus have it on the lesson page; the ones who do not need to leave knowing the four-line loop, the coin-flip loss 0.693, and that autograd computes the same gradients they could check by nudging. The second risk is the opposite: PyTorch syntax without the honest comparison. Both projects end with the same verdict (a network beats logistic regression on churn and ties gradient boosting at best). Protect the moment the room sees 0.846 against 0.850: it is the course's habit applied to a shiny new tool.

Known environment traps

ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'torch' in the notebook onlyThe notebook kernel is not the course .venvPick the .venv kernel (VS Code: Select Kernel), or start JupyterLab from the activated terminal.
