Warm-up

0:00

Warm-up: Transfer Learning and Text

10 min
How to run it
  • Five questions on the slide, two minutes alone, answers by show of hands. Homework check: who has landcover_resnet18.pt (12.1 will talk about saving files like it) and review_follow_up.csv.
Questions and answers
W1
What does this print?
from torch import nn
from torchvision import models
m = models.resnet18(weights=None)
for p in m.parameters():
    p.requires_grad = False
m.fc = nn.Linear(512, 5)
print(sum(p.numel() for p in m.parameters() if p.requires_grad))
2565
Why: Frozen first, then a new 5-class head: 512 × 5 + 5 = 2,565. New layers are trainable by default.
W2
What does this print?
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import CountVectorizer
print(CountVectorizer().build_analyzer()("Not bad, NOT bad at all!"))
['not', 'bad', 'not', 'bad', 'at', 'all']
Why: Lowercased, punctuation dropped, every occurrence kept in order by the analyzer. Counted as a bag: not 2, bad 2, at 1, all 1. A unigram model cannot tell Not bad from bad.
W3
Why must the backbone be in eval() mode while you extract features?
Batch normalization must use its stored ImageNet statistics; in training mode a tile's features depend on its batch and the stored statistics drift.
Why: no_grad does not do this: it only switches off gradient tracking.
W4
Someone fits a TfidfVectorizer on all reviews, then splits. What is wrong, and how do you fix it?
Test reviews shape the vocabulary and the IDF values: a leak. Split first, fit inside a pipeline.
Why: Today's recommender has the same rule in a new costume: the matrix is built from training purchases only.
W5
Frozen features: 0.885 with 500 labels, 0.902 with 2,000. What is the better next step than labeling more?
Fine-tune the top block (layer4) with a small learning rate: validation 0.876 → 0.924 in the project.
Why: Measuring what more data buys, before paying for it, is the habit.

Lesson Plans

11.2

Recommendation Systems

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Build a sparse customer-by-product matrix from purchase records with SciPy
  • Evaluate a recommender with a leave-last-out split and hit rate at 10, against random and best-seller baselines
  • Implement item-based collaborative filtering with cosine similarity, and matrix factorization with TruncatedSVD
  • Use product attributes for content-based recommendations, and explain how they rescue a brand-new product
  • Judge a recommender on more than accuracy: catalog coverage and popularity bias
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the clerk who remembers; the grid; leave the last one out
30mLive demo: grid, long tail, split, helpers, baselines, item-kNN, one customer, SVD, k, cold start, coverage
20mGuided lab: segment_baselines_starter.py, then user_based_starter.py
15mStart the project: Picked for You, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

"A long-serving clerk at the Bend store has watched thousands of customers. When a regular buys a climbing harness she does not reach for the best-selling socks: 'People who buy that harness usually come back for a chalk bag.' No rule book; she noticed which products end up with the same customers. That is collaborative filtering. A new clerk can only point at the best sellers (popularity) or read the labels (content-based). Today we build all three and measure which helps customers most."

Then the shape of the problem: no target column, a big mostly empty grid of customers × products, and the job is to RANK the empty cells. "The tools you know: cosine similarity from kNN, a cousin of PCA, and holding out data you never train on."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

Notebook lesson_11_2. Everything runs in about a second per cell. Ask for predictions before Steps 7, 9 and 13.

  1. Two files (2m). "One row per customer and product bought; a product table. No target column this time." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
interactions = pd.read_csv("data/interactions.csv")
products = pd.read_csv("data/products_ml.csv")
print(interactions.shape, products.shape)
print(interactions.head(3))
    (32178, 5) (200, 8)
  customer_id product_id  purchases  rating last_purchase_date
0     C100001   APL-3021          2     5.0         2024-03-20
1     C100001   KIT-3003          2     5.0         2024-08-27
2     C100001   KIT-3013          1     NaN         2024-09-23
    Implicit feedback (purchases) vs explicit (ratings, 38% of rows). We use purchases.
  2. How empty is the grid? (2m). "A typical customer has bought 9 of 200 products." 
    n_customers = interactions["customer_id"].nunique()
n_products = interactions["product_id"].nunique()
print(f"{n_customers:,} customers x {n_products} products: {len(interactions) / (n_customers * n_products):.1%} filled")
print(interactions.groupby("customer_id").size().describe()[["mean", "50%", "min", "max"]].to_dict())
    3,000 customers x 200 products: 5.4% filled
{'mean': 10.726, '50%': 9.0, 'min': 3.0, 'max': 45.0}
    An empty cell is not a dislike: they may have bought a tent elsewhere. That is why we RANK empty cells instead of predicting yes or no.
  3. The long tail (2m). "Two sunscreens reach a third of all customers each; the typical product sells to about 120." 
    buyers = interactions.groupby("product_id").size().sort_values(ascending=False)
names = products.set_index("product_id")["name"]
print(names[buyers.index[:2]].tolist(), buyers.head(2).tolist())
print(f"median buyers {buyers.median():.0f}; top 20 products = {buyers.head(20).sum() / buyers.sum():.1%} of purchases")
    ['Larkspur Ridge Lookout Sunscreen SPF 50', 'Juniper Vane Tempo Sunscreen SPF 50'] [992, 941]
median buyers 120; top 20 products = 29.9% of purchases
    Show the lesson's long-tail chart. A best-seller list shows its left end to everyone.
  4. Leave the last one out (3m). "Hide each customer's most recent purchase, train on the rest, ask for ten suggestions. Hit = the hidden product is on the list." Draw the lesson's flow diagram. 
    interactions = interactions.sort_values(["customer_id", "last_purchase_date", "product_id"])
test = interactions.groupby("customer_id").tail(1)
train = interactions.drop(test.index)
print(f"{len(train):,} training rows, {len(test):,} test rows (one per customer)")
    29,178 training rows, 3,000 test rows (one per customer)
    Latest, not random: it mimics predicting the next purchase and keeps the future out of training (Lesson 1.2's time-split rule).
  5. A sparse matrix from training rows (2m). "Rows are customers, columns products, a 1 where they bought." 
    from scipy.sparse import csr_matrix
customer_ids = np.sort(interactions["customer_id"].unique())
product_ids = products["product_id"].to_numpy()
row_of = pd.Series(np.arange(len(customer_ids)), index=customer_ids)
col_of = pd.Series(np.arange(len(product_ids)), index=product_ids)
R = csr_matrix(
    (np.ones(len(train)), (row_of[train["customer_id"]].to_numpy(), col_of[train["product_id"]].to_numpy())),
    shape=(len(customer_ids), len(product_ids)),
)
print(R.shape, f"{R.nnz:,} stored ones")
    (3000, 200) 29,178 stored ones
  6. Two helpers for every recommender (3m). "Any recommender is a score matrix. Mask what they own, sort descending, take ten." Narrate each line of top_k. 
    owned = R.toarray() > 0
test_col = col_of[test.set_index("customer_id").loc[customer_ids, "product_id"]].to_numpy()

def top_k(scores, k=10):
    scores = np.array(scores, dtype=float)      # a copy, so the caller's matrix is untouched
    scores[owned] = -np.inf                     # never recommend what they already have
    return np.argsort(-scores, axis=1)[:, :k]   # column numbers of the k highest scores

def hit_rate(scores, k=10):
    return (top_k(scores, k) == test_col[:, None]).any(axis=1).mean()

print(owned.shape, test_col[:5])
    (3000, 200) [196  46 197  98 107]
  7. The floor and the baseline (2m). Ask for predictions first: "Random? Best sellers?" 
    rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
random_scores = rng.random(R.shape)
popularity = np.asarray(R.sum(axis=0)).ravel()
popularity_scores = np.tile(popularity, (R.shape[0], 1))
print(f"random      HR@10 = {hit_rate(random_scores):.3f}")
print(f"popularity  HR@10 = {hit_rate(popularity_scores):.3f}")
    random      HR@10 = 0.047
popularity  HR@10 = 0.209
    Board: random 0.047, best sellers 0.209. "A personal recommender must beat 0.209 to earn its complexity."
  8. The clerk's trick: item similarity (3m). "Two products are similar if mostly the same customers bought them." 
    from sklearn.metrics.pairwise import cosine_similarity
item_sim = cosine_similarity(R.T)        # products x products
np.fill_diagonal(item_sim, 0)
harness = col_of["CLM-3001"]
nearest = np.argsort(-item_sim[harness])[:4]
print(item_sim.shape, names.iloc[harness])
print(products.iloc[nearest][["name"]].assign(similarity=item_sim[harness, nearest].round(3)))
    (200, 200) Juniper Vane Headwater Climbing Harness
                                       name  similarity
121    Juniper Vane Summit Climbing Harness       0.282
113  Pinecrest Timberline Locking Carabiner       0.268
125         Cedar Notch Wayfinder Chalk Bag       0.267
124                Pinecrest Tempo 60m Rope       0.254
    Nobody told the computer that harnesses, carabiners and ropes belong together.
  9. Score = similarity to what they own (2m). "One multiplication: R @ item_sim." 
    knn_scores = R @ item_sim
print(type(knn_scores).__name__, knn_scores.shape)
print(f"item-kNN    HR@10 = {hit_rate(knn_scores):.3f}")
    ndarray (3000, 200)
item-kNN    HR@10 = 0.308
    0.308: about 50% more hits than the best sellers. The core result, in two lines of linear algebra.
  10. One customer (2m). "Numbers convince; one example builds intuition." 
    r = row_of["C100027"]
print("bought:", names.iloc[np.flatnonzero(owned[r])].tolist())
print("recommended:", names.iloc[top_k(knn_scores, 3)[r]].tolist())
print("really bought next:", names.iloc[test_col[r]])
    bought: ['Wrenfield Trailhead Approach Shoes', 'Pinecrest Wayfinder Approach Shoes', 'Tallgrass Talus Chalk Bag']
recommended: ['Juniper Vane Summit Climbing Harness', 'Juniper Vane Tundra Climbing Pants', 'Wrenfield Meadow Climbing Pants']
really bought next: Cedar Notch Cairn Climbing Harness
    Three climbing purchases in, the model offers climbing gear; the harness bought next is on the full top-5 list (lesson page).
  11. Matrix factorization (3m). "Maybe 200 preferences are really a handful of tastes." Write R ≈ customer factors @ product factors. 
    from sklearn.decomposition import TruncatedSVD
svd = TruncatedSVD(n_components=5, random_state=42)
customer_factors = svd.fit_transform(R)
product_factors = svd.components_
svd_scores = customer_factors @ product_factors
print(customer_factors.shape, product_factors.shape, f"variance captured {svd.explained_variance_ratio_.sum():.1%}")
print(f"SVD k=5     HR@10 = {hit_rate(svd_scores):.3f}")
    (3000, 5) (5, 200) variance captured 17.1%
SVD k=5     HR@10 = 0.311
  12. What did the factors learn? (2m). "The activity column was never shown to the model: a fair check." 
    activity = products["primary_activity"]
for f in range(5):
    top_products = np.argsort(-product_factors[f])[:10]
    print(f"factor {f}:", activity.iloc[top_products].value_counts().to_dict())
    factor 0: {'general': 8, 'hiking': 2}
factor 1: {'paddling': 4, 'general': 3, 'running': 3}
factor 2: {'hiking': 7, 'general': 3}
factor 3: {'climbing': 10}
factor 4: {'running': 8, 'general': 1, 'camping': 1}
    Hiking, climbing, running come out cleanly; factor 0 is popularity. Naming a factor is your interpretation.
  13. How many factors? (2m). "More factors fit the past better. Do they predict better?" 
    svd_hr = {}
for k in [2, 5, 10, 20, 40]:
    model = TruncatedSVD(n_components=k, random_state=42)
    svd_hr[k] = round(float(hit_rate(model.fit_transform(R) @ model.components_)), 3)
print(svd_hr)
    {2: 0.22, 5: 0.311, 10: 0.258, 20: 0.227, 40: 0.19}
    Peak at 5, worse than best sellers at 40: bias-variance in a new costume. Then confess: "We just picked k on the test item. The project does it properly."
  14. Content and the cold start (2m). "A product launched yesterday has no buyers: collaborative filtering can never show it." 
    from sklearn.preprocessing import OneHotEncoder
attributes = OneHotEncoder(sparse_output=False).fit_transform(products[["primary_activity", "category"]])
content_sim = cosine_similarity(attributes)
np.fill_diagonal(content_sim, 0)
content_scores = R @ content_sim
print(f"content     HR@10 = {hit_rate(content_scores):.3f}")
new_product = col_of["PDL-3018"]
cold = R.copy().tolil()
cold[:, new_product] = 0                       # wipe its history: a brand-new product
print("largest item-kNN similarity after wiping:", cosine_similarity(cold.tocsr().T)[new_product].max())
print("content neighbors:", names.iloc[np.argsort(-content_sim[new_product])[:2]].tolist())
    content     HR@10 = 0.192
largest item-kNN similarity after wiping: 0.0
content neighbors: ['Pinecrest Timberline Kayak Paddle', 'Larkspur Ridge Foothill Deck Bag']
  15. Beyond hit rate (2m). "Two lists with the same hit rate can be very different for the shop." 
    best_sellers = np.argsort(-popularity)[:20]
methods = {"best sellers": popularity_scores, "item-kNN": knn_scores, "SVD k=5": svd_scores, "content": content_scores}
for name, scores in methods.items():
    lists = top_k(scores)
    print(f"{name:12s} HR@10 {hit_rate(scores):.3f}  products shown {len(np.unique(lists)):3d}  "
          f"top-20 slots {np.isin(lists, best_sellers).mean():.0%}")
    best sellers HR@10 0.209  products shown  31  top-20 slots 100%
item-kNN     HR@10 0.308  products shown 161  top-20 slots 51%
SVD k=5      HR@10 0.311  products shown 153  top-20 slots 51%
content      HR@10 0.192  products shown 200  top-20 slots 20%
    Then the responsibility box: recommendations create the purchases they are trained on (feedback loops), and purchase lists are personal data.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Similarity on the wrong axis. ValueError: matmul: dimension mismatch with signature (n,k=200),(k=3000,m)->(n,m). Coach: "Print the shape of your similarity matrix. Products × products?" Pass R.T.
  • Sorting ascending, or forgetting the mask. Silent: HR@10: 0.001 (ascending) and owned slots: 14%, HR@10 without the mask: 0.270. Coach: "Compare with random (0.047). A model that loses to random is a bug."
  • Tuning on the test item. Silent and tempting after the k chart. Coach: "Which purchase did you use to choose k?" The project's validation item is the answer.
Guided lab
  • Lab A (everyone, 12m): labs/module-10/l11_2-recommendations/segment_baselines_starter.py; reference labs/module-10/l11_2-recommendations/segment_baselines_solution.py. Is a smarter best-seller list enough? Best sellers of the customer's home store and of their favorite activity, against global best sellers and item-kNN, on the lesson's honest test.
  • Expected, Lab A: global 0.209; home-store best sellers 0.209 (36 products shown, 98% of slots from the top 20: stores barely differ); favorite-activity best sellers 0.265 (183 products, 31%); item-kNN 0.308 (161, 51%). Stretch: customers whose favorite is "general" 0.229 vs 0.280 for item-kNN.
  • Debrief (2m): a cheap, explainable segment baseline closes about half the gap between best sellers and item-kNN, and shows more of the catalog. That is the bar a vendor's "AI recommendations" should be measured against, not the global best-seller list.
  • Lab B (fast pairs, 8m): labs/module-10/l11_2-recommendations/user_based_starter.py; reference labs/module-10/l11_2-recommendations/user_based_solution.py. User-based filtering with n neighbors, n chosen on a validation item, then one test score; and a "because you just bought" list. Expected: validation 0.202 / 0.282 / 0.292 / 0.266 for 10 / 50 / 200 / 1,000 neighbors (item-kNN 0.286); chosen 200; test user-kNN 0.321, item-kNN 0.308; last purchase only 0.212. The last-purchase result is an honest negative: in this data the purchase dates within the period carry little order, so the whole history beats the most recent item.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-10/l11_2-recommendations/debug_recommender.py: a broadcasting error and the wrong axis (loud); the FIRST purchase as the test item and an ascending sort (silent). Fixed: random 0.047, best sellers 0.209, item-kNN 0.308.
Start the lesson project

Picked for You: five products per customer, the model chosen on a validation item and scored once. In the room: Steps 1 to 3.

  • Step 1: three-way split by time. Check: fit 26,178 validation 3,000 test 3,000.
  • Step 2: the helpers as functions of their inputs. Check: (3000, 200) 26178.
  • Step 3: candidates on the validation item. Check: best sellers 0.209, item-kNN 0.286, SVD k=5 0.285: chosen item-kNN (a near tie; either is defensible).
  • Goes home: Step 4 (test once: 0.308 vs 0.209, +47%), Step 5 (retrain on everything, picked_for_you.csv, 15,000 rows), Step 6 (sanity checks: 0 owned, 112 products, 63% of slots from the top 20).
Checks for understanding
  • What does one row of R @ item_sim hold? Answer: each product's summed similarity to everything that customer owns.
  • Why is a random list's hit rate about 0.05? Answer: 10 picks from about 190 unowned products.
  • Why can item-kNN never recommend a brand-new product? Answer: its column is all zeros, so its similarity to everything is 0.
  • Name one cost of the best-seller list besides hit rate. Answer: coverage (31 products); it buries the long tail and new products.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6.

Answers: Q1 B) A bug, such as sorting the scores ascending or forgetting to mask owned products; Q2 [1. 0.41 0.41]; Q3 D) Owned products are similar to what the customer bought, so they score high and crowd the list; customers would see items they already have; Q4 A) Catalog coverage: how many different products appear on anyone's list; Q5 (6, 3) (3, 8); Q6 C) k was tuned on the test item, so 0.311 is optimistic; choose k on a validation item and score the test item once. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: the workbook's worked example (four customers, four products) by hand on paper before the demo's 3,000 × 200.

Stretch: Exercise 4 (who benefits: 11+ products 0.357 vs 0.265 for 2 to 5) and the Bonus Challenge (a blended hybrid chosen on validation).

12.1

Reproducibility and Experiment Tracking

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Name the five ingredients of a reproducible result (code, data, environment, randomness, settings) and capture each one
  • Fingerprint a data file with SHA-256 and record library versions next to a model
  • Save and load a fitted pipeline with joblib, and explain why loading a pickle from an untrusted source can run arbitrary code
  • Save and load models with skops.io, reviewing the untrusted types before you trust them
  • Track parameters, metrics and models with MLflow, query the runs, and load a logged model back
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the lab notebook; five ingredients; a model file is code
30mLive demo: seeds, fingerprint, versions, joblib and the pickle, skops, model card, MLflow runs and a query, three mistakes
20mGuided lab: track_demand_starter.py, then safe_handover_starter.py
15mStart the project: four tracked candidates, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

"A good chemist writes every experiment in a bound lab notebook: which batch of each chemical, which instrument, the temperature, the result, even the failures. Anyone can repeat it, and nobody argues about what was done." Write the five ingredients: code (Git), data (the exact bytes), environment (versions), randomness (seeds), settings (hyperparameters, features, split). "Record all five plus the results and you have a lab notebook for models. MLflow is a lab notebook that fills itself in."

Then the uncomfortable half: "A saved model file is not data. A pickle is a small program, and opening it runs it. How you load a model decides whether a stranger's file can run commands on your machine."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

Notebook lesson_12_1 in a fresh folder (so the MLflow query shows only today's runs). The first MLflow call prints INFO lines while it creates the database: say so before it happens.

  1. The model we look after (2m). "Module 2's churn pipeline: preprocessing inside, so it travels with the model." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer
from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline, make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import OneHotEncoder, StandardScaler

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
numeric = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order", "returns_12m",
           "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user", "email_opens_90d"]
categorical = ["home_store", "region", "age_group", "loyalty_tier", "preferred_category"]
X, y = customers[numeric + categorical], customers["churned"]
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=42)

def build_pipeline(model):
    prep = ColumnTransformer([
        ("num", make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median"), StandardScaler()), numeric),
        ("cat", make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="most_frequent"), OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown="ignore")), categorical),
    ])
    return Pipeline([("prep", prep), ("model", model)])

print(X_train.shape, X_test.shape)
    (4500, 16) (1500, 16)
  2. Randomness: seeds (3m). "Fit the same forest twice without a seed, then with one." 
    from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier
from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score

def forest_probabilities(seed):
    forest = build_pipeline(RandomForestClassifier(n_estimators=50, min_samples_leaf=5, random_state=seed))
    return forest.fit(X_train, y_train).predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]

print("no seed:  two fits identical?", np.array_equal(forest_probabilities(None), forest_probabilities(None)))
print("seed 42:  two fits identical?", np.array_equal(forest_probabilities(42), forest_probabilities(42)))
for seed in [42, 7]:
    print(f"seed {seed:2d}: test AUC {roc_auc_score(y_test, forest_probabilities(seed)):.4f}")
    no seed:  two fits identical? False
seed 42:  two fits identical? True
seed 42: test AUC 0.8612
seed  7: test AUC 0.8560
    Fix the seed so results repeat; and a third-decimal difference between seeds is noise, not a finding.
  3. Data: a fingerprint, not a file name (2m). "Change one character and all 64 change." 
    import hashlib

def file_sha256(path):
    digest = hashlib.sha256()
    with open(path, "rb") as f:
        for chunk in iter(lambda: f.read(1_000_000), b""):   # 1 MB at a time: works for huge files
            digest.update(chunk)
    return digest.hexdigest()

data_hash = file_sha256("data/customers_churn.csv")
print(len(data_hash), "hex characters, starting", data_hash[:16])
    64 hex characters, starting ac541c7e8691d33f
    Same first 16 characters as the lesson page means the same file.
  4. Environment: write down the versions (1m). Then show (do not run) python -m pip freeze > requirements-lock.txt. 
    import platform
from importlib.metadata import version
environment = {"python": platform.python_version()}
for package in ["scikit-learn", "pandas", "numpy", "skops", "mlflow"]:
    environment[package] = version(package)
print(environment)
    {'python': '3.12.14', 'scikit-learn': '1.9.1', 'pandas': '3.0.6', 'numpy': '2.5.4', 'skops': '0.16.0', 'mlflow': '3.17.1'}
    Their versions may differ; what matters is that they record theirs.
  5. Saving with joblib (2m). "Save the whole pipeline: imputer medians, scaler means and categories are part of the model." 
    import joblib
from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingClassifier
churn_model = build_pipeline(HistGradientBoostingClassifier(random_state=42)).fit(X_train, y_train)
test_auc = roc_auc_score(y_test, churn_model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1])
print(f"test AUC: {test_auc:.4f}")
joblib.dump(churn_model, "churn_model.joblib")
reloaded = joblib.load("churn_model.joblib")
print("same predictions after reload:", np.array_equal(reloaded.predict_proba(X_test), churn_model.predict_proba(X_test)))
    test AUC: 0.8598
same predictions after reload: True
  6. A pickle runs code (3m). "Now the catch. This file contains no model; watch what LOADING it does." 
    import pickle

class NotReallyAModel:
    def __reduce__(self):                       # tells pickle how to "rebuild" this object
        return (print, ("This line ran while the file was being LOADED.",))

with open("innocent_looking_model.pkl", "wb") as f:
    pickle.dump(NotReallyAModel(), f)
loaded = joblib.load("innocent_looking_model.pkl")
print("what came back:", loaded)
    This line ran while the file was being LOADED.
what came back: None
    Pause here. "It was print. It could have been anything, with your permissions." Never load a pickle you did not make or fully trust.
  7. skops: review before you trust (2m). "skops describes objects instead of storing instructions. Ask the file what it needs before loading it." 
    import skops.io as sio
sio.dump(churn_model, "churn_model.skops")
print(sio.get_untrusted_types(file="churn_model.skops"))
    ['numpy.dtype', 'sklearn.ensemble._hist_gradient_boosting.predictor.TreePredictor']
  8. Load with a list you wrote out (2m). "Type the list; do not paste whatever the file reports. A future file that asks for something new should stop you." 
    trusted_types = ["numpy.dtype", "sklearn.ensemble._hist_gradient_boosting.predictor.TreePredictor"]
safe_model = sio.load("churn_model.skops", trusted=trusted_types)
print(type(safe_model).__name__, [name for name, _ in safe_model.steps])
print("same predictions:", np.array_equal(safe_model.predict_proba(X_test), churn_model.predict_proba(X_test)))
    Pipeline ['prep', 'model']
same predictions: True
  9. A model card (2m). "A model without its story is half a lab notebook." 
    import json
model_card = {
    "model_file": "churn_model.skops", "estimator": type(churn_model[-1]).__name__,
    "features": numeric + categorical, "data_file": "data/customers_churn.csv", "data_sha256": data_hash,
    "split": {"test_size": 0.25, "stratify": "churned", "random_state": 42},
    "train_rows": len(X_train), "test_auc": round(test_auc, 4),
    "environment": environment, "skops_trusted_types": trusted_types,
}
with open("churn_model.json", "w") as f:
    json.dump(model_card, f, indent=2)
print({key: model_card[key] for key in ["estimator", "train_rows", "test_auc"]})
    {'estimator': 'HistGradientBoostingClassifier', 'train_rows': 4500, 'test_auc': 0.8598}
    Add aloud what professional cards add: intended use ("rank customers for retention offers, not decisions about individual accounts"), limitations, fairness checks.
  10. MLflow with a SQLite store (2m). "A lab notebook that fills itself in. Local file, no server, no account." Warn about the INFO lines on first use. 
    import mlflow
mlflow.set_tracking_uri("sqlite:///mlflow.db")     # a single local database file in this folder
mlflow.set_experiment("churn-lesson")
print(mlflow.get_tracking_uri())
    sqlite:///mlflow.db
  11. One run per candidate (3m). "Log every hyperparameter, including the defaults you never typed." 
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold, cross_val_score

cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
candidates = {"logreg": LogisticRegression(max_iter=1000), "hgb": HistGradientBoostingClassifier(random_state=42)}
for name, model in candidates.items():
    with mlflow.start_run(run_name=name):
        scores = cross_val_score(build_pipeline(model), X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
        mlflow.log_params(model.get_params())
        mlflow.set_tags({"estimator": type(model).__name__, "data_sha256": data_hash})
        mlflow.log_metrics({"cv_auc": scores.mean(), "cv_auc_std": scores.std()})
        print(f"{name:7s} CV AUC {scores.mean():.3f} ± {scores.std():.3f}")
    logreg  CV AUC 0.832 ± 0.023
hgb     CV AUC 0.861 ± 0.013
  12. Query the store (2m). "In a real project the runs come from different days and people: query, do not scroll." 
    runs = mlflow.search_runs(experiment_names=["churn-lesson"], order_by=["metrics.cv_auc DESC"])
print(runs[["tags.mlflow.runName", "metrics.cv_auc", "params.max_iter"]].round(4).to_string(index=False))
    tags.mlflow.runName  metrics.cv_auc params.max_iter
                hgb          0.8615             100
             logreg          0.8324            1000
    Parameters are text, metrics numbers. If time allows, mlflow ui --backend-store-uri sqlite:///mlflow.db in a terminal and open http://127.0.0.1:5000 for a minute; Ctrl+C to stop.
  13. Three loud mistakes (3m). "No reviewed list; an old tutorial's mlruns folder; changing a parameter inside a run." (First line of each message.) 
    try:
    sio.load("churn_model.skops")
except Exception as e:
    print(type(e).__name__ + ":", str(e).splitlines()[0])

try:
    mlflow.set_tracking_uri("./mlruns")
    mlflow.set_experiment("churn-lesson")
except Exception as e:
    print(type(e).__name__ + ":", str(e).split(". ")[0] + ".")
mlflow.set_tracking_uri("sqlite:///mlflow.db")

try:
    with mlflow.start_run(run_name="oops"):
        mlflow.log_param("learning_rate", 0.1)
        mlflow.log_param("learning_rate", 0.05)
except Exception as e:
    print(type(e).__name__ + ":", str(e).split(". ")[0] + ".")
    UntrustedTypesFoundException: Untrusted types found in the file: ['numpy.dtype', 'sklearn.ensemble._hist_gradient_boosting.predictor.TreePredictor'].
MlflowException: The filesystem tracking backend (e.g., './mlruns') is in maintenance mode and will not receive further updates.
MlflowException: Changing param values is not allowed.
    Each refusal is a feature: skops makes you decide, MLflow 3.17 needs a database (sqlite:///mlflow.db), a logged parameter cannot be rewritten (one run per setting; the failed run stays in the store).
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Saving the classifier instead of the pipeline. ValueError: could not convert string to float: 'Asheville' (raw columns reach a model that expects preprocessed numbers). Coach: "What does your file contain: type(obj)?" Save the whole Pipeline.
  • Trusting whatever the file asks for. trusted=sio.get_untrusted_types(file=path) silences skops and defeats it. Coach: "Read me the list. Which of these did you expect for this model?" (Lab 12.1 B: the list depends on the model.)
  • The old mlruns store and parameters changed inside a run. Loud, with good messages. Coach: one run per setting, sqlite:///mlflow.db, and read the message to the end: it names the fix.
Guided lab
  • Lab A (everyone, 12m): labs/module-10/l12_1-reproducibility/track_demand_starter.py; reference labs/module-10/l12_1-reproducibility/track_demand_solution.py. A lab notebook for the weekly demand forecast: CONFIG, fingerprint, three candidates tracked in MLflow (SQLite and artifacts inside OUTPUT), the winner chosen by querying the store (lowest MAE: ASC), one test score, a filtered query and a seed check. About 30 seconds.
  • Expected, Lab A: fingerprint c48cd2582060519c; fit 3,840 / validation 1,248 / test 4,896; validation MAE ridge 12.63, forest 11.31, hgb 10.74; hgb first; test MAE (2025) 8.21; below 12: hgb and forest; 4 runs in the store; hgb 10.7445 for every seed; forest 11.3146 twice and 11.2821 with seed 7.
  • Debrief (2m): two traps learners hit: DESC for an error metric (it puts the worst first), and believing a seed matters for every model. The HGB regressor on 3,840 rows uses no randomness (early stopping only switches on above 10,000 rows), so its seed changes nothing; the forest's does. The validation weeks (October to December) are harder than 2025 as a whole, so validation MAE is higher than test MAE: a split by season, not a bug.
  • Lab B (fast pairs, 8m): labs/module-10/l12_1-reproducibility/safe_handover_starter.py; reference labs/module-10/l12_1-reproducibility/safe_handover_solution.py. Hand a fraud model to another team: skops, a refused load, trust lists for three model kinds, a model card, and a verify function that rebuilds the score from the card alone. Expected: AP 0.4743; HGB needs only TreePredictor; logistic regression []; forest ['sklearn.tree._tree.Tree']; verify (True, True, 0.4743) for the original, a byte copy and a pandas re-save; (False, True, 0.4743) after a one-cent edit; skops 462 kB vs joblib 142 kB.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-10/l12_1-reproducibility/debug_reproducibility.py: ./mlruns and a parameter logged twice (loud); an unseeded forest and an automatic trust list (silent). Fixed, two runs print the same lines ending test AUC 0.8637.
Start the lesson project

A tracked, reproducible churn model. In the room: Steps 1 to 3.

  • Step 1: one CONFIG, one fingerprint. Check: data fingerprint: ac541c7e8691d33f.
  • Step 2: data, split, four candidates. Check: (4500, 16) ['logreg', 'forest', 'hgb', 'hgb-slow'].
  • Step 3: one run per candidate. Check: CV AUC logreg 0.8324, forest 0.8678, hgb 0.8615, hgb-slow 0.8588.
  • Goes home: Step 4 (pick from the store: forest; test once 0.8612), Step 5 (churn_final.skops with trusted numpy.dtype and sklearn.tree._tree.Tree, churn_final.json, the final run), Step 6 (Reproduced : True). Keep both files and mlflow.db: Lesson 12.2 serves exactly this model.
Checks for understanding
  • Name the five ingredients. Answer: code, data, environment, randomness, settings.
  • Same seed, same data, different laptop: could the AUC differ? Answer: in the last digits, yes (thread count, math libraries, versions): that is why the environment is recorded.
  • What did the harmless pickle prove? Answer: loading a pickle runs code chosen by whoever made the file.
  • Why write the trusted list by hand? Answer: so a file that asks for something new stops you instead of loading.

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12.

Answers: Q7 C) The random seed of the forest; Q8 64 True; Q9 A) UntrustedTypesFoundException: it names the HistGradientBoosting tree type, which you must review and pass in trusted=; Q10 D) Loading a joblib file (a pickle) can run arbitrary code; skops only rebuilds known types or types you trust explicitly; Q11 True None; Q12 B) mlflow.search_runs(experiment_names=["churn"], order_by=["metrics.cv_auc DESC"]). Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: the five-ingredient checklist from the workbook next to the keyboard; tick each one as the demo records it.

Stretch: Exercise 4 (train_churn.py, run twice); the Bonus Challenge (autolog vs explicit logging).

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)
  1. Item-kNN scores HR@10 0.308 and the best sellers 0.209. Explain in one sentence what that comparison means for Pinecrest's email. Answer: the personal list contains the product a customer really bought next for about 31% of customers against 21% for the best-seller row: about 50% more hits, from the same purchase history.
  2. Name the five ingredients of a reproducible result and one tool or habit for each. Answer: code (Git, a script), data (SHA-256 fingerprint), environment (versions, pip freeze), randomness (seeds), settings (a CONFIG dict, logged params).
  3. A teammate sends model.joblib. What do you do before loading it? Answer: only load it if you fully trust the source; better, ask for a skops file, review get_untrusted_types, and load with an explicit trusted= list.
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish both projects. 11.2 Steps 4 to 6 (test HR@10 0.308 vs 0.209, picked_for_you.csv, sanity checks); 12.1 Steps 4 to 6 (forest, test AUC 0.8612, churn_final.skops + churn_final.json, Reproduced : True).
  • Lesson exercises. 11.2: Exercises 1 to 3 (list lengths, purchase counts 0.302, cold start: 100% paddlers); 12.1: Exercises 1 to 3 (fingerprint sensitivity, a logreg trust list, a filtered query). Exercise 4 of each is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes and both journal entries (12.1: the five ingredients for an earlier project of yours).
  • Before the Next Lesson (from 12.1): run mlflow ui --backend-store-uri sqlite:///mlflow.db and explore; add mlflow.db, mlruns/ and *.skops to .gitignore.
Bring list for Session 11
  • A laptop on which check_environment.py ends with READY: Session 11 uses FastAPI, uvicorn and httpx (all in the course requirements.txt; nothing new to install).
  • The 12.1 project files churn_final.skops and churn_final.json (and mlflow.db): Lesson 12.2 serves exactly this model. (12.2 can recreate them, but bring yours.)
  • The pinecrest-ml folder with data/ (customers_churn.csv) and labs/ (Session 11 starters are in labs/module-11/).
  • The workbook and the journal; a terminal that can start uvicorn.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: 11.2 demo Steps 4 to 9; 12.1 demo Steps 6 to 8 and 12; Lab A of each lesson to TODO 3.
  • Cut first (saves 4m): 11.2 demo Steps 11 and 14: show the lesson page's tables.
  • Cut second (saves 3m): 12.1 demo Steps 4 and 9: one sentence each.

Running short: what to add

Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 11: Serving, Monitoring and the Churn Case Study

How to teach the last two technical lessons of Advanced Machine Learning and launch the capstone: serve the churn model behind a validated FastAPI service with a match test and a drift monitor, then run the whole churn case study from a manager's brief to a memo in dollars. Learners leave with a capstone problem chosen and a one-paragraph plan written.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 12.2–13.1 Session 11 of 13

How to Use This Guide

  • Two lessons that close the technical course: 12.2 takes the churn model out of the notebook (a validated API and a drift monitor); 13.1 runs the whole churn project end to end, ending in a memo. The last ten minutes before the wrap-up start the capstone: each learner chooses one of Lesson 13.2's five problems and writes a one-paragraph plan.
  • Same rhythm for both lessons: frame → live demo (you type, they follow) → guided kit lab (new practice: a FRAUD API and monitor; a leak audit and cost scenarios) → start the lesson project. Exercises, quizzes and journals are homework.
  • Every code block below was run to produce the output under it (Python 3.12, pandas 3.0.6, scikit-learn 1.9.1, FastAPI 0.143, pydantic 2.14, skops 0.16, 4 CPU threads). Ask for a prediction before each run that prints a number.
  • Keep mlops_deployment.html and case_study_churn.html open for the diagrams and charts (the order-window flow, the drift histograms, the profit curve, the partial-dependence plots). Labs are in labs/module-11/; the capstone reference notes for Sessions 12 and 13 are in final-assessment.html at the kit root: read the problem summaries before today's last segment.
The #1 risk of Session 11: two big lessons and a capstone launch in three hours

12.2 has two new libraries and two new files; 13.1 has twelve steps that each recap a module. Both demos are long, and the capstone choice at 2:40 must not be eaten. Protect the clock: start the 13.1 randomized search (demo Step 6) while you talk, never wait for it in silence; if 12.2 runs over, cut 12.2 Steps 11 to 13 to the PSI table alone (it is on the lesson page) rather than touching the capstone segment. A learner who leaves without a chosen problem loses the first hour of Session 12.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Run the kit check: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-11 from the kit folder: 8 passed (about 40 seconds). Run fraud_api_solution.py once on the projector machine so the imports are warm.
  • ☐ Learner files from Session 10: the Lesson 12.1 project's churn_final.skops and churn_final.json. Anyone without them runs the recreate block at the top of Lesson 12.2's "Package the Model" (about 5 seconds): no blocker.
  • ☐ Packages: fastapi, pydantic, httpx (TestClient needs it), uvicorn and skops are in the course requirements.txt and in the Session 1 environment check. No downloads today: no image data, no weights, no internet.
  • ☐ Projector machine: a new folder serving/ next to data/, an editor tab for schemas.py and one for serve.py (empty), a notebook lesson_12_2, a second notebook lesson_13_1, a terminal with (.venv) for the optional uvicorn demo. Font 18 pt or more.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: the four-row monitoring table (input drift · data quality · prediction drift: no labels; performance: labels, months later); the PSI rules of thumb (below 0.1 stable, 0.1 to 0.25 watch, above 0.25 shift); and the 13.1 outcome table (TP +$45, FP -$15, FN $0, TN $0).
  • ☐ Print or share: the workbook (its last page has the capstone plan box), and the five problem summaries from Lesson 13.2 (one screen or a printed half-page per table).
Known environment traps
SymptomCauseFix
StarletteDeprecationWarning printed when importing fastapi.testclientA deprecation notice inside the installed StarletteHarmless (stderr only). The labs filter it; say so once.
ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'schemas' in the notebookschemas.py was saved in another folder than the notebook's working folderprint(Path.cwd()); save both files next to the notebook (the serving/ folder) and restart the kernel.
Changes to serve.py do not show up in the notebookPython imported the module once and caches itRestart the kernel (or importlib.reload(serve)); with uvicorn, --reload restarts on save.
ERROR: [Errno 98] Address already in use (or 10048 on Windows) from uvicornA server from earlier still runs on port 8000Ctrl+C in its terminal, or uvicorn serve:app --port 8001.
'uvicorn' is not recognized / command not foundThe terminal's environment is not activeActivate .venv; or python -m uvicorn serve:app --reload.
UntrustedTypesFoundException when loading the skops fileThe file needs a type that is not in the trusted listPrint sio.get_untrusted_types(file=...), review it, write the list out. Never pass the file's list blindly.
InconsistentVersionWarning while loading the modelThe file was saved with another scikit-learn versionTreat as an error: reinstall the pinned versions or re-save the model in this environment.
ValueError: Out of range float values are not JSON compliant: nanA DataFrame with NaN sent straight to the clientto_json_records: missing values travel as None.
The 13.1 randomized search runs a minute or moreSlow laptop, or another heavy programFine: start it and keep talking; or set n_iter=5 (the best settings may differ slightly; say so).
Windows asks to allow Python through the firewall when uvicorn startsA server opens a portAllow on private networks only, or cancel: 127.0.0.1 works either way.

In the room. The pages estimate 105 + 120 = 225 minutes for a solo learner; the room has 140 for the two lessons (70 each), plus ten minutes for the capstone choice. So the room protects the mental model, the demo, one kit lab and the first project steps of each lesson.

Warm-up

0:00

Retrieval from Lessons 11.2 and 12.1

10 min
Five questions (with answers)
  1. Lesson 11.2 hid each customer's LATEST purchase for testing, not a random one. Why? Answer: It mimics the real job (predict the next purchase from earlier ones) and keeps future purchases out of the training matrix: the same rule as splitting by time in Lesson 1.2.
  2. Best sellers scored a hit rate at 10 of 0.209 and item-kNN 0.308. Say what 0.308 means in one sentence. Answer: For about 31% of customers, the product they really bought next was in their personal top-10 list (already-owned products excluded): about 50% more often than the best-seller list.
  3. Why did SVD with 40 factors recommend worse than with 5? Answer: With many factors it memorizes each customer's past basket instead of broad tastes, so it generalizes worse (0.19 against 0.311 at k = 5).
  4. A colleague emails you churn_model.joblib. What is the risk of loading it, and what did Lesson 12.1 use instead? Answer: A joblib file is a pickle: loading it can run arbitrary code. Lesson 12.1 saved with skops, which loads only reviewed types passed to trusted=.
  5. Your model card stores a SHA-256 of customers_churn.csv. Today the hash differs. What do you know, and what don't you? Answer: The file is not byte-for-byte the one the model was trained on; the hash cannot say what changed (one row, the order, a column).
How to run it

Questions on the slide, four minutes on paper (workbook Warm-up box), then answers called out. While they write, ask who has the 12.1 files (churn_final.skops, churn_final.json); the others will run the recreate block in demo Step 1.

Serving and Monitoring Models

70 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Package a fitted Pipeline with the metadata a server needs (features, dtypes, threshold, version)
  • Describe valid input with a pydantic model and read its validation errors
  • Serve predictions with FastAPI, test every endpoint with TestClient, and start the server with uvicorn
  • Measure input drift with the Population Stability Index and prediction drift with simple batch statistics
  • Tell a data-quality bug from a real change in customers, and say which one needs labels to confirm
Timing in the room

Mental model to say

"The fitted model is a kitchen that can cook one dish. Customers never walk into the kitchen: they hand an order through a window, and the person at the window checks it first. Is every field filled in? Is Denver one of our stores? Is a discount share of 1.4 even possible? Bad orders go back with a note; good ones go to the kitchen. FastAPI is the window, pydantic is the person checking orders, your Pipeline is the kitchen."

Then point at the monitoring table on the board: "A deployed model does not wear out. It fails quietly: the customers change, or a pipe breaks, and it keeps answering with status 200. Pinecrest only learns who churned six months later, so we watch what we can see now: inputs, data quality, the scores."

  3. The order window: schemas.py (3m). Create schemas.py in the editor, not a notebook cell. "Every limit comes from the data dictionary, not from the model." Point at extra="forbid", the two optional fields and the Literal stores. 
    """schemas.py: what a valid request to the churn API looks like."""
from typing import Literal

from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, Field

Store = Literal["Online", "Boise", "Bend", "Flagstaff", "Asheville"]
Region = Literal["Northwest", "Southwest", "Southeast", "Mountain", "Midwest", "Northeast"]
AgeGroup = Literal["18-24", "25-34", "35-44", "45-54", "55-64", "65+"]
Tier = Literal["Basic", "Silver", "Gold"]
Category = Literal["Accessories", "Apparel", "Camp Kitchen", "Climbing", "Footwear",
                   "Packs", "Sleeping", "Snow", "Tents"]


class Customer(BaseModel):
    """One customer's 12-month summary, in the same units the model was trained on."""
    model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid")      # unknown fields are an error, not ignored
    tenure_months: int = Field(ge=1, le=600)
    orders_12m: int = Field(ge=0, le=500)
    spend_12m: float = Field(ge=0)
    avg_order_value: float = Field(ge=0)
    days_since_last_order: int = Field(ge=0, le=365)
    returns_12m: int = Field(ge=0)
    return_rate: float = Field(ge=0, le=1)
    support_tickets_12m: int = Field(ge=0)
    discount_share: float = Field(ge=0, le=1)
    app_user: int = Field(ge=0, le=1)
    email_opens_90d: float | None = Field(default=None, ge=0)   # may be unknown
    home_store: Store
    region: Region
    age_group: AgeGroup | None = None                           # may be unknown
    loyalty_tier: Tier
    preferred_category: Category


class Score(BaseModel):
    """What the API sends back for one customer."""
    churn_probability: float
    offer: bool
    model_version: str
    Ask: "Which two fields may be missing, and why those two?" (they are missing in training too).

}

    5.0 float

  6. serve.py: the whole service (4m). Create serve.py. Read it top to bottom with them: the model loads ONCE at start-up; to_frame is the most important function (columns, order AND dtypes); three endpoints. 
    """serve.py: the Pinecrest churn-scoring API."""
import json

import pandas as pd
import skops.io as sio
from fastapi import FastAPI

from schemas import Customer, Score

with open("churn_serving.json") as f:
    SERVING = json.load(f)
with open(SERVING["model_card"]) as f:
    CARD = json.load(f)
MODEL = sio.load(CARD["model_file"], trusted=CARD["skops_trusted_types"])   # only the reviewed types
FEATURES = SERVING["features"]
DTYPES = SERVING["dtypes"]
THRESHOLD = SERVING["threshold"]
VERSION = SERVING["version"]

app = FastAPI(title="Pinecrest churn scoring", version=VERSION)


def to_frame(customers: list[Customer]) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Validated customers to a DataFrame with the training columns, order and dtypes."""
    rows = [c.model_dump() for c in customers]
    return pd.DataFrame(rows, columns=FEATURES).astype(DTYPES)


def to_score(p: float) -> Score:
    return Score(churn_probability=round(float(p), 4), offer=bool(p >= THRESHOLD), model_version=VERSION)


@app.get("/health")
def health() -> dict:
    return {"status": "ok", "model_version": VERSION}


@app.post("/predict")
def predict(customer: Customer) -> Score:
    return to_score(MODEL.predict_proba(to_frame([customer]))[0, 1])


@app.post("/predict_batch")
def predict_batch(customers: list[Customer]) -> list[Score]:
    return [to_score(p) for p in MODEL.predict_proba(to_frame(customers))[:, 1]]
    Say: "If this file has a bug, it is almost always in to_frame."
  7. Test it without a server (2m). "TestClient sends requests straight to the app object: no network, milliseconds." Some machines print a StarletteDeprecationWarning on the import: harmless (trap table).

print(client.get("/health").json())
response = client.post("/predict", json=example)
print(response.status_code, response.json())

    def to_json_records(frame):
    """DataFrame rows as dicts, with missing values as None (JSON null)."""
    return frame.astype(object).where(frame.notna(), None).to_dict(orient="records")
week = X_test.head(5)
scores = pd.DataFrame(client.post("/predict_batch", json=to_json_records(week)).json())
print(scores)
direct = churn_model.predict_proba(week)[:, 1]
print("API matches the notebook:", np.allclose(scores["churn_probability"], direct, atol=1e-4))

  10. A bug with no error (2m). "Build the one-row frame WITHOUT the stored dtypes. Watch the probability." 
    row = {**example, "age_group": None}

    Training-serving skew: a None makes the column object, the imputer passes it through, the encoder treats it as unknown. Small here; it can flip an offer.
  11. A rough season, simulated (2m). "Half the customers hit by a 60-day delay, a long sale, a broken email tracker." 
    rng = np.random.default_rng(42)       # seeded, so the simulation is the same every run
stable = X_test.copy()
shifted = X_test.copy()
delayed = rng.random(len(shifted)) < 0.5
shifted.loc[delayed, "days_since_last_order"] = (shifted.loc[delayed, "days_since_last_order"] + 60).clip(upper=364)
shifted["discount_share"] = (shifted["discount_share"] * 1.5).clip(upper=0.833)
lost_email = rng.random(len(shifted)) < 0.25
shifted.loc[lost_email, "email_opens_90d"] = np.nan
for name, frame in [("reference", X_train), ("stable", stable), ("shifted", shifted)]:
    print(f"{name:10s} days {frame['days_since_last_order'].mean():5.1f}   "
          f"discount {frame['discount_share'].mean():.3f}   email missing {frame['email_opens_90d'].isna().mean():.1%}")
    reference  days  83.5   discount 0.209   email missing 9.3%
stable     days  83.6   discount 0.204   email missing 7.9%
shifted    days 112.7   discount 0.299   email missing 31.8%
  12. PSI points at what moved (3m). Write the PSI formula and the 0.1 / 0.25 conventions on the board. "Chosen before you look." 
    def psi(reference, current, bins=10):
    """Population Stability Index of one numeric column; missing values get their own bin."""
    edges = np.unique(np.quantile(reference.dropna(), np.linspace(0, 1, bins + 1)))
    edges[0], edges[-1] = -np.inf, np.inf
    def shares(values):
        counts = np.append(np.histogram(values.dropna(), bins=edges)[0], values.isna().sum())
        return np.clip(counts / len(values), 1e-4, None)      # avoid log(0) for empty bins
    ref, cur = shares(reference), shares(current)
    return float(np.sum((cur - ref) * np.log(cur / ref)))
drift = pd.DataFrame({
    "psi_stable": {col: psi(X_train[col], stable[col]) for col in NUMERIC},
    "psi_shifted": {col: psi(X_train[col], shifted[col]) for col in NUMERIC},
})
drift["status"] = pd.cut(drift["psi_shifted"], [-np.inf, 0.1, 0.25, np.inf], labels=["stable", "watch", "SHIFT"])
print(drift.round(3))
                           psi_stable  psi_shifted  status
tenure_months               0.008        0.008  stable
orders_12m                  0.001        0.001  stable
spend_12m                   0.005        0.005  stable
avg_order_value             0.012        0.012  stable
days_since_last_order       0.002        0.336   SHIFT
returns_12m                 0.000        0.000  stable
return_rate                 0.008        0.008  stable
support_tickets_12m         0.001        0.001  stable
discount_share              0.013        0.200   watch
app_user                    0.000        0.000  stable
email_opens_90d             0.009        0.350   SHIFT
    Untouched features stay stable; recency and email opens SHIFT, discount share 'watch'. That is where the investigation starts.
  13. Prediction drift, against the reference (2m). "More customers cross 0.25: the offer budget was planned for 24%." 
    batch_scores = {}
for name, frame in [("stable", stable), ("shifted", shifted)]:
    batch_scores[name] = pd.DataFrame(client.post("/predict_batch", json=to_json_records(frame)).json())
    s = batch_scores[name]
    print(f"{name:9s} mean probability {s['churn_probability'].mean():.3f}   offers {s['offer'].mean():.1%}")
print(f"reference mean probability {serving['reference_mean_probability']:.3f}   "
      f"offers {serving['reference_offer_rate']:.1%}")
    stable    mean probability 0.163   offers 24.1%
shifted   mean probability 0.209   offers 35.7%
reference mean probability 0.166   offers 24.4%
    Close with the three answers: email = data bug (fix the feed), delay = real change (someone decides about budget), sale = possible concept drift (only labels confirm).
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • NaN sent as JSON. client.post(..., json=frame.to_dict("records")) with gaps gives ValueError: Out of range float values are not JSON compliant: nan. Coach: "Which value is not JSON?" → to_json_records.
  • The frame built without the stored columns or dtypes. No error, a slightly different number (Step 10). Coach with the match test: "Does the API agree with the notebook on rows WITH gaps?"
  • Drift read as a verdict. "PSI is high, retrain!" Coach: "Data bug or real change? Which labels would you retrain on?" A broken feed needs a fix; retraining on it teaches the model the bug.
Guided lab
  • File: labs/module-11/l12_2-serving/fraud_api_starter.py (reference labs/module-11/l12_2-serving/fraud_api_solution.py). The same three habits on a NEW model: card fraud. A model trained on January to April, a threshold at the review team's capacity (the riskiest 1% of May), a Payment schema, /score and /score_batch, then June through the API.
  • Expected: threshold 0.0775; bad has 4 problems and the API answers 422; the API matches the notebook (True); June: 83 of 8,262 held (1.00%), 35 of the 64 frauds among them.
  • A teaching moment in TODO 1: skops asks to trust functools.partial and check_array besides the tree predictor (they come from categorical_features="from_dtype"). Ask: "Would you trust functools.partial from a stranger's file?" That is why the list is reviewed and written out, never copied from the file.
  • Circulate for: a threshold set at 0.5 (nothing is held: fraud scores are tiny); categories rebuilt without the training list; predict used for holds.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-11/l12_2-serving/fraud_monitor_starter.py: REAL months, no simulation. Every PSI stays under 0.01 and average precision is steady as the fraud rate falls; then a broken velocity_24h feed drops the hold rate to 0.25% and the frauds held from 19 to 10. Or labs/module-11/l12_2-serving/debug_serving.py: four bugs between JSON and the model.
Start the lesson project

The project is a weekly drift report: a monitor.py module, three simulated weeks scored through /predict_batch, and a note per week. In the room: Steps 1 to 3 (load, write monitor.py, simulate).

  • Done in the room: churn-forest-2025-06-30 (4500, 17) (1500, 16); monitor.py saved; tenure 44.0 / 25.0 / 42.7 and email missing 8% / 6% / 100% for weeks 1 to 3.
  • Goes home: Steps 4 to 6: 500 scores per week with status 200; the summary table (week 2 tenure PSI 0.383, week 3 email PSI 8.368); drift_report.csv and three different actions.
Checks for understanding
  • Why does the model load once, at start-up? Answer: loading takes far longer than predicting; per request it would make every call slow.
  • Which monitoring check needs labels? Answer: performance (AUC, offer precision), months later. Input drift, data quality and prediction drift do not.
  • Week 3's PSI is 8.4 but the scores barely moved. How? Answer: the imputer filled every missing email value with the median; only the missing-rate and PSI checks warned.

Workbook self-check, Q1 to Q6:

Answers: Q1 B) pydantic rejects it before the model runs: status 422, and the body names home_store and the allowed stores; Q2 60 1.0; Q3 D) A one-row table with a None gets the object dtype, the imputer does not see it as missing, and the model scores a slightly different customer with no error; Q4 A) A broken data feed: fix it and re-score the week; do not retrain on it; Q5 C) 0.416; Q6 B) No: with enough rows any difference is detectable; PSI says the shift is tiny, and an alert should be about size. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: pairs share one keyboard for the lab; give strugglers the schema and to_frame from the lesson to adapt rather than write. Stretch: 12.2 Exercise 4 (KS vs PSI) and the Bonus Challenge (request logging, then the label check); debug_serving.py.

13.1

Case Study: Predicting Churn

70 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Translate a business goal into a target, a metric and a dollar value for each kind of right and wrong prediction
  • Detect a target leak with cross-validation and a crosstab, and remove it before modeling
  • Compare and tune models inside a Pipeline with cross-validation, keeping the test set locked until one final check
  • Choose a decision threshold from business costs with TunedThresholdClassifierCV and report the result in dollars
  • Explain the model honestly and write a short recommendation for a non-technical stakeholder
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the expedition plan; the brief in dollars
30mLive demo: Steps 1 to 12
20mGuided lab: leak_audit_starter.py, then cost_scenarios_starter.py in pairs
10mStart the project: the retention campaign plan, Steps 1 and 2
Mental model to say

"A mountain guide does not start by choosing boots. Destination and budget first, then hazards on the route, a fallback plan, and only then packing. At the end, a report to the people who paid, in their words. In a machine-learning project the algorithm is the boots: important, chosen late, and rarely what the client asks about."

Then the brief: "The manager asks: who should get the offer, and what will it earn? That is a decision, not a prediction. Every step today serves that decision."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_13_1. Start Step 6's search and keep talking; it takes about 20 seconds on a laptop, longer on slow ones. Ask for a prediction before Steps 4, 5, 7 and 8.

  1. The brief in dollars (2m). "Start from the decision and its costs. They set the metric, the baselines and even the threshold." 
    OFFER_COST = 15     # dollars per customer contacted
CHURNER_VALUE = 60  # expected dollars per real churner contacted
print(f"net gain per churner offered     : ${CHURNER_VALUE - OFFER_COST}")
print(f"net cost per non-churner offered : ${OFFER_COST}")
print(f"break-even churn probability     : {OFFER_COST / CHURNER_VALUE:.2f}")
    net gain per churner offered     : $45
net cost per non-churner offered : $15
break-even churn probability     : 0.25
    Write the four-row outcome table: TP +$45, FP -$15, FN $0, TN $0. Accuracy is not on the list.
  2. Explore with a question in mind (3m). "Is there signal, and what shape is it?" 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
pd.set_option("display.width", 150)
customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
print(customers.shape, f"churn rate {customers['churned'].mean():.1%}")
recency = pd.cut(customers["days_since_last_order"], [-1, 30, 90, 150, 250, 364])
print(customers.groupby(recency, observed=True)["churned"].mean().round(3))
    (6000, 20) churn rate 16.5%
days_since_last_order
(-1, 30]      0.091
(30, 90]      0.095
(90, 150]     0.152
(150, 250]    0.415
(250, 364]    0.558
Name: churned, dtype: float64
    A threshold near 150 days, not a slope. Mention the other two shapes in one breath (tickets × app interaction, tenure U): they argue for trees.
  3. Split once, and the two policies that need no model (3m). "Lock the test set away now." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
NUMERIC = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
           "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user", "email_opens_90d"]
CATEGORICAL = ["home_store", "region", "age_group", "loyalty_tier", "preferred_category"]
FEATURES = NUMERIC + CATEGORICAL
train, test = train_test_split(customers, test_size=0.25, stratify=customers["churned"], random_state=42)
as_float = {col: float for col in NUMERIC}
X_train, y_train = train[FEATURES].astype(as_float), train["churned"]
X_test, y_test = test[FEATURES].astype(as_float), test["churned"]
def campaign_profit(y_true, flagged):
    """Expected profit of contacting every flagged customer."""
    y_true, flagged = np.asarray(y_true), np.asarray(flagged)
    tp = ((flagged == 1) & (y_true == 1)).sum()
    return CHURNER_VALUE * tp - OFFER_COST * flagged.sum()
print(X_train.shape, X_test.shape)
print("offer to nobody  ", campaign_profit(y_train, np.zeros(len(y_train), dtype=int)))
print("offer to everyone", campaign_profit(y_train, np.ones(len(y_train), dtype=int)))
    (4500, 16) (1500, 16)
offer to nobody   0
offer to everyone -22860
    Offering to everyone loses about $23,000 on 4,500 customers: five offers in six go to stayers.
  4. The leak check (4m). "Nobody tells you which column is the trap. Add the suspicious one and see." 
    from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer
from sklearn.preprocessing import OneHotEncoder, StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold, cross_val_score
def make_prep(numeric, categorical):
    return ColumnTransformer([
        ("num", make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median"), StandardScaler()), numeric),
        ("cat", make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="most_frequent"),
                              OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown="ignore", sparse_output=False)), categorical),
    ])
cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
with_reason = train.assign(cancellation_reason=train["cancellation_reason"].fillna("none"))
leaky = make_pipeline(make_prep(NUMERIC, CATEGORICAL + ["cancellation_reason"]), LogisticRegression(max_iter=1000))
honest = make_pipeline(make_prep(NUMERIC, CATEGORICAL), LogisticRegression(max_iter=1000))
for name, pipe, data in [("with cancellation_reason", leaky, with_reason), ("without", honest, train)]:
    scores = cross_val_score(pipe, data, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
    print(f"{name:25s} CV AUC {scores.mean():.3f}")
print(pd.crosstab(train["cancellation_reason"].notna(), train["churned"]))
    with cancellation_reason  CV AUC 0.937
without                   CV AUC 0.832
churned                 0    1
cancellation_reason           
False                3756  278
True                    0  466
    0.937 against 0.832 and a zero in the stayers' column: recorded after the outcome. Today's lab turns this into a function for every column.
  5. Compare fairly (4m). "Same preprocessing, same folds, same metric." Predict the ranking before running. 
    from sklearn.dummy import DummyClassifier
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier, HistGradientBoostingClassifier
candidates = {
    "baseline (prior)": DummyClassifier(strategy="prior"),
    "logistic regression": LogisticRegression(max_iter=1000),
    "random forest": RandomForestClassifier(n_estimators=200, min_samples_leaf=5, random_state=42, n_jobs=-1),
    "hist gradient boosting": HistGradientBoostingClassifier(random_state=42),
}
cv_auc = {}
for name, model in candidates.items():
    scores = cross_val_score(make_pipeline(make_prep(NUMERIC, CATEGORICAL), model), X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
    cv_auc[name] = scores
    print(f"{name:23s} AUC {scores.mean():.3f} +/- {scores.std():.3f}")
    baseline (prior)        AUC 0.500 +/- 0.000
logistic regression     AUC 0.832 +/- 0.023
random forest           AUC 0.868 +/- 0.020
hist gradient boosting  AUC 0.861 +/- 0.013
    Forest and boosting are within one spread: honestly undecided.
  6. Tune the one with room to improve (4m). "Note the model__ prefix." The search takes about 20 seconds: talk through why boosting's defaults suit bigger data. 
    from sklearn.model_selection import RandomizedSearchCV
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
hgb_pipe = Pipeline([("prep", make_prep(NUMERIC, CATEGORICAL)),
                     ("model", HistGradientBoostingClassifier(random_state=42))])
param_distributions = {
    "model__learning_rate": [0.03, 0.05, 0.1, 0.2],
    "model__max_leaf_nodes": [7, 15, 31],
    "model__min_samples_leaf": [20, 40, 80],
    "model__l2_regularization": [0.0, 0.1, 1.0],
    "model__max_iter": [100, 200, 300],
}
search = RandomizedSearchCV(hgb_pipe, param_distributions, n_iter=10, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc", random_state=42)
search.fit(X_train, y_train)
print(search.best_params_)
print(f"best CV AUC {search.best_score_:.3f}  (defaults: {cv_auc['hist gradient boosting'].mean():.3f})")
best_model = search.best_estimator_
    {'model__min_samples_leaf': 80, 'model__max_leaf_nodes': 7, 'model__max_iter': 100, 'model__learning_rate': 0.05, 'model__l2_regularization': 0.1}
best CV AUC 0.879  (defaults: 0.861)
    The gentlest settings won: small trees, slow learning, 80 customers per leaf.
  7. Threshold by cost, confirmed on data (3m). "Theory says 0.25 if the probabilities are honest. Check it." 
    from sklearn.metrics import make_scorer
from sklearn.model_selection import TunedThresholdClassifierCV
profit_scorer = make_scorer(campaign_profit)
tuned = TunedThresholdClassifierCV(best_model, scoring=profit_scorer, cv=cv, store_cv_results=True)
tuned.fit(X_train, y_train)
print(f"best threshold {tuned.best_threshold_:.3f}   mean profit per validation fold (900 customers) ${tuned.best_score_:,.0f}")
curve = pd.Series(tuned.cv_results_["scores"], index=tuned.cv_results_["thresholds"])
for t in [0.1, 0.25, 0.5]:
    print(f"threshold near {t:.2f}: ${curve.iloc[np.abs(curve.index - t).argmin()]:,.0f}")
    best threshold 0.233   mean profit per validation fold (900 customers) $3,373
threshold near 0.10: $2,284
threshold near 0.25: $3,302
threshold near 0.50: $2,358
    Show the lesson's profit curve: flat from about 0.2 to 0.3; the default 0.5 gives up about a third.
  8. Open the test set, once (3m). Make a small ceremony of it: "Every decision is made. No more changes after this." 
    from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score, precision_score, recall_score
test_proba = tuned.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
offers = tuned.predict(X_test)
print(f"test AUC          {roc_auc_score(y_test, test_proba):.3f}")
print(f"offers sent       {offers.sum()} of {len(offers)}")
print(f"precision         {precision_score(y_test, offers):.3f}   (share of offers that reach a churner)")
print(f"recall            {recall_score(y_test, offers):.3f}   (share of churners who get an offer)")
print(f"model plan        ${campaign_profit(y_test, offers):7,}")
print(f"offer to everyone ${campaign_profit(y_test, np.ones(len(y_test), dtype=int)):7,}")
print(f"offer to nobody   ${campaign_profit(y_test, np.zeros(len(y_test), dtype=int)):7,}")
    test AUC          0.875
offers sent       384 of 1500
precision         0.477   (share of offers that reach a churner)
recall            0.738   (share of churners who get an offer)
model plan        $  5,220
offer to everyone $ -7,620
offer to nobody   $      0
    0.875 against CV 0.879: the choices held up. About half the offers reach a churner, and at $15 against $60 that is plenty.
  9. Explain: what the model relies on (2m). "The manager will ask 'why these customers?'" 
    from sklearn.inspection import permutation_importance
result = permutation_importance(tuned, X_test, y_test, scoring="roc_auc", n_repeats=5, random_state=42)
importance = pd.Series(result.importances_mean, index=FEATURES).sort_values(ascending=False)
print(importance.head(5).round(3))
    days_since_last_order    0.155
discount_share           0.088
tenure_months            0.058
app_user                 0.016
support_tickets_12m      0.016
dtype: float64
    Say the sentence that goes in every memo: these describe the model, not why customers leave.
  10. The memo, built from variables (2m). "So it can never disagree with your analysis." Show the first lines; the full memo is on the lesson page. 
    plan_profit = campaign_profit(y_test, offers)
memo = f"""TO: Customer-care manager          RE: Who should get the retention offer?
Recommendation: send the offer to customers whose predicted churn risk is above {tuned.best_threshold_:.0%}.
On 1,500 customers the model had never seen, that meant {offers.sum()} offers ({offers.mean():.0%} of customers).
{precision_score(y_test, offers):.0%} of the offers reached someone who was about to leave, and the list included
{recall_score(y_test, offers):.0%} of all customers who left.
Expected value: about ${plan_profit:,} for every 1,500 customers, using your figures ($15 per offer,
$60 per would-be leaver reached)."""
print(memo)
    TO: Customer-care manager          RE: Who should get the retention offer?
Recommendation: send the offer to customers whose predicted churn risk is above 23%.
On 1,500 customers the model had never seen, that meant 384 offers (26% of customers).
48% of the offers reached someone who was about to leave, and the list included
74% of all customers who left.
Expected value: about $5,220 for every 1,500 customers, using your figures ($15 per offer,
$60 per would-be leaver reached).
    Point at the last line on the lesson page: the hold-out experiment.
  11. Mistake: deciding with predict() (1m). "Same model, same ranking, a different cut-off." 
    default_offers = best_model.predict(X_test)        # predict uses 0.5
print(f"threshold 0.5   : {default_offers.sum()} offers, ${campaign_profit(y_test, default_offers):,}")
print(f"tuned threshold : {offers.sum()} offers, ${campaign_profit(y_test, offers):,}")
    threshold 0.5   : 144 offers, $3,600
tuned threshold : 384 offers, $5,220
  12. Mistake: a threshold picked on the test set (1m). "Grading your own homework with the answer key open." 
    grid = np.arange(0.05, 0.9, 0.01)
test_profits = [campaign_profit(y_test, (test_proba >= t).astype(int)) for t in grid]
peek = grid[int(np.argmax(test_profits))]
print(f"threshold picked on the test set {peek:.2f}: ${max(test_profits):,} (looks better)")
print(f"threshold picked by CV           {tuned.best_threshold_:.2f}: ${campaign_profit(y_test, offers):,} (honest)")
    threshold picked on the test set 0.26: $5,280 (looks better)
threshold picked by CV           0.23: $5,220 (honest)
    Only $60 here, because CV already chose well; it grows with smaller test sets and more knobs.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • The step name forgotten in a Pipeline search. ValueError: Invalid parameter 'learning_rate' for estimator Pipeline(steps=[('prep',... Coach: "Which step owns learning_rate?" → model__learning_rate; hgb_pipe.get_params().keys() lists the names.
  • Rows with gaps dropped. dropna keeps 5,007 of 6,000 customers, and the dropped ones churn more (0.216 vs 0.155). Coach: "Who did you throw away, and will they stop arriving in production?"
  • The default threshold, or a threshold tuned on the test set. predict sends 144 offers for $3,600 instead of 384 for $5,220; the test-set threshold looks $60 better and is not. Coach: "Where did the number 0.5 come from? Which data chose your threshold?"
Guided lab
  • Lab A (10m, everyone): labs/module-11/l13_1-case-study/leak_audit_starter.py (reference labs/module-11/l13_1-case-study/leak_audit_solution.py). The case study checked one column by hand; this writes an audit() that screens EVERY column: cross-validated AUC of the column alone, "filled for only one class", and ID-like text.
  • Expected, Lab A: churn flags cancellation_reason (0.815 alone; filled for 63% of churners and 0% of stayers) and customer_id (ID-LIKE, 0.500); the strongest honest column is days_since_last_order (0.715). Fraud: only transaction_id is flagged; velocity_24h is the strongest single column (0.782). Debrief: "A clean audit is a finding. Write it in your capstone's data section."
  • Lab B (10m, pairs): labs/module-11/l13_1-case-study/cost_scenarios_starter.py. The case-study model without age, out-of-fold probabilities on the training customers. Four offer costs: does the best threshold follow cost / 60? Then the profit of each block of 100 offers, and one look at the test set for a $25 offer.
  • Expected, Lab B: best thresholds 0.17 / 0.24 / 0.31 / 0.43 for $10 / $15 / $20 / $25 (break-even 0.167 / 0.250 / 0.333 / 0.417); the first losing block of 100 offers ends at offer 1,200 (lowest p 0.213); test, $25 offer: 0.25 gives 360 offers, profit $1,620, the new threshold 183 offers, profit $2,745.
  • Debrief: the threshold belongs to the business numbers, so it must be re-derived when they change, and served with the model (Lesson 12.2's churn_serving.json).
Start the lesson project

The project is the retention campaign plan: a $3,000 budget (200 offers), no age_group, a group check and an updated memo. In the room: Steps 1 and 2.

  • Done in the room: 4500 1500 offers allowed: 200; CV AUC with age 0.879, without 0.880.
  • Goes home: Steps 3 to 6: 360 above break-even, lowest probability on the list 0.403; the group report; 131 churners reached and $4,860 against -$540 for 200 random offers; campaign_list.csv with IDs and priority only.
Checks for understanding
  • Where does the 0.25 come from? Answer: $15 / $60: an offer pays when p × 60 > 15, if the probabilities are calibrated.
  • Why not use accuracy here? Answer: with 83.5% stayers it rewards offering to nobody; AUC compares rankings, profit judges the decision.
  • Name one thing you may NOT write in the memo. Answer: a cause ("discounts cause churn"); permutation importance describes the model.

Workbook self-check, Q7 to Q12:

Answers: Q7 C) 0.417; Q8 60; Q9 A) The data does not yet say which tree family is better: the gap is inside one fold-to-fold spread; Q10 D) Split, leak check, compare with CV, tune with CV, threshold with CV, test once, explain and write up; Q11 B) Hold back the offer from a random 10% of the list and compare who stays; Q12 A) The 200 riskiest customers, and never anyone below the break-even probability. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: in Lab A, give the per-column loop and ask only for the flag rules. Stretch: 13.1 Exercise 4 (three hand-made features lift logistic regression from 0.832 to 0.877) and the Bonus Challenge (the save rate between 10% and 40%).
