How to run it
  • 2 minutes: put the five problems on screen. Churn (13.1) and daily store sales (the lesson's worked example) are taken. Say: "Choose the one you are most curious about; curiosity carries you through the dull parts better than ease does. And 'do not deploy' can be a full answer."
  • 6 minutes: each learner writes the one-paragraph plan in the workbook's Capstone box: decision, user, target, prediction moment, split, metric and its business meaning, baseline, and what result would make them say "do not deploy". This is the start of Milestone 1.
  • 2 minutes: a show of hands per problem (A to E); write the counts on the board and photograph the plans (or collect them). Anyone undecided picks by the end of the wrap-up.
  • Coaching notes for each problem (strong work, traps, honest negative results) are in final-assessment.html. Read them before Session 12; do not hand them out.
  • Own problem: allowed if it passes the lesson's three checks (a decision, data and labels in data/, not an existing project). Approve it in writing before Session 12.
What a good paragraph sounds like (read one aloud as a model)

"The two-person review team checks about 1% of online payments each day (83 in June). I will rank June payments by fraud risk with a model trained on January to May and report average precision against the no-model 0.008, plus how many frauds the top 1% holds. A payment is scored at checkout, so only fields known then are features. I will not recommend the model if its top 1% holds no more frauds than a simple velocity rule."

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, 4 minutes)
  1. Name two places where a churn API can return a wrong number with status 200, and the check that catches each. Answer: a frame built without the training dtypes (the match test on real rows with gaps); a broken feed filled by the imputer (missing-rate and PSI checks); also an impossible value without schema limits (pydantic Field limits).
  2. The offer cost rises to $20 (value $60). The new break-even probability? Would you trust it without a check? Answer: 0.333. Check it on out-of-fold predictions or with TunedThresholdClassifierCV, because it assumes calibrated probabilities (the lab's best was 0.31).
  3. Your capstone: the problem letter, the decision it supports and the baseline number you must beat. Answer: for example "B: which 83 payments the team reviews each day; no-model average precision 0.008". Anyone without a letter gets a message from you tonight.
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish both projects. 12.2 weekly drift report Steps 4 to 6 (drift_report.csv, three actions); 13.1 retention campaign plan Steps 3 to 6 (campaign_list.csv, the updated memo).
  • Exercises. 12.2: 1 to 3 (rejections, a model-info endpoint, category PSI); 13.1: 1 to 3 (profit by store, the better model in dollars, calibration). Exercise 4 of each is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes and both journal entries.
  • Before the Next Lesson: 12.2: start the real server and send one request from /docs; change the threshold to 0.4 and watch the week-2 offer rate. 13.1: reread the memo as the manager; one sentence per other Pinecrest table.
  • Capstone (about 60 minutes): read all of Lesson 13.2 (the brief, the five problems, the worked example, the model card skeleton, the checklist, the rubric). Finish Milestone 1: create capstone.ipynb with the plan cell, starting from today's paragraph.
  • Optional: fraud_monitor_starter.py, debug_serving.py, and whichever lab you did not reach.
Bring list for Session 12
  • A laptop with the course environment (nothing new to install) and the pinecrest-ml folder with data/ and labs/ (Session 12's files are in labs/module-12/).
  • Your capstone folder with capstone.ipynb whose first cell holds the written plan (Milestone 1), and the problem letter you chose.
  • Your Lesson 13.1 notebook (its structure is the capstone template) and the 12.2 files serve.py, schemas.py, monitor.py (for the optional serving milestone).
  • The workbook and journal. No downloads.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long
  • Never cut the capstone choice segment: move the 13.1 project start home instead.
  • Cut first (saves 5m): 12.2 Steps 11 and 13; show the PSI table from the page and say the three answers.
  • Cut second (saves 6m): 13.1 Steps 2 and 9 (the shapes and the importance are on the page).
  • Cut third (saves 10m): the 12.2 project start; give its check numbers instead.
Running short
  • Run uvicorn and the /docs page live; send one request from the browser.
  • fraud_monitor_starter.py for everyone: a model on real months that does not drift, and a feed that breaks.
  • Let learners compare capstone plans in pairs with the five-question test: decision? prediction moment? split? metric? 'do not deploy' bar?
Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 12: Capstone Kickoff and Studio I

How to run the capstone kickoff: frame Lesson 13.2's brief and rubric, test every plan in a clinic, and run a timed studio for Milestones 2 to 4 (data checks and the feature-timing table, two honest baselines and an evaluation harness, a first pipeline). The capstone reference numbers and coaching notes are in final-assessment.html.

1 lesson 3 hrs session Lesson 13.2 Session 12 of 13 Studio

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson, no new tools. Sessions 12 and 13 are studios for Lesson 13.2 (480 minutes for a solo learner). Today: the kickoff and Milestones 1 to 4 (plan, data checks and split, baselines and harness, a first pipeline). Milestone 5 (improve) goes home; Session 13 does Milestones 6 to 8 and the showcase.
  • Your answer notes are in final-assessment.html at the kit root: for each problem A to E the steps of a sound project, the reference numbers (baselines, good models, traps, honest negative results), follow-up questions and the rubric with the scoring rules. Read the notes for the problems your room chose (the Session 11 plan photos tell you the mix). Nothing from it is said aloud as an answer: the lesson has no answer key.
  • Coach by asking. Every move in the studio is a question ("which columns do you know on the day you predict?"). The template already fixes the split, the metric and the baseline: today makes sure nobody quietly breaks them.
  • The labs in labs/module-12/l13_2-capstone/ are capstone tools, not exercises: a second honest baseline per problem and an evaluation harness with a first pipeline. Each learner runs only the part for their own problem; the solutions hold reference numbers for all five.
The one risk of Session 12: an evaluation setup that is quietly wrong

The most expensive capstone mistake is made in the first hour and discovered in the last: a random split on time-ordered data, a feature known only after the prediction moment, a test item the customer already owned, a k chosen on the test set. Nothing errors, and every later number is wrong. So the room protects Milestones 2 and 3: the feature-timing table and the harness are checked by you or a partner before anyone builds a model. A learner who leaves with a correct harness and a modest first pipeline is in better shape than one with a tuned model on a broken split.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Read final-assessment.html, at least the Kickoff traps and the reference numbers for the problems your learners chose. Group tables by problem if the room allows (two to four learners per table).
  • ☐ Run python3 labs/check_labs.py module-12: 4 passed (about 15 seconds). The folder also holds capstone_start.py, the lesson's starter template as a module, which the two labs import.
  • ☐ Traffic-light cards or three colors of sticky notes per table; a visible countdown timer; the rubric (lesson page, The Rubric) on the second screen.
  • ☐ The board, before learners arrive: the five plan-clinic questions; a log grid (learner, problem, baseline, harness score, first pipeline) for the 2:40 milestone check.
  • ☐ No downloads, no servers, no internet needed. Learners need their capstone folder with data/ beside the notebook and labs/module-12/.
  • ☐ Own problems approved in writing since Session 11 (the lesson's three checks: a decision, data and labels in data/, not an existing project).

In the room today: about 125 minutes of milestone work (plan clinic, Milestones 2 and 3, most of 4). At home before Session 13: finish Milestone 4 and do Milestone 5 (about 110 minutes, with a log of attempts). Learners must NOT open the test set at home: Milestone 6 happens in Session 13.

Protect, in this order: the feature-timing table (Milestone 2), the harness with the baseline in it (Milestone 3), the milestone check. Cut first: shorten the frame to 10 minutes and the clinic to 15; never cut the 0:30 check-in of Milestone 2.

Warm-up

0:00

Retrieval from Session 11 and a Capstone Check

10 min
Five questions (with answers)
  1. Why does serve.py rebuild the request frame with the stored training dtypes? Answer: Otherwise a None in a one-row table makes an object column the imputer does not treat as missing: a silently different score (training-serving skew).
  2. Of input drift, data quality, prediction drift and performance, which needs labels? Answer: Only performance (AUC, offer precision), and the labels arrive months later.
  3. Where does the churn threshold 0.25 come from, and what does it assume? Answer: $15 / $60: an offer pays when p × 60 > 15. It assumes calibrated probabilities, which the case study confirmed with TunedThresholdClassifierCV.
  4. In the case study, when is the test set used? Answer: Once, after features, model, hyperparameters and threshold are all chosen.
  5. Your capstone in one sentence: the decision, and the baseline number you must beat. Answer: No single answer. Listen for a decision (not a topic) and a number; anyone without one gets the first visit in the plan clinic.
How to run it

W1 to W4 on paper for four minutes, answers called out in two. Then W5 around the room, ten seconds each; write problem letters on the board. Anyone with no decision or no number is your first visit in the plan clinic.

Lesson Plan

13.2

Capstone Project

160 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Frame a business request as a machine-learning task with a decision, a target, a leakage-safe split, a metric and a baseline, written down before any modeling
  • Build, compare and improve models in leakage-proof pipelines, touching the test set once
  • Analyze errors by subgroup and explain the model honestly, without claiming causes
  • Review your model against a responsible-ML checklist and document it in a model card
  • Deliver a project that reruns from a fresh start and score it yourself against a rubric
Frame · 15 min: the trail guide's report

Say: "A guide who scouts a new route hands the club a short report: where it goes, how it compares with the old route, where it is dangerous, who should not attempt it. Your notebook is the scouting trip; the model card is the report. Pinecrest's teams will read the card, not the code, and their data team will rerun the notebook."

Put the rubric on screen and read the Proficient column of each row aloud. Then three sentences from the lesson, on the board: "Do not deploy" can be a full answer. The one loop that is not allowed is from the test set back to tuning. The template is the agreed rules of the competition (split, metric, baseline): change one only with a written reason.

Today's clock, said once: plan clinic, Milestone 2 with check-ins every 15 minutes, a break with Run All, Milestone 3 with the baselines measured in class, Milestone 4, and a stand-up where everyone reads three numbers.

Plan clinic · 20 min (Milestone 1)
  • Pairs, ideally across problems (8 min): each reads the other's plan cell and asks the five questions on the board: (1) Who makes which decision with the predictions, and how often?; (2) What is the prediction moment, and which columns are known then?; (3) Which split does the template use, and does it match the data's structure (time, groups)?; (4) Which metric, in the business's units, and what does the baseline score on it?; (5) What result would make you write "do not deploy"? Every "I don't know" becomes a line in the plan.
  • Rewrite (7 min): each learner fixes their plan cell. Circulate to the learners who had no decision in the warm-up.
  • Two plans read aloud (5 min), with permission: one strong, one that improved. Name the part that made it strong (usually the prediction moment or the "do not deploy" bar).
  • Done when someone else could read the plan and predict roughly what the notebook will contain (the lesson's own done-when).
Studio I · 40 min · Milestone 2, load, check and split
  • 0:00 check-in (2 min). Cards: green (data loaded with the template, split printed), amber (loaded, checks unclear), red (not loaded). Reds first; ambers pair with a green.
  • Work (13 min). Shapes, dtypes, date ranges, missing values by group, duplicate keys, the target's distribution, odd values; one line of conclusion under each. The relevant part of data/README.md.
  • 0:15 check-in (2 min). One word: "timing", "quirks", "stuck". Then everyone starts the feature-timing table: every column, "known at prediction time? yes / no / only as a plan".
  • 0:30 check-in (3 min). Partners swap feature-timing tables for 60 seconds each: "Which yes would you challenge?" Coaching round on the challenged rows.
  • Done when each learner can say what one row is, how many rows train and test hold, and which columns they will never use.
Studio I · 35 min · Milestone 3, baselines and the harness
  • Baselines first (12 min): labs/module-12/l13_2-capstone/baselines_starter.py with PROBLEM set to their letter. One more honest baseline on the same split and metric, and the "do not deploy" sentence written into the plan.
  • Reference (from the solution): demand per product × store 12.953, same week last year 13.810 (template 13.419); fraud velocity rule average precision 0.250, 19 of 64 frauds in its top 1%; reviews keyword rule accuracy 0.788, negative recall 0.477; recs random unowned 0.053; segments five random groups -0.023.
  • Then the harness (23 min): labs/module-12/l13_2-capstone/harness_starter.py: ONE function that scores any candidate on the training data the way the split works (rolling folds for demand, May for fraud, stratified folds with negative recall for reviews, a second hidden purchase for recs, silhouette plus seed stability for segments). The baseline goes through it first.
  • Coach: "Does any row of your validation data come from AFTER its training rows?" "Does the baseline go through the same function?" Learners stuck for more than five minutes look at the solution's structure for their problem only.
Studio I · 30 min · Milestone 4, a first pipeline
  • 0:00 (2 min): "The simplest sensible model, as a Pipeline, through your harness." One-hot + ridge; scaled numbers + one-hot + logistic regression; TF-IDF + logistic regression; item-kNN; K-Means on scaled features.
  • Work (13 min), 0:15 check-in (2 min): one word: "beats", "ties", "loses". Every "loses" gets a visit: look for a bug before a better model.
  • Reference (harness solution, training data only): demand ridge 13.128 vs baseline 13.924 in rolling folds (pessimistic: unseen months, see Q12); fraud logistic regression average precision 0.539 on May vs 0.008; reviews TF-IDF + LR accuracy 0.857 with negative recall 0.577; recs item-kNN 0.286 vs popularity 0.209 on the validation item; segments k = 5 silhouette 0.470, seed ARI 1.000.
  • Last 13 minutes: start the attempts log (a small DataFrame or results.json): candidate, CV score, spread, one line of why. Milestone 5 at home fills it.
Kickoff coaching notes by problem (no answers aloud)

A · Demand. Strong early work: Plans the order decision three weeks ahead, so only planned fields are features (store, product, calendar, price and promo as planned); adds the per product × store mean as a second baseline (12.95) and a harness that respects time.
Traps to listen for: Actual avg_high_temp_f as a feature (known only after the week); a random split of weekly rows; the 14 planted stockout zeros read as zero demand; reading the 40%-off weeks (9.7 units against 20.1 at full price) as "discounts lower sales" (they are off-season clearance).
Ask: "Which columns do you know on the day the order is placed?" · "Is a zero always zero demand?" · "Inside 2024, which weeks validate which?"

B · Fraud. Strong early work: Keeps the January-to-May / June split, validates inside it on May, names the team's capacity (1% of June = 83 payments) and measures a one-line rule (velocity) as the second baseline (average precision 0.250, 19 of 64 frauds in its top 1%).
Traps to listen for: Accuracy (0.9923 for "nothing is fraud" in June); a stratified random split that mixes months; resampling before the split; ignoring the drift in the fraud rate (1.00% in January to 0.77% in June).
Ask: "How many payments can the team look at, and how many frauds does that hold today?" · "Which month validates your choices?" · "Who is kept waiting by a hold?"

C · Reviews. Strong early work: Uses the text (and says whether titles are allowed), keeps the stratified split, reports accuracy AND the recall of negative reviews from the first harness, and decides up front what the live system does with 3-star reviews.
Traps to listen for: Accuracy alone ("always positive" is 0.745 and catches no complaint); the English stop-word list, which removes "not" (accuracy drops to 0.864); fitting the vectorizer on all reviews before the split.
Ask: "Which error is the costly one here?" · "What happens to a 3-star review in production?" · "Does your vocabulary know the word 'not'?"

D · Recs. Strong early work: Keeps leave-last-out for the test, carves a SECOND hidden purchase out of the training rows for validation, masks owned products everywhere, and measures random (about 0.05) and popularity (0.209) first.
Traps to listen for: Choosing k on the test hit rate (Lesson 11.2 shows the k curve on the test set for teaching; a capstone must use the validation item); forgetting the mask (an unmasked item-kNN scores 0.113 because 69.1% of its slots are already owned); evaluating on training purchases.
Ask: "Which purchase validates your k?" · "Can your list contain something the customer owns?" · "What does a brand-new customer see?"

E · Segments. Strong early work: Chooses behavior columns only, scales (and considers logs), compares k with several signals (silhouette, inertia, BIC, stability) and plans the stability check (seeds, halves) before naming anything.
Traps to listen for: Unscaled features (silhouette 0.545, HIGHER than the scaled 0.470, because average order value dominates: a better number from a worse clustering); all 11 numeric columns (silhouette 0.189 at k = 5); age_group in the clustering; choosing k from one number.
Ask: "Which column dominates the distances, and why?" · "Do your segments survive another seed and half the data?" · "What will you do with age_group?"

Milestone check · 10 min

Stand-up in problem order. Each learner reads three numbers: the baseline, the baseline through their harness, their first pipeline through the harness. Write them in the board grid and photograph it: it is your list for the first coaching round of Session 13 (anyone without a harness, and anyone whose pipeline loses, gets an email tonight).

Checks for understanding

Workbook self-check, all twelve items (project skills), as homework or a five-minute pair activity:

Answers: Q1 A) Each morning the two-person review team decides which of the day's payments to hold; the model ranks them; Q2 B) avg_high_temp_f, the actual temperature of the forecast week; Q3 [3, 5]; Q4 C) The model will score future months, and the fraud rate drifts; validating on a later month mimics that; Q5 D) The best k on a validation purchase carved from the training rows (each customer's second-to-last); Q6 A) The scaled ones: unscaled distances are dominated by average order value, so the higher number comes from a worse clustering; Q7 2.0; Q8 C) Look for a bug (a split, a feature, a leak in reverse) before looking for a better model; Q9 D) Say what the live system does with them and measure it (how many get flagged as negative); Q10 B) Before any modeling, in the plan; Q11 A) Name who bears the errors, keep a human review with a fast release path, and report results by account age; Q12 C) Each validation block contains months the folds have never seen, so a month effect learned from one year cannot be checked forward inside that year. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: learners who arrive without a plan write it in the clinic with you and narrow the scope (one metric, one baseline, one pipeline); they still meet every rubric row. Stretch: the Exemplary column early: a leak demonstrated with before-and-after scores, a second baseline that is genuinely hard to beat, a written "do not deploy" bar in the business's units.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, 4 minutes)
  1. Write your harness in one sentence: which rows train, which rows validate, which metric. Answer: for example "January to April train, May validates, average precision". Check that validation never precedes its training rows on time-ordered data.
  2. Your two baselines and their numbers. Answer: the template's and the one from today's lab, on the same split and metric.
  3. Name one column you will never use, and why. Answer: for example the actual week's temperature (unknown at order time), transaction_id (a label), age_group in the segments (a sensitive attribute; check it afterwards instead).
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish Milestone 4 if it is not done: the first pipeline through the harness, beating the baseline (or a bug found).
  • Milestone 5, improve (about 90 minutes): better features, then a stronger model, then modest tuning; keep a change only if it beats the previous best by more than the fold spread. Log every attempt with its CV score.
  • Do NOT score the test set. Choose a final candidate on the training data only; Session 13 opens the test set once, in the room.
  • The lesson's warm-ups: Exercise 1 (an interval for an improvement) and Exercise 2 (who gets held) are good practice for Milestone 6; the Quick Quiz (five questions).
  • Journal: one entry on the hardest decision so far (the lesson's prompt is for the end).
Bring list for Session 13
  • A laptop with the course environment and pinecrest-ml with data/ and labs/ (Session 13's files are in labs/module-13/).
  • Your capstone notebook through Milestone 5: plan, checks, harness, the attempts log, and ONE chosen final candidate, with the test set still unopened.
  • The rubric (lesson page) and the model card skeleton, open in a tab; your journal.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long
  • Shorten the frame to 10 minutes and the plan clinic to 15; keep all Milestone 2 check-ins.
  • If Milestone 3 runs over, Milestone 4 starts at home: the harness with the baseline in it is the protected outcome.
Running short
  • The twelve self-check items in pairs.
  • Pairs read each other's harness code and try to find a row that validates before it trains.
  • Start Milestone 5's first idea in the room (one feature, through the harness).

Session 13: Capstone Studio II and Showcase

How to run the last session: a timed studio for Lesson 13.2's Milestones 6 to 8 (one look at the test set with an interval, errors by subgroup, the responsible-ML review, a finding-first figure and the first sections of the model card), a showcase, and the close of the course. Reference numbers and the scoring rules are in final-assessment.html.

1 lesson 3 hrs session Lesson 13.2 Session 13 of 13 Studio + showcase

How to Use This Guide

  • The last session of Advanced Machine Learning and of the course. A studio for Lesson 13.2's Milestones 6 to 8 (test once, explain and review, the figure and the first sections of the card), a showcase, and the close. Milestones 8 (rest) and 9 go home, with the submission due one week from today.
  • Your answer notes and the rubric with the scoring rules are in final-assessment.html: for each problem the reference test numbers, the honest negative results, the traps and the follow-up questions. The short versions below are for coaching only; nothing is said aloud as an answer.
  • The labs in labs/module-13/l13_2-capstone/ are capstone tools: intervals (paired and by week), and the hand-in steps (results.json, skops round trip, a card written from the results, a number check). debug_capstone.py is a classmate's notebook to review: it breaks four checklist rules and still prints a rosy number.
The one risk of Session 13: the test set opened at home, then "improved"

Some learners will have peeked. Do not punish it; make it visible. In the warm-up status ask "has anyone already scored the test set?" and say: "Then your card says so, and the score you report is the first one you saw." The Evaluation row rewards honesty about this far more than a polished number. And in the room, nobody tunes after Milestone 6: post the rule on the board.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Read the results notes in final-assessment.html for the problems in your room; the Session 12 board photo tells you who has no harness yet (first visits today).
  • ☐ Run python3 labs/check_labs.py module-13: 4 passed (about 25 seconds).
  • ☐ Decide the showcase format from the room size (table below); print rotation cards for a gallery walk; a visible timer.
  • ☐ The board: "Milestone 6 happens once. No tuning after it." and the responsible-ML checklist (ten items from the lesson page).
  • ☐ The course-close content: next steps (the four "going further" readings, a second capstone on open data), your feedback form, the submission deadline (one week from today).

Warm-up

0:00

Retrieval and Status

10 min
Five questions (with answers)
  1. Why does the fraud harness validate on May, not on shuffled folds? Answer: The model scores future months and the fraud rate drifts; validating on a later month mimics that.
  2. What is the one loop the lesson does not allow? Answer: From the test set back to tuning: once you have looked at the test score, the model is finished.
  3. Name two parts of a model card that a weak card usually lacks. Answer: The baseline on the same test set (with an interval for the model), and limitations specific to the data; also often the out-of-scope uses.
  4. What does "explanations are not causes" mean for your card? Answer: Importance and SHAP describe what the model relies on; words like "causes" or "drives" need a design that supports them (none in this course).
  5. Status: your final candidate, its CV score and the baseline's, in one line. Answer: No single answer. Listen for a candidate chosen on training data, and for anyone who has already looked at the test set: they write that in their card.
How to run it

W1 to W4 on paper, four minutes; answers in two. W5 around the room: write each status on the board grid next to the Session 12 numbers; anyone without a final candidate is your first visit.

Lesson Plan

13.2

Capstone Project

130 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Frame a business request as a machine-learning task with a decision, a target, a leakage-safe split, a metric and a baseline, written down before any modeling
  • Build, compare and improve models in leakage-proof pipelines, touching the test set once
  • Analyze errors by subgroup and explain the model honestly, without claiming causes
  • Review your model against a responsible-ML checklist and document it in a model card
  • Deliver a project that reruns from a fresh start and score it yourself against a rubric
Frame · 10 min: test once, then read the errors

Say: "Today you open the test set, once. Refit the final pipeline on all the training data, score the test set, write the number down next to the baseline, and never change the model again. Then the interesting part starts: where is it wrong, and who pays for those errors?" Read the Evaluation and Error analysis rows of the rubric. Point at the checklist on the board: "Every item gets an answer specific to your problem. 'Not applicable, because...' is fine; skipping is not."

Studio II · 40 min · Milestone 6, test once, interval, errors
  • 0:00 (3 min): each learner says their final candidate aloud, then runs the test once. Write the number in the notebook and on the board grid.
  • Work (12 min): a bootstrap interval for the test metric, paired with the baseline (labs/module-13/l13_2-capstone/bootstrap_ci_starter.py shows both kinds: paired resamples on fraud, whole weeks on demand). Choose the operating point if the problem has one (the review team's 1%, top 10 for recommendations).
  • Reference (lab solution): fraud June average precision logistic regression 0.539 (95% CI 0.408 to 0.658), tuned boosting 0.556, difference +0.017 (-0.046 to +0.077): not distinguishable; top 1% holds 35 of 64 frauds (recall 0.55, 0.43 to 0.68). Demand: units saved 5.23, row interval 4.90 to 5.53, week interval 4.68 to 5.81.
  • 0:15 check-in (2 min): one word: "interval", "operating point", "stuck".
  • Work (13 min): errors by at least one subgroup (store, category, account age, history length, segment) with the baseline's errors beside them; read the twenty worst cases.
  • 0:30 check-in (3 min): each learner writes ONE finding about where the model fails. Read four aloud. Done when the test metric has an interval next to the baseline, and one written failure finding exists.
Studio II · 30 min · Milestone 7, explain and review
  • Explain (10 min): permutation importance or SHAP on the test set; two sentences that describe the MODEL ("relies on"), never causes.
  • Review (15 min): every responsible-ML checklist item answered for the problem: leakage audit, one look, baseline, subgroups, who bears the errors, sensitive attributes, humans in the loop, explanations vs causes, privacy, monitoring and fallback. If an answer changes the system (a review step, a removed feature, a threshold), make the change and note its effect.
  • Pairs (5 min): swap checklists; each challenges one answer that is too generic.
Results coaching notes by problem (no answers aloud)

A · Demand. A sound project finds: Gradient boosting on store, product, category, month, prices, promo and holiday: 2025 MAE 8.19 against 13.42 (95% CI of the units saved 4.91 to 5.53 resampling rows; a week bootstrap gives 4.68 to 5.81); ridge with one-hot 11.29; Poisson loss 8.12.
Honest negatives and traps: Temperature adds nothing measurable once the month is in: actual 8.16, last year's for that week 8.18, none 8.19. "Same week last year" (13.81) is worse than the plain per-product mean. Errors are largest for Footwear and Camp Kitchen in units; Snow in summer is near zero both ways.

B · Fraud. A sound project finds: June average precision: logistic regression 0.539, boosting with defaults 0.477, tuned boosting 0.556 (95% CI 0.423 to 0.668); the top 1% (83 held) catches 33 / 32 / 35 of 64 frauds.
Honest negatives and traps: Tuned boosting minus logistic regression: 0.017 (95% CI -0.046 to 0.077): the two cannot be told apart, and on May validation logistic regression is as good (0.539 vs 0.532). New accounts are 8.4% of legitimate June payments but 29.2% of the false alarms: a human review step and a fast release path belong in the card.

C · Reviews. A sound project finds: TF-IDF + logistic regression: test accuracy 0.877 (95% CI 0.855 to 0.901) but negative recall only 0.660; with class_weight="balanced" accuracy 0.873 and negative recall 0.873; a threshold set on CV for 90% negative recall gives 0.909 on the test set while flagging 36.8% of reviews.
Honest negatives and traps: Bigrams barely help on this vocabulary (CV 0.860 vs 0.857). Titles alone score 0.969: legitimate if titles arrive with the review, but they restate the rating, so the card must say which was used. On 3-star reviews the model flags 20.8% at 0.5 and 64.8% at the 90%-recall threshold: unvalidated, and a caveat.

D · Recs. A sound project finds: Item-kNN 0.308 and SVD with 5 factors 0.311 against popularity 0.209 (item-kNN minus popularity 0.099, 95% CI 0.082 to 0.116); validation picks k = 5 (0.285).
Honest negatives and traps: SVD minus item-kNN: 0.003 (95% CI -0.009 to 0.016): a tie, so the simpler, explainable model is a fine choice. Content-based on activity alone scores 0.176, BELOW popularity (with a popularity tie-break 0.272): an honest negative, but it is the only answer for a new product. Popularity recommends 31 distinct products, item-kNN 161.

E · Segments. A sound project finds: K-Means on the four scaled behavior columns: silhouette by k peaks at 5 (0.47; log + scaled 0.478), seed stability ARI 1.000, half-sample ARI 0.986 to 0.996; five nameable profiles whose churn runs from 0.03 to 0.32 (never trained on).
Honest negatives and traps: Gaussian-mixture BIC keeps falling past 5 (43175 at 5, 42990 at 6, 42803 at 7): BIC alone would not pick 5, which is why several signals are needed. k = 4 is unstable across seeds (ARI down to 0.512).

Studio II · 20 min · Milestone 8, the figure and the card
  • The figure (10 min): one chart that carries the argument (model against baseline by subgroup, a capacity or threshold chart, segment profiles), with a title that states the finding, saved from code.
  • The card (10 min): Recommendation and Performance, written FROM the results dictionary (labs/module-13/l13_2-capstone/model_card_starter.py shows the pattern and the number check). The rest of the card goes home.
  • Reference (model-card lab, review triage): CV picks the balanced model (negative recall 0.849 vs 0.57); test accuracy 0.873 (95% CI 0.85 to 0.895) vs 0.745, negative recall 0.873; skops needs no extra trusted types; a hand-typed 0.877 is caught.
Showcase prep · 10 min

One figure and three sentences: the decision and the recommendation; the main number against the baseline with its interval; one specific limitation. A learner whose answer is "do not deploy" says so first, with the evidence: model that sentence once for the room.

Showcase · 30 min
LearnersLightning talks (30 min)Gallery walk (30 min)
8One circle: 8 × (3 min + 30 s for one question) = 28 min. You keep time.Two halves of 4: 4 visits of 3 min (13 min), swap, 13 min, 4 min debrief.
16Two circles of 8 in parallel, a learner timekeeper in the second; or 2-minute talks in one circle.Two halves of 8: each visitor makes 4 visits of 3 min to assigned stations; swap; 4 min debrief.
24Three circles of 8 with timekeepers.Two halves of 12 with printed rotation cards. Recommended at this size.

Audience job: one question per presenter on a sticky note; the presenter keeps them for the card's Limitations. Your job: keep time, say nothing evaluative in public, note one strength per learner for the written feedback.

Course close · 10 min
  • What the course built (2 min): from fit and predict in Lesson 1.1 to a model chosen, checked for leaks, explained honestly, served, monitored and documented.
  • The last journal entry (4 min): the lesson's prompt: the one decision they are proudest of, in three sentences as to an interviewer; then reread the Lesson 1.1 entry and write what they would tell that person about baselines.
  • What next (2 min): the four "going further" readings (AutoML, federated learning, quantum machine learning, genetic algorithms), a second capstone on open public data, depth in one area. Repeat the rule: never practice on private or work data without written permission; publish .skops, never pickles.
  • Feedback (2 min): your form, and one spoken question: "Which session would you change first, and how?"
Checks for understanding

Workbook self-check, all twelve items, as homework with the submission or as a pair activity if the showcase runs short:

Answers: Q1 A) After every choice (features, model, hyperparameters, threshold or list size) is made on the training data, once; Q2 [ 3.475 97.525]; Q3 C) The two cannot be told apart on 64 June frauds; choose on other grounds (simplicity, explainability, cost) and say so; Q4 D) The week interval: rows in the same week share the same conditions, so they are not independent; Q5 B) On 774 held-out reviews the model finds 87% of the negative reviews (the baseline finds none), at accuracy 0.873 (95% CI 0.85 to 0.895) against 0.745; Q6 D) Customers in this segment churned more often (about 32%); the segments describe behavior, not why customers leave; Q7 {'Bend': 3.0, 'Boise': 5.0}; Q8 B) Compare each group's error with its baseline and its typical volume: Snow sells about 12 units a week, Footwear about 46; Q9 A) Hit rate at 10 by customer history length, for the model and the baseline; Q10 C) Keep it out of the clustering, then check whether the segments differ by age; Q11 D) Write the card from the results (results.json and an f-string), and check every card number against it; Q12 A) The decision and the recommendation, the main number against the baseline with its interval, one specific limitation, pointing at one figure. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: learners without a final candidate take their first pipeline as final today, run Milestone 6 on it, and say so in the card: a modest, honest model meets every rubric row. Stretch: the Exemplary column: an error finding turned into a fix and measured (the worked example's closed-day rule), a system change because of a checklist answer, the optional FastAPI endpoint with a match test and a drift check.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, 4 minutes)
  1. Your main result in one sentence: the metric with its interval, against the baseline. Answer: for example "June average precision 0.556 (95% CI 0.42 to 0.67) against 0.008 with no model; the top 1% holds 35 of 64 frauds."
  2. One limitation specific to your data (not "more data"). Answer: for example "only 64 June frauds, so two good models cannot be ranked" or "3-star reviews are not validated".
  3. What is left, in order, before you submit? Answer: the rest of the card, then Milestone 9: save with skops, results.json and requirements.txt, Restart and Run All, check every card number against the output, self-score on the rubric.
Submission (within one week) and what goes home
  • After class (about 55 minutes required): the rest of Milestone 8 (the card's remaining sections), Milestone 9 (save, pin, Restart and Run All, check numbers, self-score). Optional: Serve it (60).
  • Hand in the project folder: capstone.ipynb (reruns from a fresh kernel), model_card.md, one to three figures, model.skops, results.json, requirements.txt, a README (what it is, how to run it, the fictional-data line, and what help was used), and the self-scored rubric. Deadline: one week from today. Rules, late options and the scoring sheet are in final-assessment.html.
  • Bring list: there is no Session 14. Do not hand in the course data/ folder: link to pinecrest-ml-data.zip, as the lesson's Sharing Your Work section says.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long
  • Showcase prep to 5 minutes; the course close to the journal prompt and the deadline.
  • Never cut the one test-set run or the interval in Milestone 6.
Running short
  • Everyone reviews labs/module-13/l13_2-capstone/debug_capstone.py in pairs: four broken rules, which rubric row each costs, and the honest numbers.
  • The twelve self-check items in pairs.
  • Start Milestone 9 in the room: Restart and Run All, then check three card numbers against the output.
